# S9 — Agent identity on a shared host (design, rev 36 — rev 35 plus the chair's closure: queue precedence rests on the session's `cancelled` state, never on the wipe mark; rev 34's review round applied: the lane account **index** moves *inside the lane*, `<lane>/claude-accounts/index.json`, so the startup reconciliation compares the lane against itself in fact and not only in wording and a lost or corrupt Orca state file can no longer make every login in every lane unindexed at once; an index that is missing or will not parse **quarantines** unindexed directories rather than deleting them; and the per-lane write queue is stated as **three** members — the in-lane switch, the login capture and the wipe — with the wipe holding precedence and both other members re-checking **after** they acquire the queue. Rev 34 applied rev 33's review round: the lane account store gains an **index** and a **startup reconciliation**, so a login that outlives the host process cannot leave an unreaped grant at rest; the host-inline login takes the designation precondition and a **required** `--email`; a designation *move* cancels the session it moves off; `loginStatus` gets its caller rule; and the in-lane switch and the login capture share one per-lane write queue. Rev 33 applied rev 32's review round: the lane login session joins §2f's lifecycle fence, the wipe's scope covers the per-lane account store, I6 re-scoped, the two login entry points fenced as one session, and the push-model leftovers cleared out of §2a/§2h/§2k/§3/§5/§7/§9. Rev 32 re-based the credential source on per-lane OAuth login and deleted the push/lease/L1 model, rewriting §2a–§2f, §2h, §2k, §2l, §3, §4, §5, §6, §7, §8 and §9 — the title through rev 32 said "§2b–§2f, §3, §4", which under-stated the blast radius a reviewer must re-open)

Repo read-only at `/home/ubuntu/orca-integration` @ `feat/resilience-presence-integration` (B2 merged); **as of rev 25 the citations below are re-derived against the *implementation* tree, `/home/ubuntu/orca-s9` @ `feat/agent-identity-s9`, which now carries S9a, S9b, S9c and S9d (§10).** Problem: two developers share one dev box and one operating-system account; each has several personal Claude accounts. **Host pivot, rev 11:** that box now runs **Windows Server**, not Linux; both developers work from Windows 11 desktops; the owner's personal VPS stays Linux and single-owner and is out of S9's scope. The macOS and Linux material below is kept in full — Orca ships all three and nothing here is demoted for being non-Windows — but **the shared host this design is built for is Windows Server (§2m)**, and every platform-dependent claim was re-opened against `win32` for this revision. Requirements as approved: **R1** a terminal's Claude credential is controlled only by the **principal** that opened it (rev 11: a principal is a *person*, and several of that person's grants — desktop and phone — share one lane; Q1, §2a); **R2** the opener switches among *their own* accounts live and every terminal they opened re-resolves with no restart; **R3** the other developer's terminals never move; **R4** both keep viewing/typing into each other's terminals (B2 presence shows the owner); **R5** account inventory stays on the owner's desktop. Accepted trade-off: the lane's credential **is** materialized on the dev box, in that **principal's** own lane directory, `0700`/`0600` — and since rev 32 it is the lane's *own* OAuth grant rather than a copy of a desktop's, resident from the lane's login until an explicit logout, a last-grant revoke or a deprovision (§2f). The disconnect wipe and the startup wipe are deleted and the at-rest window is unbounded, which is the one axis on which this model is weaker than the push model it replaces (R-32a, §4, §7 question 12).

**R1 is achieved against remote *RPC* authority, not against a hostile paired peer.** A paired grant is shell-equivalent on this host today (§4). Read §4 before §2; the rest of the design is written to that limit and says so at each seam.

**Rev 24 is a reconciliation, not a redesign.** S9a and S9b are *implemented* (`feat/agent-identity-s9`, 163 commits on top of the `feat/resilience-presence-integration` tip this document was written against). Every `file:line` citation below was mechanically re-derived against that tip — the tree moved far enough that `orca-runtime.ts` alone grew from 28k to 39,472 lines and moved to `src/main/runtime/` — and the places where the *implementation* departed from this design are recorded, with the reason, in the new **§10**. Nothing in §2–§7 was redesigned to match the code; where the code is right and the doc was wrong, §10 says so and the sentence is corrected in place. Evidence grade on every claim: **[code]** re-read at the cited line in this tree, **[doc]** from Claude Code documentation, **[inferred]** reasoned, not observed. All line numbers re-opened for rev 9; every line rev 10 touched re-opened again; and for rev 11 every claim this revision adds or re-keys was re-opened at its cited line, together with every platform-dependent claim in the document read against `win32`. Two rev-10 **[code]** claims were imprecise on re-opening and are corrected in place, at the site, rather than quietly: the pull path's function name (§2k) and the provider's base env merge (§2 preamble, step (a)). **Rev 12 applies review round 1 against rev 11**, and every claim it touches was re-opened again at its cited line in the same tree. Four rev-11 **[code]** claims did not survive that re-opening and are corrected at their sites with the correction named rather than the citation silently swapped: the Windows justification for the `loadSucceeded` gate (§2i — `hardenExistingSecureFile` cannot throw on `win32`); the `scope: 'desktop'` both the delegable-switch precondition and the second-desktop refusal keyed on (§2e, §2l — `DeviceScope` is `'mobile' | 'runtime'`); the daemon's WSL export gate (§2m(5) — `:740`, not `:789`); and the statusline parser's two line numbers (§2k — `:64`/`:87`). Two rev-11 *decisions* were unsound rather than mis-cited and are rewritten: the revoke wipe (§2f, grant-keyed, would have destroyed a live principal's lane) and the second-desktop closure (§2e, refused a push while leaving the rotator running). Where a rev-11 sentence was right and a review note was not, the sentence stands and the re-opened line is stated. **Rev 13 applies review round 2 against rev 12**, and every claim it touches was re-opened again at its cited line in the same tree. It fixes one *blocker* — §2e's rev-12 restatement of the delegation lease exempted the lease-holding desktop from suppression, which re-opened double rotation on the ordinary one-desktop-plus-phone principal and made §5's own named control unpassable; the lease means **one pusher and one puller**, never one licensed rotator — and six majors, each of which was a decision that could not be implemented at the site it was written at, or two rules that could not both ship: the federated create's lane (§2a), `openInMyLane`'s authorization join (§2g), the withdrawn Windows `hardenExistingSecureFile` mode still asserted in §2a, the WSL refusal sited at a function that provably cannot observe the resolved shell (§2a/§2m(5)/§2n), the stale-probe termination attributed to a disposable that only removes a Set entry (§2k), and §2m(4)'s blanket UNC refusal against §2n's UNC-rooted S9e lane. Three rev-12 **[code]** citations were imprecise on re-opening and are corrected at their sites rather than silently swapped: `prepareClaudeAuth`'s path-A call site (`:4587-4588`, not `:4586-4587`), §2i's win32 directory dispatch (`secure-file.ts:64`, cache `:66` — `:70-73` is the short-circuit into it), and §2m(1)'s [inferred] ACL reasoning, whose conclusion survives for a different reason than the one stated. **Rev 14 applies review round 3 against rev 13**, and every claim it touches was re-opened again at its cited line in the same tree. Its three *blockers* share the shape rev 13 named and did not finish clearing — a decision written at a site that cannot carry it — but each is now on a path that runs on **every** lane rather than on a corner: the delegation lease had **no bootstrap**, so §2e's trap-door and §3 row 1 / §5's flat refusal could not both ship and one of the two made *every* lane unloadable (§2e, §2a, §6 — the lease joins the consent write set and the trap-door is deleted); §2f's close-wipe had **no rule for an in-flight usage probe**, so a lane the header promises is wiped could hold a live credential at rest afterwards (§2f, §2k); and §2m(5)'s Windows env-key **collapse was sited at the anchor, upstream of the provider merge that creates the duplicate**, on the pivot platform (§2m(5), §2 preamble — it moves post-merge into the provider and the daemon, where the in-tree precedent already sits). Seven majors are fixed at their sites, three of them corrections to rev-13 **[code]** claims that did not survive re-opening: the Windows local-IPC endpoint every "anonymous local caller" refusal is scoped to (`runtime-rpc.ts:1853-1870`, **not** the daemon PTY-host pipe rev 13 cited), the `grep -rn "case-insensitive"` claim in §2m(5) (it returns four non-test hits, and one of them *refutes* the premise the collapse decision was justified by), and §2a's consent-disambiguation refusal, which was **unsatisfiable as written** and is replaced by the control that actually exists. Rev 14 adds no scope and reverses no owner decision. **Rev 15 is not a review round.** It applies two answers that arrived after rev 14 was drafted, and every claim it adds was opened at its cited line in the same tree. **Owner, 2026-08-22 — the shared box runs *native* Windows shells only: Git Bash (the owner) and PowerShell (the other developer), deliberately not WSL.** That resolves §7's first new open question as **native**; **S9e is deferred** — a designed, independently shippable slice, out of the S9 delivery order and out of every gate, with no "blocking" framing left anywhere — and, rather than removing work, it puts two shell-specific obligations into the new **§2m(8)**: Git Bash's MSYS conversion rewrites path-looking *arguments* and passes the **environment block through unchanged**, which is what carries a Windows lane path to a native `claude.exe` from a Git Bash pane (**[inferred]**, gated by the new §5 live step **(12c)**, refused `terminal.lane_shell_unsupported` if it fails); and PowerShell's **`$PROFILE`** runs at pane start — after `computeLaneLaunch`, after §2m(5)'s post-merge collapse and after guard 3 — so it is a **§4 residual** of the shell-rc class, named and not closed. Row 18 names both shells and states what makes both load-bearing: `terminalWindowsShell` is a single **host-wide** value (`ipc/pty.ts:2433` → `local-pty-provider.ts:596-600`; path A passes `requestedShellOverride: undefined`, `shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:41-44`) **[code]**, so the two developers cannot each have their own default, and "native only" is a habit rather than an enforcement — a project runtime of kind `wsl` still forces a WSL shell (`:52-57`) **[code]**, where a lane pane is refused rather than silently un-isolated, which is what makes the deferral safe. **Orchestrator, 2026-08-22 — the federated-link question rev 13 raised is answered (b):** a link→principal binding ships as the **fifth** host-side consent write in `principal-registry.ts`, keyed by the link's home-peer fingerprint (`runtime/orchestration/types.ts:205`, column `runtime/orchestration/db.ts:504`, already authorized against at `orchestration-federation-relay.ts:227` and `orchestration-federation-control.ts:175`) **[code]**, so a federated create binds to the **link's principal** or fails closed `terminal.lane_link_unbound` — one extra human tick per link, and no grant-keyed lane anywhere. Rev 15 also finishes the half of round 3's second blocker that rev 14's kill-first-sweep-second rule left open: **a probe must not *start* into a lane whose wipe is pending or whose `laneState` is `absent`, and the sweep re-reads the lane directory before reporting the wipe done** (§2f, §2k, §5) — killing what is in flight says nothing about the tick that fires a millisecond later. Rev 15 reverses no decision and adds no scope. **Rev 16 applies review round 4 against rev 15**, and every claim it touches was re-opened again at its cited line in the same tree. It fixes one *blocker*, and it is a fail-**open** one rather than the fail-closed kind the earlier rounds found: rev 15's link→principal binding was keyed on the home-peer fingerprint, which rev 15 called "the only stable link identity this tree has" on the strength of where the value is *stored* and *compared*. Re-opened at the function that **produces** it, `authenticatedCallerFingerprint` is `sha256(request.authToken || request.deviceToken || 'authenticated_transport')` (`rpc/orchestration-mutation-executor.ts:128-134`) **[code]**, and the runtime mints **one** `authToken` for every local caller (`runtime-rpc.ts:507`, enforced `:1664-1669`) **[code]** — so on this one-Windows-account box that value is shared by the `orca` CLI, the renderer and **both developers**, and binding a principal to it would have created a federated worktree, its startup terminal and its setup pane in one person's lane **for the other**. The answer stays (b); the binding gains a **bindability rule** (§2a): the two host-wide fingerprints are refused by name, a bindable value must resolve to exactly one paired grant's `token`, and a re-pair falls back to `terminal.lane_link_unbound` until re-ticked. Four *majors*, each a rule that could not hold at the site it was written at: the lease's eligibility predicate was `scope: 'runtime'` — the exact value §2l proves cannot identify a pushing desktop — so it is re-stated as **host-observed push capability** — itself wrong, and corrected by rev 17 below — and §2l's "no request is ever left pending against nobody" is made conditional on it (§2a, §2e, §2l); the `win32` lane-key **collapse** had no site on the usage-probe path, which §2m(5) itself calls the exception, so the probe's env build becomes the **third** collapse site beside the provider's and the daemon's (§2m(5), §2k Fact 3); row 18's "one host-wide shell" holds only on path A — `pty:spawn` carries a per-tab `shellOverride` that wins in the same resolver line (`ipc/pty.ts:5923` → `:6074`/`:6167` → `local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:41`) **[code]** — so the resolved shell is **per-pane**, and that input is enumerated and dispositioned as the new **row 36**; and §6 gate (v) made two S9e-only §5 arms an S9a merge gate, contradicting the deferral two bullets below it, so each is split into the half S9a implements and the half that **activates with S9e**. Minors: `restrictWindowsPathSync`'s false-on-throw is `:60-63`; the four post-anchor `win32` steps are re-stated **in the order the code runs them** (the expansion at `local-pty-provider.ts:847` runs immediately *before* step (d) at `:851`, not after it); row 19 now points at the mechanism that makes "ignored for lanes" true on `win32`; a stalled wipe publishes an additive **`laneWipePending`** beside `absent` so the launch value and the residency claim stop sharing one string; §3 row 11 records the S9e deferral; and three off-by-one citations are corrected at their sites (`local-pty-provider.ts:701-703`, `environment.ts:36`, `claude-fetcher.ts:244-245`). Rev 16 reverses no decision and adds no scope beyond the bindability rule the blocker forces. **Rev 17 applies review round 5 against rev 16**, and every claim it touches was re-opened again at its cited line in the same tree. Its one *blocker* is rev 16's own repair of round 4's first major, and it is the shape this document keeps naming — a decision written at a site that cannot carry it. Rev 16 replaced the lease's `scope: 'runtime'` predicate with "the host has seen it present the `accounts.lane.push` **capability** — the capability gate §3 row 1 already puts on that method", and that sentence joined two capability namespaces that run in **opposite directions**. §3 row 1's gate is the **host-advertised** runtime capability `agent.identity-lanes.v1` (`shared/protocol-version.ts:111-150`), which the **client** checks — the precedent §3 itself cites runs that way (`cli/handlers/account.ts:276-284`, check at `:278`) **[code]** — so it puts no per-grant value in the host's hands; the only per-caller capability set in the tree, `ctx.clientCapabilities` (`rpc/core.ts:79`), is **client-declared** at handshake (`rpc/e2ee-channel.ts:256` through `parseRuntimeClientCapabilities`, which allowlists nothing — any array of ≤64 strings of ≤128 chars, `rpc/runtime-client-capabilities.ts:3-12`) **[code]**, gates no method's *existence* (the dispatcher's method table does that; the set is read in exactly two payload projections, `session-tab-close-methods.ts:14` and `session-tab-agent-status-projection.ts:18`) **[code]**, and **no client in this tree advertises anything resembling `accounts.lane.push`** — all five advertisement sites send the same fixed two-element list (`shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:140`, `:512`; `remote-runtime-request-connection.ts:213`; `remote-runtime-shared-control-frame-handler.ts:44`; `renderer/src/web/web-runtime-client.ts:462`) **[code]** and the mobile clients send none at all. Implemented literally, no grant is designatable, **every** provisioning is refused `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated`, and R1–R3 fail on the one path that gates every lane: round 3's first blocker, back through the fix to round 4's first major. **Rev 17's repair was a wire change plus a re-grading:** the advertisement shipped as a client-declared capability `accounts.lane.push.v1` in the `e2ee_auth` handshake, additive under Rule 1, filtering the consent surface and authorizing nothing. **Rev 19 deletes that mechanism outright (below), so its transport plumbing and its call-site list are not carried forward.** **And rev 16's grading is deleted rather than softened, which is round 5's *major*:** the signal is **client-asserted and host-recorded**, not host-observed — `rpc/core.ts:78`'s own comment says the set is *bound to the socket*, i.e. declared at authentication — so the capability **filters the consent surface and authorizes nothing**; the exclusions of a phone and of a CLI grant are properties of Orca's own builds and are stated as that; the control on designating a non-pusher is the human tick plus re-designation (rule (iii)'s shape — prevention is not claimed, recovery is); and §2l's "no request is ever left pending against nobody" is restated to the scope it actually holds — no frame is emitted against a **disconnected** lease-holder, while a *connected and lane-status-subscribed* one that cannot act still expires `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out`, a named residual of the mis-tick class rather than a hole the predicate closes. §5 gains both arms, including a doctored-handshake phone fixture whose whole point is that it is **not** refused. **Minors:** rule (iii)'s audit sentence still said "each of the three" after the count reached five and described a row shape two of the five do not have; §6's gate (iii) and S9a's gate line restated §2m(1)'s DACL fail-closed rule without the `parseWslUncPath` carve-out every other site carries (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:60-63`, `shared/wsl-paths.ts:6-17`) **[code]** — dormant, since S9e is deferred, and now written scoped anyway; §6's `lane-usage-pull.ts` budget had no site for §2k's two **start-side** probe preconditions, which left S9c's `laneWipePending` flag with a writer and no reader and would have shipped the kill-only fence §5's own mutation proof shows passing while the lane re-loads; `projectRuntime` is row 36's sibling on the same args object and **outranks** it — `kind: 'wsl'` returns `shellOverride: 'wsl.exe'` and a distro regardless of the per-tab override (`shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:52-57` against `:41`) **[code]** — so it is enumerated as **row 37** and asserted as §5's third path-B arm, with no control added, because §2a's refusal already sits on the *resolved* `shellPath`; rev 16's clause (b) made the fifth consent write derivable from the first, so the link row drops its own `principalId` and the create reads `principalOf(boundDeviceId)` — one authority, one revocation point, the extra human tick unchanged (§2a, §6, §7 q8); the runtime credential write is `:421-422`, not `:420-422`, at both remaining sites; and §2m(7)'s Windows-only list gains **(12b)**. Rev 17 reverses no decision and adds no scope beyond the advertisement the blocker forces. **Rev 18 applies review round 6 against rev 17**, and both of its majors are the same shape one last time — a sentence stated at a scope its cited carrier cannot express — so both are fixed by supplying the carrier rather than by softening the sentence. **(1) The eligibility predicate's scope had no storage.** Rev 17 said the surface offers a row for a grant that has presented the string “on at least one connection of this runtime” and, in the same breath, that it “needs no new field on `DeviceEntry`, because the set already lives on the socket” — but the socket record is per-socket and `handleClose` deletes it (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:36-42`, written at `:165-172`, deleted at `:209-212`) **[code]**, so a disconnected grant leaves the runtime nothing. It matters on this design's own transports, because the desktop advertises largely on **one-shot** sockets — opened, authenticated and closed per request (`shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:114`, `:140`, `:512`) **[code]** — while only two connections are **pooled** — `RemoteRuntimeRequestConnection` and `RemoteRuntimeSharedControlConnection` (`ipc/runtime-environment-request-connections.ts:39`, `:122`) **[code]**. **Rev 20 corrects the stronger form this preamble carried through rev 19 — “only the two pooled connections are long-lived” — which was a wrong [code] claim, and it is the sentence a reader hits first (round 8's first minor).** *Pooled* and *long-lived* are not the same property: a subscription socket is held open for the subscription's lifetime without being pooled (`subscribeRemoteRuntimeRequest`, `shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:494`, its handshake at `:512`, sole production caller `ipc/runtime-environment-transport-routing.ts:246`) **[code]**, which is precisely the fact §2l's liveness precondition rests on. §2l always stated the conjunction correctly; this line did not, and now does. Rev 18 budgeted the carrier the stated scope required: a process-lifetime, unpersisted `declaredPushers` set in `runtime/principal-registry.ts`, never a `DeviceEntry` field, emptied by a restart. **Rev 19 deletes that memo with the predicate it carried** — the designation is persisted in the principal registry instead, so nothing about it has to be re-derived from a socket at all. **(2) The advertisement's implementation-site list was wrong twice — rev 17's named a file that sends no handshake, and rev 18's replacement mis-attributed a handshake literal one function down (round 7's major).** Both corrections are **deleted rather than corrected again**, because rev 19 deletes the advertisement they described. **Rev 19 is not a review round; it is the orchestrator's simplification of the pusher designation, 2026-08-22, and it deletes far more than it adds.** Rounds 4, 5 and 6 each repaired the *eligibility predicate* — `scope: 'runtime'`, then a host-advertised capability, then a client-declared one with a runtime-lifetime memo behind it — and each repair was sound against the round before it and wrong in the same place: it asked the **host** to infer which paired grant is a pushing desktop, from a signal the client controls. Round 7 then found the third repair's own site list mis-cited, which is where a fourth repair would have gone. **The orchestrator's decision removes the inference instead of repairing it again.** Deleted at every site: the client-declared capability `accounts.lane.push.v1` (§3 row 12 is struck), the `declaredPushers` memo in `principal-registry.ts`, the `advertisePushCapability` transport option and its call-site list, the §5 fixtures and mutation proofs that existed only for that mechanism, and every “first connect” / “the moment the human is present” framing of *designation*. **What replaces it:** `delegatedGrantId` is set by a plain **host-side consent tick over the principal's already-bound grants**, offered at **any** time on the same local UI — and the same local `orca` command — that binds grants, **persisted in the principal registry**, with an audit row like the other consent writes. Bootstrap for one human with one desktop is **one tick**. A push from any grant other than the principal's `delegatedGrantId` is refused `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated`; a principal with no designation is refused provisioning and push with `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated`. **The host does not try to infer device type, and rev 19 grades each candidate signal at its own site rather than in one sweep:** `clientCapabilities` is client-asserted, copied verbatim from the `e2ee_auth` payload (`rpc/core.ts:78-79`, `rpc/e2ee-channel.ts:256`) **[code]**; `clientKind` is *not* — it is host-stored `device.scope` (`rpc/core.ts:77` ← `runtime-rpc.ts:1784`) **[code]** — but it is written at **pairing-offer creation** from `args.scope ?? 'runtime'` (`runtime-rpc.ts:725`, spent `:733`/`:736`) **[code]** and its type has no desktop value (`shared/runtime-types.ts:56`, `runtime-rpc.ts:660`) **[code]**, so it names the kind of *invite*, never the kind of device. **The orchestrator's brief called `clientKind` client-asserted; re-opening the tree says otherwise, and the correction is stated here rather than carried silently — the conclusion is unchanged and better founded.** The human at the machine is the authority, full stop. **Mis-designation is harmless and recoverable:** tick a phone or an `orca` CLI grant and no push ever arrives, `laneState` stays `absent`, the UI names “no push received from \<label\>”, and the human re-ticks. **Rule (i) is rewritten to the scope its own heading always had:** its “never a free-form list assembled afterwards” prohibition governs **binding** a grant to a principal — at mint, or at that grant's named invite — and designation, being a choice among already-bound grants, is allowed afterwards (§2a). **Rev 19 also settles §2l's liveness precondition against the transport shape this tree actually has** (the delegated desktop must hold the pooled shared-control connection its lane-status subscription runs on; `desktop_unavailable` and `switch_timed_out` partition on that), and applies the round-7 findings that survive the simplification: row 37's `projectRuntime` citations are `ipc/pty.ts:6076`/`:6169`, not `:6075`/`:6168` (§2 row 37, §5's Windows-only arm); §2e's carrier sentence is reconciled with §2a rather than left pointing at a record that cannot answer it; and the §2 preamble's second `TERM` restore is `local-pty-provider.ts:728-730`. Round 7's `:497` / `sendRemoteRuntimeRequestWithStatusPreflight` major is **moot**, and its sentences are deleted with the mechanism rather than corrected. Owner decisions (§7) stand, native Windows shells stand, S9e stays deferred; rev 19 reverses no decision and **removes** scope — one wire row, one memo, one transport flag and one client-side budget line. **Rev 20 applies review round 8 against rev 19**, and every claim it touches was re-opened again at its cited line in the same tree. Round 8 found no blocker and one major, and the major is a *budget* claim rather than a mechanism one: rev 19 recorded the deletion of the advertisement plumbing with the sentence “nothing under `src/shared/remote-runtime-*`, `src/cli/runtime/websocket-transport.ts`, `renderer/src/web/web-runtime-client.ts` or `mobile/` is touched by S9 any more”, which over-reached from the plumbing onto the whole program and contradicted §3's own Mobile degradation row in the same document. Re-opened: `mobile/app/h/[hostId]/accounts.tsx` is the phone's **only** account-switch caller (the method string at `:178`, the send at `:160`) and `grep -rn "claudeAiOauth\|refreshToken\|refreshClaudeOauth" mobile/src mobile/app` returns **zero** hits **[code]** — so §2l's phone half needs a real `mobile/` edit, no revision ever budgeted one, and rev 19 turned that silence into an affirmative denial. §6 now scopes the sentence to the plumbing and **budgets the mobile client in S9b** (§6, §3's Mobile row). The surviving minors are applied at their sites: the preamble's pooled/long-lived claim above, §2l's decision headline and the carrier for the subscription→socket half of its precondition, the disposition of a `delegatedGrantId` whose grant is unbound or revoked (§2a rule (iii)), §2a's stale “on that device's row” phrasing, the **push** arm of `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated` (§2a, §3 row 1, §3's old-client list, §5), §5's pane-binding closing clause, and §2m(5)'s `:728-729`. Rev 20 reverses no decision and adds no scope beyond the mobile client the design already required. What survives from that exercise is one fact the transport reasoning below still rests on, re-opened in this tree: the desktop's ordinary request path is **one-shot** (`sendRemoteRuntimeRequestOnSocket` `shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:114`, reached from `sendRemoteRuntimeRequest` `:86` and `sendRemoteRuntimeRequestWithStatusPreflight` `:96`, handshake `:140`), `subscribeRemoteRuntimeRequest` (`:494`, handshake `:512`) is a *separate* entry point that holds **its** socket open for the subscription's lifetime, and exactly two connections are **pooled** (`ipc/runtime-environment-request-connections.ts:39`, `:122`) **[code]** — the correction above applies here too: *pooled* and *long-lived* are different properties, and §2l's precondition keys on the second. §2l now keys its liveness precondition on that shape instead of on “an authenticated socket” in the abstract. **Minors:** §2a and §5 stated what the predicate made true — a lane was **first-connect-provisionable only**, because a freshly minted row had declared nothing — **and rev 19 withdraws that consequence with the predicate**, so a lane is provisionable at any time after at least one grant is bound (§2a rule (i), §5's designation fixtures); §5's federated mutation proof no longer names rev 17's own grant-keyed link row as the mutation that must turn red (the red arm is the lane *value* `{ kind: 'grant' }`); §2l's closing sentence drops the last surviving “*host-observed*” grading of the push signal for *host-written*; and §2's own revision narrative, which stopped at rev 15, is carried through revs 16–18. Rev 18 reverses no decision and adds no scope. **Rev 21 applies review round 9 against rev 20**, which found no blocker and two majors, and every claim either of them touches was re-opened at its cited line in the same tree (`feat/resilience-presence-integration` @ `21d9e378f1`). **(1) A designated `orca` CLI grant was described as subscribing “like anything else”, and the tree says it cannot subscribe at all.** `subscribeRemoteRuntimeRequest` (`shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:494`) has exactly one production caller, `ipc/runtime-environment-transport-routing.ts:246`, in the desktop's main process; the CLI's whole transport is `src/cli/runtime/websocket-transport.ts`, 51 lines exporting `sendWebSocketRequest` (`:11` → `:19`) and `sendWebSocketRequestWithStatusPreflight` (`:28` → `:37`), one-shot, with no subscribe entry point and no shared-control use (`grep -rn subscribe src/cli`: zero hits) **[code]**. So a `requestSwitch` against a CLI-designated principal — and against a retired or offline desktop — is refused `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable` **before any frame**, never `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out`, and rev 20's §5 arm could only have passed against a subscription no CLI client can open. The `switch_timed_out` residual is scoped to grants that are connected **and subscribed**, which is what §2a already said in its own words, and the **phone** case is unchanged — a phone genuinely does subscribe (`mobile/app/h/[hostId]/accounts.tsx:122`) **[code]** and genuinely answers nothing, which is the mis-designation residual §2a owns. Corrected consistently at §2l, §5 and §3's degradation row. **(2) Rev 20's mobile budget broke the ceiling it cited.** `mobile/app/h/[hostId]/accounts.tsx` is 395 lines / **370** countable under `skipBlankLines`/`skipComments` against the flat `**/*.tsx` max of **400** (`.oxlintrc.json:144-149`), with no override in `mobile/.oxlintrc.json` matching `app/h/*/accounts.tsx` and no entry on the ratchet **[code]** — **30** lines of headroom against a ~40-line edit, and a per-file bump is forbidden (`AGENTS.md:16`, `config/max-lines-baseline.txt:1-4`) **[code]**. §6 now budgets the extraction that makes room — `renderProviderSection` (`:193-327`, ~135 lines) out to a new `mobile/src/accounts/ProviderAccountSection.tsx` (~150) — so the slice lands legally and the file ends smaller than it started. Rev 21 reverses no decision, adds no scope, and changes nothing structurally outside those two findings. **Rev 25 reconciles S9c and S9d against the shipped tree** (§10(c)/(d)): S9c landed with no deviation from §2f/§6; S9d's backend (`terminal.openInMyLane`) landed with three named deviations — the refusal codes named in prose only, the wire-level `seedPrompt` cap ahead of the runtime bound, and no auto-submit on delivery — while its renderer half (the designation surface, the curation tick, the chip wiring, the consent rows) did not ship and is restated as still-budgeted, not silently dropped (§10(e)); two rev-24 §10 items (32, 33) that described the pre-S9c state are superseded in place; the refusal-code enumeration (§3) and the S9d module bullet (§6) are re-derived against the tree; and a new §9 gathers the Windows-only verification the owner must still run before deploy.

**Rev 32 is not a review round. It is the chair's re-basing of the S9 credential *source*, 2026-08-27, and it deletes more than every prior revision combined.** Revisions 1–31 all rest on one premise: a lane's credential is a **copy** of the desktop's, moved over the Orca wire by `accounts.lane.push`. Everything that made S9 hard was a consequence of that copy — invariant L1, the delegation lease and its four rules, the watermark and its three writers, `pullRotated`, the residency index, R-DR2, and the `service.ts:475` refusal that produced the 2026-08-27 incident. **The premise is retired, because it was never a fact about accounts.** `account-residency-index.ts:5-8` states it as a bare assertion — "A refresh token is single-use, so residency is per ACCOUNT" — and `lane-auth-state.ts:10-13` closes the circle: rotation "stays serialized per ACCOUNT, because the single-use refresh token is an account fact **and L1 makes that axis single-valued across every lane**" **[code]**. L1 is the thing that makes the axis single-valued, so it cannot also be the evidence for L1. What the implementation actually performs is rotation *within one blob's token chain*: `refreshClaudeOauthCredentials` reads `claudeAiOauth.refreshToken` out of one credentials JSON, POSTs `grant_type=refresh_token`, and persists whatever new refresh token comes back into that same blob (`oauth-refresh.ts:103-108`, `:124-149`) **[code]**. Nothing in that path is keyed by account. Two authorization-code grants mint two independent chains — which is why the owner's two laptops coexist today, and why `runtime-credential-lane-clearing.ts:11-22` has to say a local `claude` "rotates **the lane's** single-use refresh token": it is *the lane's* token only because push placed a byte-identical copy on both machines. Remove the copying and the sentence has no referent.

**What replaces it, stated once: each lane holds its own OAuth grant, obtained by running `claude auth login --claudeai` inside the lane.** The host spawns that command with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` set to the lane, holds the process alive, relays the authorization URL the CLI prints to the *requesting person's own device*, and writes the code that person pastes back into the **same** host process's stdin. The verifier never leaves that process (PKCE, `code_challenge_method=S256`), no credential byte crosses the Orca wire in either direction, and the lane ends up holding a grant that is **independent of the desktop's** — the ordinary two-laptops case, on a box instead of a laptop. **Orca already ships this exact flow twice**: `cli/handlers/account.ts:201-210` runs `['auth','login','--claudeai']` with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` set and then registers the captured dir, and `claude-accounts/service.ts:611-613` runs the identical command through `runClaudeCommand` with `keepStdinOpen: true` and an `AbortController`, followed by `['auth','status','--json']` at `:615-620` and `captureAuthFromConfigDir` at `:622-626` **[code]**; `service.ts:118-123`'s own docstring states the purpose verbatim — "run `claude login` in the user's own terminal on a headless host, then register the captured credentials without a desktop GUI." Per-lane login is that function with the temp dir replaced by a lane-owned dir and the capture **kept in the lane** instead of copied into the managed store.

**Verified on the VPS, 2026-08-27, by execution and not by inference.** `claude --version` is **2.1.247** (Orca's OAuth constants are pinned against **2.1.177** at `oauth-refresh.ts:4-8` **[code]** — the pin has drifted, and §4 makes re-verifying it a slice gate). `claude auth login --help` on that build offers `--claudeai`, `--console`, `--email <email>` and `--sso`, and no `--no-browser`, `--print-url`, `--code` or device-code flag; none is needed, because the paste path is unconditional. A headless login run in a scratch config dir succeeded as `superiorpvpmc@gmail.com`, produced a grant whose access token **differs from the pushed lane blob's**, a real inference ran on it, and the account remained usable elsewhere throughout. That converts the central claim from strongly-evidenced to **demonstrated for the first grant pair**; what remains unproven is *longevity* — whether each chain still refreshes independently after the first access token expires, and whether Anthropic caps concurrent grants per account (§4, §5's live steps, §6's Gate 0).

**Owner defaults applied below, and every one of them is marked owner-pending rather than ratified.** (1) A lane's credential **stays at rest until an explicit logout, a revoke, or a deprovision** — the disconnect wipe and the startup wipe are deleted, and the unbounded at-rest window that follows is the one place this model is *weaker* than push (§2f, §4). (2) The push/lease/L1/R-DR2/local-clearing/watermark/`pullRotated`/delegable-list model is **deleted outright**, with no compatibility flag (§2b–§2f, §3, §6's S9-L3). (3) A **phone may start a lane login** and display the URL, and may accept the pasted code, because it holds no secret either way (§2l). **Kept unchanged:** per-person lanes and the lane key (§2a), the binding/designation/consent CLI and its five consent writes (§2a, §10 item 39) — with designation **re-meant**, from "which grant may push credentials into this lane" to "which grant may *start a login* for this person's lane, and which grant *sees the URL*" — lane env pinning and the closure principle (§2 preamble, §2a), presence labels (§2h), usage attribution through the statusline (§2k), and the shared lane's behaviour (§2d Q5).

**Rev 33 is the review round on rev 32, 2026-08-27 — five blockers and ten majors applied, every minor that was cheap, and no decision reversed.** Five things changed materially. **(1)** The **lane login session is a post-sweep writer** and now sits inside §2f's lifecycle fence exactly as the usage probe does — cancel the process group on logout / last-grant revoke / deprovision, refuse a start into a lane whose wipe is pending, with a degradation-matrix row and a §5 control of its own (§2f, §3, §5, §6's S9-L1). **(2)** The **wipe's scope is stated normatively** and covers the per-lane account store §2c introduced: a lane holds N logins, and "the credential is gone from this box" was false for every non-active one (§2f, §3 row 2, §5, §9 step 10). **(3)** **I6 is re-scoped.** `expectedEmail` is caller-supplied and the host holds no allowlist to check it against, so I6 binds the lane to the account *the starting human named* and is no defence against an attacker who names the victim's email — §4's URL-substitution bindings drop from five to four and the labelled confirmation is the last one standing (§2b, §3 row 1, §4). **(4)** The **`unverified-legacy` migration is named as the last R-DR2 member** and put on the desktop rotator's clock, because the slice that creates it also deletes the lease and rule (iv) that suppressed that rotator (§3, §4, §6's S9-L3, §7 q17). **(5)** The **host-inline `orca lane login`** is fenced into the same per-lane session as the RPC path, since §9 makes it mandatory on a headless box (§2d(iv), §6's S9-L2, §9 step 0 item 8). Everything else is the removal of push-model text the re-basing missed — §2a's provisioning paragraph rewritten in place rather than appended to, §2h's client-asserted email, §2k's switch-gate and row-join bullets, the §2 surface table's row 4, §3 rows 6 and 8, three §5 fixtures, five stale §7 questions, and §9's step 14 and step 0 items 5–6 — plus the honest re-grades the round asked for: Orca cannot serialize two `claude` processes inside one lane (§2k, §2e), and rev 32 *did* create open review findings (§8).

**Rev 34 is the review round on rev 33, 2026-08-27 — one blocker and five majors applied, every minor the round returned, and no decision reversed.** **(1) The blocker: a login can outlive the host process.** Rev 33's fence stops a post-sweep writer while Orca is running; it stops nothing across a `kill -9`. The lane account store now has an **index** written only after I6 passes, its `.orca-managed-claude-auth` marker is written **before** the child is spawned (so a crashed login's directory stays resolvable and therefore purgeable), `purgeLaneAccountStore` is a **directory walk** rather than a marker-gated loop over the account list, and startup gains an **account-store reconciliation** that deletes every unindexed directory, re-reads, and surfaces a surviving child instead of reporting a clean store — ordered `sweep tmp → reconcile → seed → listen` and deliberately **not** gated on the device registry's `loadSucceeded` (§2a, §2c, §2d, §2f, §3, §5, §6's S9-L1, §9 step 10). **(2)** Binding (i) **splits into (i-a), a precondition on the lane, and (i-b), a test on the caller**, so the host-inline `orca lane login` is exempt from the caller test alone and still refuses a principal holding no designation — closing the contradiction rev 33 opened between §2d(iv) and §2a, §3's revoke row and §5 (§2d, §3, §5, §6's S9-L2, §9 step 0). **(3)** `--email` is **required on every entry point**, not bracketed on the CLI, because the runbook makes that path mandatory on a headless box. **(4)** A designation ***move*** now **cancels** the session it moves off, which is what makes “recovered by a re-tick” true in the window where recovery matters (§2d rule (3), §2a, §2l, §3). **(5)** `loginStatus` gets the caller rule the other three RPCs had, since its session id is published to every bound grant. **(6)** The in-lane switch and the login capture share `lane-auth-state.ts`'s per-lane write queue, so the switch gate's bare `Error` is unreachable by construction and named `accounts.lane.switch_in_progress` on the defensive arm. Minors: the store is capped at eight logins (`accounts.lane.login_store_full`), an **unverifiable** `auth status --json` fails closed exactly as a mismatch does, the login arm of the wipe fence gets the probe's not-confirmed-dead outcome, submit authority is stated across surfaces, the four spawn-side gate citations are re-derived (`ipc/pty.ts:4527`, `:4593`, `:6163`, `:6210` — rev 33 and §6 carried two different wrong sets), §2k's close-wipe bullet stops sourcing its staleness path from the deleted push, and the three remaining I6-as-a-bound-on-mis-designation leans are re-based on rule (i) alone. Refusal arithmetic: **fourteen minted against sixteen retired, 57 → 55**. New owner question: **§7 q19**, on destroying an unindexed login directory at startup and on the eight-login cap.

**Rev 35 is the review round on rev 34, 2026-08-27 — two blockers applied, no decision reversed, and both fixes are the chair's.** **(1) The blocker under rev 34's own reconciliation: it deleted against an index it kept in the wrong place.** Rev 34 wrote "the pass is not gated on `loadSucceeded` because it compares the lane against itself", and then put the account index in `principal-lane-store.ts` — Orca's persisted state, where `Persistence.load` falls through its backup chain to `getDefaultPersistedState(homedir())` on a corrupt file (`persistence.ts:3160`, `:3866-3868`, `:3888`) **[code]** and every collection reads empty, silently. One truncated write would have made every login in every lane unindexed and the pass would have deleted all of them. **The index now lives in the lane** — `<lane>/claude-accounts/index.json`, `0600`, atomic — so the premise is true, the blast radius of a bad index is one lane, and the pass gains a second arm: an index that is **missing, unparseable, or empty over a non-empty store** makes it **quarantine** — rename each `<laneAccountId>` to `<laneAccountId>.quarantined-<ts>`, log, delete nothing — with deletion reserved for directories absent from an index that **parsed** (§2a rule (2) and its orphan-reconciliation paragraph, §2c rules (1) and (2), §2f classes (3) and (5) and the startup pass, §3 row 1 and three matrix rows including a new one, §4's R-34a, §5, §6's S9-L1 obligations (1)(2)(3), §7 q19). **(2) The blocker inside the wipe fence: `cancel` did not reach the capture *tail*, and rev 34's queue gave that tail a patient wait behind the wipe.** The capture step is not the child — it runs after the child exits — so "confirm the child dead" was satisfied trivially while a complete grant was still landing, and a capture queued behind a logout would have written it *after* the sweep, the re-read and the `absent` publish. **The queue has three members, not two** — `selectAccount`, the capture step and the wipe — **the wipe takes precedence** and marks the lane before entering, **both other members re-check after acquiring the queue** (the capture re-checking wipe-pending *and* its own session's liveness) and are refused `accounts.lane.wipe_in_progress`, and **`cancel` invalidates a session in any state before `captured`**, child-exited-but-capture-pending included (§2b, §2c, §2d rules (2) and (3), §2e, §2f's fence, §3 row 9 and two matrix rows, §5's fence fixture, §6's S9-L1 obligation (4)). The shipped tree's `lane-wire-authority.ts:112`-vs-`:120-122` guard — outside the `serializeLaneWrite` it then enters — is named throughout as the pattern S9-L1 must not repeat. No refusal is minted or retired: the count stays **fourteen against sixteen, 57 → 55**. §7 q19's ratification text absorbs both, and gains a third thing the owner is accepting: a quarantined directory is a complete grant left at rest until an operator or the next wipe removes it.
**Rev 36, 2026-08-27 — the chair's own closure of the one blocker rev 35's review left.** Rev 35 grounded the wipe's precedence over a queued login capture in the wipe-pending mark; the mark is cleared in the wipe's own continuation after its queue turn, so a capture admitted afterwards could write a grant into a lane already published `absent`. Rev 36 moves the closure onto the login session's own monotonic `cancelled` state, re-checked by the capture under the queue (§2c), with the mark demoted to an advisory refusal; §2f, §3 row 9, §5 and §6 S9-L1 carry the same sentence. One new refusal code, `accounts.lane.login_cancelled` (fifteen minted / sixteen retired, 57 → 56). No decision reversed; written by the chair under the two-try rule after two delegated rounds.

## 1. What exists today

**One host, one credential file.** All Claude launches resolve through `ClaudeRuntimePathResolver.getRuntimePaths()` — `$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` of the *Orca process* or `~/.claude` (`src/main/claude-accounts/runtime-paths.ts:14-25`) **[code]**. A switch writes the selected account's blob into that one file atomically at `0o600` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:477-478`, `:1725-1743`) **[code]** and merges the account identity into `.claude.json`'s `oauthAccount` (`:1574-1579`) **[code]**. Per-account storage is `<userData>/claude-accounts/<id>/auth/` holding exactly `.credentials.json` and `oauth-account.json`, guarded against symlinks, escapes and foreign ownership by a marker file (`managed-auth-path.ts:12-14`, `:16-66`, `:95-108`) **[code]**.

**A launch only gets Claude auth if its *command string* looks like Claude.** Both spawn paths compute `const isClaudeLaunch = !preAdoptedStablePane && !args.connectionId && isClaudeLaunchCommand(args.command)` (`ipc/pty.ts:4517-4518`, `:6153-6154`) over a regex on the command (`:1924-1931`), and `prepareClaudeAuth` runs **only** when that is true (`:4587-4588`, `:6205-6208`); otherwise `claudeAuth` is null and the spawn env is `sshScopedEnv` with no auth patch merged at all (`:4632-4634`, `:6221-6223`) **[code]**. `markClaudePtySpawned` is gated identically (`:5332-5334`, `:6948-6950`) **[code]**. Today this costs nothing — there is one credential file and `~/.claude` is the default for *every* process on the box, whatever launched it. Under lanes it is the single largest failure mode, and §2a is written around it.

**The live CLI re-reads the file.** Orca depends on this in two directions: read-back preserves tokens a running `claude` rotated into the runtime file before overwriting (`runtime-auth-service.ts:410-453`, persisted at `:440`) **[code]**, and managed refresh is *deferred* whenever any live Claude PTY exists, precisely because refreshing would double-rotate a single-use refresh token (`:459-475`, gate at `live-pty-gate.ts:138-176`) **[code]**. Both run inside `doSyncForCurrentSelection`, whose only drivers are `prepareForClaudeLaunch` (`:137-163`), `prepareForRateLimitFetch` (`:165-177`) and the constructor (`:134`) **[code]** — *a launch or a usage tick is what makes Orca look at the file at all*. That is the mechanism R2 keeps, and it is why the UI still hedges "Restart live Claude terminals before continuing old sessions" (`src/renderer/src/components/settings/AccountsPane.tsx:802`) **[code]**.

**The security gap, plainly.** `accounts.selectClaude` takes an account id and nothing else, and its handler ignores caller identity entirely (`src/main/runtime/rpc/methods/accounts.ts:118-127`) **[code]**; it rewrites the single host file. So **any** paired client — either developer's phone or desktop, any grant — moves the account under **every** live terminal on the box. Its siblings are equally open: `accounts.removeClaude` (`:147-151`), `accounts.selectCodex` (`:128-132`), `accounts.selectCodexForTarget` (`:133-140`), and the disclosure pair `accounts.list` (`:103-117`, snapshot at `:110`) and `accounts.subscribe`, which emits the whole snapshot at two points — the shared fan-out (`:194-196`) and the synchronous ready frame (`:209`) **[code]**. The two credential-capture RPCs are local-socket-only, refused for any identified socket (`:157-172`, refusal `:163-165`; `:173-185`, refusal `:177-179`) **[code]** — §2d shows why that guard is not enough once lanes exist. The auth-switch gate is process-global, so one person's switch blocks everyone's spawns (`live-pty-gate.ts:178-191`; read at four spawn sites, `ipc/pty.ts:4527`, `:4593`, `:6163`, `:6210`) **[code]**.

**Selection is keyed by `{runtime, wslDistro}` only** (`runtime-selection.ts:7-15`, `getSelectedClaudeAccountIdForTarget` `:44-50`) **[code]** — no per-caller dimension exists; one must be added.

**The plumbing precedent exists — and so does a recorded decision against it.** A per-launch `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` already isolates WSL managed accounts: `getPreparation` returns `envPatch: { CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR: activeAccount.wslLinuxAuthPath }` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:683-697`, patch at `:693`) **[code]**, and the launch path applies that patch last (`ipc/pty.ts:4632-4634`, mirrored `:6221-6223`) **[code]**. But Orca also wrote the opposite choice into its settings type — "persist only per-account auth (not a `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` swap) so switching accounts doesn't fork Claude's shared chat/session context" (`shared/global-settings-types.ts:253`) **[code]**. §2a rebuts it explicitly and states what a lane loses; it is not an oversight.

**Claude Code semantics relied on** — and the two refused:
- `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` relocates credentials, `settings.json`, `.claude.json`, session transcripts and memory as a unit **[doc]**; Orca already treats the co-located `.claude.json` as canonical when the dir is inherited (`runtime-paths.ts:27-33`) **[code]**.
- `.credentials.json` is a plain file at `0600` on Linux **[doc]**; on macOS the credential is a Keychain item whose service name is suffixed with the first 8 hex of `sha256(CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR)` (`keychain.ts:85-93`) **[code]** — macOS isolates *by config dir already*.
- The running CLI re-reads `.credentials.json` rather than caching it for the process lifetime **[inferred from the two dependencies above; the single assumption R2 rests on, verified on the box in §5, with a stated fallback in §4 if it fails]**.
- **Deliberately not relied on:** `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` (read once at start, restart to change **[doc]**) — Orca strips it and three siblings at launch (`environment.ts:9-14`; strip armed at `ipc/pty.ts:4742-4744` and `:6467-6469`) and *refuses* a managed launch that defines them (`ipc/pty.ts:4594-4598`, `:6211-6215`) **[code]**; and `apiKeyHelper`, which would be an elegant per-lane hook (re-run on TTL or 401 **[doc]**) but sends its output as both `X-Api-Key` and `Bearer` **[doc]**, has unconfirmed subscription-OAuth behavior **[doc]**, and outranks stored logins **[doc]** — a hijack primitive, not an isolation primitive. Orca has no `apiKeyHelper` in its own code and S9 keeps it that way.

## 2. Design — per-principal credential lanes

**Closure principle — stated once here, and every rule below is a consequence of it.** *For a lane launch, the effective Claude configuration (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, auth env, CLI args, settings resolution, resume paths, hooks) is computed entirely host-side from lane-owned inputs. No client-supplied launch customization and no host-wide launch customization is honored for a lane launch unless it is (i) lane-scoped by construction or (ii) on an explicit allowlist with a stated reason.*

Eight review rounds each found a *different* way in. Rounds 1–6 found *surfaces* — `terminal.create` `env`, `session.tabs.createTerminal` `env`, `launchConfig.agentArgs`/`agentCommand`, `createManagedWorktree`, host-wide `settings.agentDefaultArgs`. Round 7 found something else: two whole **edges** the rule was not sitting on — the renderer-backed create that round-trips through `pty:spawn`, and the sleeping-agent wake that `worktree.activate` fires. Per-surface patching does not converge, and per-edge patching cannot converge either while the lane travels as a launch *value* that some edges carry and others drop. So the rule moves twice: **a lane launch is computed, not customized** (rev 7) — one host-side function *returns* the command, args, env, config dir and resume paths that spawn, and anything not on its allowlist never reaches the CLI — and **the lane is a property of the pane record, not of the launch request** (rev 8). Round 8 found the third kind: the rule was sitting one layer *above* the convergence — two host assembly points, while the env is rebuilt a third time inside the provider and one lane-scoped RPC (`session.tabs.createTerminal`) never reached the funnel at all. So rev 9 moves the anchor down to the three `provider.spawn` calls themselves and states what may run after it. A future launch-shaping input is closed by default; a future spawn *edge* is closed by default too, because it must read the lane from the pane it is spawning into. Round 9 proved that anchor complete by exhaustive grep and found no new surface: rev 10 changes one thing only — rev 9 stated its identity-drop over a *function* whose third caller is not a create, so the drop is now scoped to the two create call sites and the `session.tabs.activate` materialize gets its own stated disposition (§a(i), row 35). **Rev 11 is not a tenth round.** Nine rounds closed rev 10 and it was reviewed sound; what changes here is *scope*, on the owner's decisions of 2026-08-22. Four scope moves: the lane key becomes a **principal** rather than a grant (Q1), so a person's desktop and phone share one lane (§2a, §2l); the **per-lane usage pull** moves out of "a later slice" and into S9b (Q7, §2k); the shared host becomes **Windows Server**, which promotes rev 10's one-paragraph Windows note into a first-class section with its own gates (§2m); and the WSL-visible lane rev 10 deferred becomes an independently shippable slice, **S9e** (§2n). Every enforcement decision rounds 1–9 produced is kept. Where a decision is *re-keyed* rather than replaced, the re-key is stated at its own site and is a widening, never a loosening; where the owner's answer overrides a rev-10 default, the override is named as such. **Rev 12 is a round, and the first one against rev 11's scope moves.** It adds no scope: it fixes two unsound decisions (the revoke wipe, §2f; the second-desktop closure, §2e), completes four re-keys rev 11 stated in one place and left contradicted in another (the ownership predicate, the per-site derivation, the pane binding, the lifecycle wipe), gives three rules a carrier they lacked (Q3's peer-visible label, §2b; the consent step's disambiguation and its unbind/audit path, §2a; the post-probe sync's own trigger arm, §2c/§2k), and closes two gaps the Windows pivot opened (a push landing mid-probe, §2k; `applyClaudeEnvPatch`'s exact-case strip on the lane probe, §2m(5)). **Rev 13 is the second round against that scope, and it adds none either.** Its findings share one shape, which is worth naming because it is the shape a scope revision produces: every one is a decision written at a site that cannot carry it. The lease suppressed the wrong desktop (§2e); the federated create binds to a key its own required field cannot express (§2a); `openInMyLane`'s join stops one hop short of the principal everything else re-keyed to (§2g); the WSL refusal sits at a function whose input type provably has no shell in it (§2m(5), §2n); the stale-probe termination names a disposable that deletes a Set entry and kills nothing (§2k); and §2m(4)'s UNC rule refuses the exact path shape §2n writes every S9e lane through. None of them is a missing surface — round 9 closed that enumeration and rev 13 does not reopen it — and each is fixed **at the site**, with the site's own code re-opened first. **Rev 14 is the third round and rev 15 is not a fourth:** rev 14's three blockers were the same shape on paths that run on *every* lane (the lease's missing bootstrap, the unfenced close-wipe, the env-key collapse sited upstream of the merge that creates the duplicate), and rev 15 carries two owner/orchestrator answers into the document — native shells, which defers S9e (§2m(8), §2n), and a link→principal binding for federated creates (§2a) — plus the start-side half of the close-wipe fence (§2f, §2k). Neither reopens the surface enumeration. **Revs 16, 17 and 18 are rounds four, five and six, and the shape holds for all three.** Round 4 refused rev 15's `scope: 'runtime'` eligibility predicate, which §2l disproves in its own paragraph; rev 16 replaced it with a *capability*, and round 5 showed that capability ran in the opposite direction — host-advertised and client-checked, therefore carrying no per-grant value the host could read — so rev 17 gave the signal a wire: a client-declared `accounts.lane.push.v1` in the `e2ee_auth` handshake, graded as client-asserted and filtering the consent surface only. Round 6 found the same gap one layer down — rev 17's predicate was stated at *runtime* scope over a *socket*-scoped record — and rev 18 put a process-lifetime memo behind it; round 7 then found that revision's own site list mis-cited, which is where a fourth repair would have gone. **Rev 19 is where that thread stops, and it stops by deletion rather than by a fourth repair.** Three rounds went into making the *host* infer which paired grant is a pushing desktop out of a signal the client controls, and the orchestrator's answer is that the host does not infer it at all: the designation is a **plain consent tick over the principal's already-bound grants**, offered at any time, persisted in the principal registry, audited like every other consent write — and a wrong tick is *recovered*, not prevented, because a grant that cannot push simply never pushes and the lane sits visibly `absent` (§2a, §2e, §2l). The capability, the memo, the transport flag and the first-connect framing are all deleted; the shape this document keeps naming — a decision written at a site that cannot carry it — is answered this once by moving the decision to the only site that was ever going to carry it, which is the human. No round since rev 13 has reopened the surface enumeration round 9 closed.

**Rev 8's correction — where the lane lives; rev 9's — how far down enforcement has to sit.** Rev 7 threaded `laneGrantId` through launch options and enforced it at the host's two `ptyController.spawn` calls (`orca-runtime.ts:26845`, `:28602`) **[code]**. Both are real edges; both are *bypassable by a client flag*. `terminal.create` accepts `focus`, `rendererBacked` and `presentation` (`terminal.ts:1558-1561`, and note the schema types `rendererBacked`/`focus`/`activate` as `z.unknown().optional()` at `:1039-1041` — coerced only in the handler, which makes them unvalidated *and*, after this section, security-adjacent) **[code]**, and `shouldCreateInBackground` (`orca-runtime.ts:26662-26673`) **[code]** is false when either is set and a window exists — so the launch is shipped to the renderer over `terminal:requestTabCreate` (`:27089-27108`; a second dispatch in `runCreateMobileSessionTerminal` at `:27479`) **[code]**, queued as a tab startup command (`useIpcEvents.ts:1849-1863`) **[code]**, and spawned from the renderer through `ipcMain.handle('pty:spawn')` (`ipc/pty.ts:5901-5902`) **[code]**: a third spawn edge that carries no lane. Worse, with `worktree` omitted `workspace` is null and `resolveAgentTerminalCreateOptions` is never called at all (`:27055-27057`) **[code]**, so anything anchored “after” it never runs. A launch-option lane is an object a client can steer around. **Decision: the lane is a property of the pane record**, bound host-side when the pane is created and read back by pane identity at every spawn.

**Binding — host-side, at pane creation, from an authenticated request.** The background branch of `createTerminal` already mints `tabId`/`leafId` in main *before* the spawn, and says why: “mint tabId in main before spawn so paneKey is known at PTY env build time” (`orca-runtime.ts:26697-26711`, `makePaneKey` at `:26711`) **[code]**. That mint is the binding moment: `lanePrincipalId` is written into the lane registry keyed by `(worktreeId, paneKey)` before any launch input is assembled. Four consequences. **(i) A lane create never takes a renderer-minted pane — and there are two branches that mint one, not one.** First, `createTerminal`'s own renderer-backed branch: `focus`/`rendererBacked`/`presentation` are presentation preferences, not authority, and the background branch's own comment records that nothing is lost — “the spawned pane is still published and revealed with `activate`” (`:26668-26673`) **[code]**. The one thing that branch hard-requires is a resolvable selector, so a lane create with no workspace selector is **refused** `terminal.lane_requires_workspace`, mirroring the `startupAgent`-requires-selector throw the funnel already has (`:26648-26653`) **[code]**. Second — the round-8 blocker — `session.tabs.createTerminal` (`session-tabs.ts:58-61`, whose handler already reads `pairedDeviceId` at `:60`) → `createMobileSessionTerminal` (`orca-runtime.ts:27302`) → `runCreateMobileSessionTerminal` (`:27373`), whose branch key is **window presence, not a client flag**: `const win = this.getAvailableAuthoritativeWindow()` (`:27060`), `if (!win) return await this.createRuntimeOwnedMobileSessionTerminal(…)` (`:27419-27420`), otherwise `win.webContents.send('terminal:requestTabCreate', …)` (`:27479`) **[code]**. On this two-developer desktop box a window always exists, so that RPC *always* takes the renderer edge: the renderer mints the tab, replies with its own `tabId` (reply handler declared `:27461-27469`, the renderer `tabId` resolved at `:27474`), and spawns through `pty:spawn` under a paneKey with no binding row — which under rev 8's rule resolves to `shared`, i.e. the other developer's `~/.claude`. Its host fallback is no better: `:27545` passes `identity: { tabId: pendingSurface.tab.parentTabId, leafId … }` — a renderer-minted identity — into `createTerminal` at `:27715` **[code]**, so `canAdoptPaneIdentity` fires and the host mints nothing. **Decision: for a lane caller — a request over an identified socket whose grant holds a lane — the host branch is primary, unconditionally.** `createRuntimeOwnedMobileSessionTerminal` (`:27690`) **[code]** runs whether or not a window exists, and for a lane caller its `identity` argument is **dropped at its two *create* call sites, `:27420` and `:27545`, and at those only**, so the host mints `tabId`/`leafId` at `:26703-26711`, binds the lane there, and then publishes and reveals exactly as the background create does. A renderer-minted identity is never adopted into a lane **on a create**. **UX consequence, stated honestly:** this inverts the primary path of that one RPC for one caller class. The lane caller's tab is no longer created by the desktop renderer, so the optimistic tab shell the renderer publishes before the PTY spawns does not appear (`afterTabId`/`targetGroupId` are still honored by the host create); the tab surfaces in the desktop window a beat later, through publish/activate rather than at `tabCreateReply`; and the `tabId` returned to the mobile client is host-minted, so a client that cached the renderer's id sees a different one. A lane-**less** caller keeps today's path byte-for-byte. **The third call site is not a create, and the drop must not reach it — the round-9 MAJOR.** `:8181` sits in `activateMobileSessionTab` (`:8092`), reached by `session.tabs.activate` (`session-tabs.ts:38-41`, `pairedDeviceId` already in scope at `:41`) under `shouldMaterializePendingTerminal` (declared `:8142-8151`), and it passes `identity: { tabId: tab.parentTabId, leafId: tab.leafId, sessionId }` (`:8185-8189`) **[code]** precisely so an **existing** pane is reattached or respawned: `stableSessionId` is then `opts.identity.sessionId` rather than a fresh `serve-…` (`:27712-27713`), and the create runs `canAdoptPaneIdentity` → `adoptStablePane` (`:26739`). Dropping identity there would mint a new session and a second tab while the tapped one stayed non-ready forever — breaking the documented wake gesture (#11598, named in the comment at `:8145-8146`) for exactly the caller class lanes exist for. **Decision — the activate/materialize disposition, stated rather than left to the create rule:** `identity` is **kept** at `:8181` for every caller class; the materialize is gated by §a(ii)'s caller-class-independent predicate at `:26704-26711` and by nothing else, so it proceeds only when the pane's persisted lane *is* the caller's lane (otherwise refused `terminal.lane_not_owned`, before any spawn), and a respawn into that paneKey runs on the **pane's persisted lane** — never the caller's, never rewritten to `shared`. A pane bound **explicitly** to `{ kind: 'shared' }` — the automation path (`index.ts:2613`, rows 3 and 33) — draws the same refusal from a lane caller, because §a(ii)'s rule turns on the caller's own lane *being* that lane and a lane holder's is not the shared one. An **unbound** pane materialized by a lane caller is **refused** `terminal.lane_pane_unbound`, the fail-closed option, for the same reason the wake below refuses rather than resuming: materializing it would put a lane holder's agent tab on the shared `~/.claude` — the other developer's credential — silently, which is the downgrade this whole subsection exists to close, while the other option, promoting the unbound row to the caller's lane, would adopt a renderer-minted identity into a lane and contradict the create rule one clause above. Consequence, stated: a lane holder's pending tab minted before its lane existed (or by a lane-less path) stays non-ready and must be re-created; a lane-**less** caller's materialize is unchanged. **(ii) A client-hinted pane identity may not adopt a bound pane — for any caller class.** `terminal.create` forwards `tabId`/`leafId` hints (`terminal.ts:1562-1563`) **[code]** that the funnel validates into a paneKey (`canAdoptPaneIdentity` `:26704-26708`, `makePaneKey` `:26711`) **[code]** and then hands to `adoptStablePane` (`:26739`) **[code]**. The ownership check lands at **`:26704-26711`** — after the hint validates, **before** the adopt — and it is **caller-class-independent**: adopting a paneKey already bound to a lane is refused `terminal.lane_not_owned` unless the caller's own lane *is* that lane. A lane-less `{ kind: 'shared' }` caller may not adopt a lane-bound pane either, and a lane caller may not adopt a pane bound to another lane. **And the pane's persisted lane governs the respawn**, the case rev 8 left unsaid: when the PTY under a bound paneKey is dead or retired, `attachStablePaneOwner` retires it and returns null (`pty.ts:838-905`) and `spawnForStablePane` spawns **fresh** into that same paneKey (`:916`) **[code]** — that fresh spawn runs on the *record's* lane, so it happens for that lane's owner and for nobody else; for anyone else the adopt is refused before any spawn, and the binding row is **never** rewritten to `shared`. The activate/materialize path (row 35) rests on exactly this split: the predicate decides *whether* the materialize proceeds, the pane record decides *which* lane the respawn runs on. **(iii) The other creators bind the same way**: `createManagedWorktree` binds each pane it mints, `terminal.split` inherits from the parent record through the ownership predicate below, federated creates bind to the **link's principal** — a fifth consent write in the principal registry, keyed by the link's home-peer fingerprint, rev 15's answer to §7 question 8 — or fail closed `terminal.lane_link_unbound`; never to a grant, which the `credentialLane` union has no case for (§a) — and the automation path passes `shared` explicitly. **(iv) The binding is persisted with the pane**, not merely held in memory (§h) — the rev-6 minor is now load-bearing, because cold restore and the wake have nothing else to read.

**And it is never accepted from the renderer.** `pty:spawn` args carry no lane field and one would be ignored if added: `pty.ts:7015` already calls `args` “untrusted IPC JSON” and bounds the paneKey it takes from them **[code]**. The renderer supplies *pane identity*, which the host validates against its own binding table; it never supplies *lane identity*. The residual is the one §2a case (ii) already concedes: a renderer that lies about which tab realized a create can aim an authorized create at a pane of its own — an anonymous local caller inheriting a lane, inside §4's envelope.

**Enforcement — one function, at the convergence point every PTY passes through.** `computeLaneLaunch(paneLane, spawnOptions)` runs inside main immediately before the PTY process exists, on **every** edge, reading the lane from the pane record by pane identity and treating renderer-supplied `env`/`envToDelete`/args/`launchConfig` as inputs it sanitizes — never as a source of lane identity. Rev 9 moves it to the true convergence point, which rev 8 missed by two steps. **Every terminal PTY in `src/main` is created by exactly three `provider.spawn` calls *above the provider layer*, all in `ipc/pty.ts`** — **as shipped, two of the three now go through `spawnWithLane` (`ipc/lane-pinned-spawn.ts:21-27`, the one `provider.spawn` for a *fresh* process at `:26`) and the third is the attach, which is deliberately not routed there (§10)** **[code]** — and the qualifier is not hedging: grep 9 — not grep 6, which is the resume-path pattern; the mis-numbering is corrected here on re-opening — returns a fourth hit, `daemon/daemon-session-owner-resolution.ts:105`, plus a sibling `direct.spawn(opts)` at `:69` the pattern misses, both inside `DaemonSessionOwnerResolver.spawnAttachOnly` (`:57`) and reached only through `daemon-pty-router.ts:42` and `degraded-daemon-owner-recovery.ts:21` **[code]**, i.e. *inside* the `IPtyProvider` implementation layer this same paragraph enumerates below, not at an edge above it — so the anchor is not bypassed and re-running the grep confirms this paragraph instead of contradicting it: `:917` inside `spawnForStablePane` (`:907-919`), reached from path A at `:5066` and path B at `:6646`; `:5019`, the agent-session-ensure branch of path A, which calls the same wrapper over the *same* `spawnOptions` object path A assembled (`:4745-4895`); and `:844` inside `attachStablePaneOwner` (`:838-905`), which is not a fourth edge because it reattaches (`attachOnly: true`, `command: undefined`) and then *validates* that it did — `result.isReattach !== true`, a changed id, or a mismatched incarnation throws `terminal_pane_owner_changed` at **`:895-903`** (the `if (` opens at `:895`), the load-bearing lines. Rev 8 rested that claim on the wrong thing: those `spawnOptions` are **not** always the ones the two assembly points built — `adoptStablePane` passes its own `{ cols, rows, cwd }` with no env at all (`:4416-4429`, esp. `:4420-4424`) **[code]** — so the reattach validation, not the env provenance, is what makes the attach edge safe. `attachStablePaneOwner` has **two** callers and only one is downstream of the anchor — `spawnForStablePane:913` is, `adoptStablePane:4416` is not — and that is harmless for the same reason: the adopt route hands it its own `{ cols, rows, cwd }` with no env, `attachOnly: true`, and the `:895-903` validation refuses anything that is not the same reattached pane, so the `spawnWithLane` wrapper structurally not covering `:844` costs nothing. Everything below the three is an *implementation* of them (`local-pty-provider.ts:538`, `ssh-pty-provider.ts:76`, the daemon adapter/router, `daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:556`, `relay/pty-handler.ts:1564` and its revive twin `:2111`, both `connectionId` panes), and `getProvider(connectionId)` (`:931-940`) **[code]** returns `localProvider` only for a null `connectionId`, so no SSH or relay pane can cross into a host lane. **Decision: the anchor is `spawnForStablePane`.** `computeLaneLaunch` runs at `:905-916` over `args.spawnOptions` — after both assembly points, before the attach delegation (`:909` → `:842`) *and* before the fresh spawn (`:914`) — plus the identical one-line application at `:5043`, which does not pass through it; S9a extracts both into a single `spawnWithLane(provider, spawnOptions, lane)` wrapper so the file cannot grow a fourth uncovered `provider.spawn`. The two assembly points remain what they were — path A, the `ptyController.spawn` implementation (`:4452`, inside `setPtyController` `:4445`), building `env` at `:4632-4634`, merging deletions `:4770-4783`, applying them `:4839`; path B, `ipcMain.handle('pty:spawn')` (`:5902`), building `:6221-6223`, merging `:6470-6482`, applying `:6498` **[code]** — but they are no longer where enforcement sits, and the two host `ptyController.spawn` call sites (`orca-runtime.ts:26845`, `:28602`) are callers of path A, covered with everything else. What the move subsumes, named: **(1)** the `agentTeamsPlan` re-derivation inside `createTerminal`, which rebuilds `command` (`:26851`), `env` (`:26832`), `launchConfig.agentEnv` (`:26812-26814`) and `envToDelete` (`:26861`) from `buildClaudeAgentTeamsLaunchPlan` (`:26766-26775`) **[code]**; **(2)** all **four** host-side `promoteAgentTeamsShimPath` calls — `pty.ts:4739` and `:4795` on path A, `:6454` and `:6487` on path B, all four reaching a **third** definition of it at `pty.ts:1160`, alongside `local-pty-provider.ts:163` and `pty-subprocess.ts:195` **[code]** (rev 9 named two) — now *pre*-anchor on **both** paths — which retires the insertion-range asymmetry rev 8 shipped (path A's range sat before `:4795`, path B's after `:6487`); **(3)** `worktree.activate`'s wake, the cold-restore resume and all nine renderer launch builders, which need no change at all because they are inputs to a computation rather than authorities; **(4)** any edge a later slice adds, since it must reach one of three `provider.spawn` calls. It also makes the *earlier* re-derivation harmless without having to be ordered against it: `resolveAgentTerminalCreateOptions` (`:26082`, called at `:26680` and `:27056`) rebuilds a bare-agent or `startupAgent` launch from host settings (`:25928-25929`) and returns a **replacement** `command`/`env`/`launchConfig` (`:26168-26175`) **[code]** — the substitution that discarded rev 6's upstream sanitizer, and that rev 7 tried to sit behind.

**What runs *after* the anchor — and the invariant that replaces “nothing may re-derive”.** Rev 8 claimed the move left “no *nothing may re-derive between here and the spawn* obligation to keep”. That sentence is retired for good, because the host env step runs a **third** time *inside* `LocalPtyProvider.spawn`: `localProvider.configure({ buildSpawnEnv })` (`pty.ts:2431`, callback `:2439`) registers a builder whose body calls `buildPtyHostEnv` at **`:2455`** with `agentStatusHooksEnabled: isAgentStatusHooksEnabled(ptySettings)` at **`:2508`** and `networkProxySettings: ptySettings` at **`:2476`**, executed at `local-pty-provider.ts:712-722` **[code]** — downstream of both assembly points *and* of the anchor. Its ctx is `{ command, launchAgent, codexHomePathOverride, cwd, shellPath, isWsl, wslDistro }` (`:725-732`) **[code]**: no paneKey, no tabId, no worktreeId, no lane.
