That is why rows 16 and 28 were unenforceable on the non-daemon path (round-8 MAJOR A) — the sites rev 8 cited, `pty.ts:4715`/`:4774`/`:4733` and `:6429`/`:6455`/`:6445`, are **daemon-only**, gated by `isDaemonHostSpawn` (`:4539-4542` with the call gate at `:4711`; `:6174-6177`) **[code]**, so a peer's ungated `settings.update({ agentStatusHooksEnabled: false })` still stripped the hook coordinates from another grant's lane spawn in local mode. **Decision: the provider env-build ctx gains the pane's lane**, set by `computeLaneLaunch` on `spawnOptions` at the anchor and threaded through `PtySpawnOptions` → `buildSpawnEnv`'s ctx, so all three third-site reads become lane-scoped: a lane pane injects the agent-hook env unconditionally (§k's only usage path) and `networkProxySettings` applies as §4-class. **The steps between the anchor and the process, enumerated and classified:** (a) the provider's base merge, `...mergeGitConfigEnvProtocol(stripInheritedBuildModeEnv(process.env), args.env)` (`local-pty-provider.ts:675-676`; daemon twin `pty-subprocess.ts:610`), whose body is a plain `{ ...baseEnv, ...overrideEnv }` (`src/shared/git-credential-prompt-env.ts:11`) **[code]** — the lane patch travels in `args.env`, so **on POSIX** it wins by exact-key overwrite. **On `win32` that is not sufficient, and rev 13 classified this step as clean on a POSIX reading — the correction is §2m(5)'s and it is stated here too, because this is the step the invariant asserts is safe.** Windows preserves the stored casing of each inherited variable, and row 19 explicitly supports the Orca process carrying its own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`runtime-paths.ts:14-25`) **[code]**; if it is stored as, say, `Claude_Config_Dir`, the spread reinstates *that* key before `args.env` adds the canonical one, **both survive into the plain object**, and the child resolves the first case-insensitive match in block order — the inherited shared-lane path, not the lane's. The tree states that rule itself: "Win32 uses the first case-insensitive key, and object order preserves block order" (`src/main/pty/windows-path-segment-merge.ts:18`, tested at `windows-environment-path.test.ts:652-655`) **[code]**. So step (a) is clean only once the duplicate is collapsed **after** it, which is where §2m(5) now sites the collapse; (b) `buildSpawnEnv` (`:705-714`) — lane-scoped by the decision above; (c) the **`envToDelete` replay**, `for (const key of args.envToDelete ?? []) delete finalEnv[key]` (`:717-718`; a **first** replay over the same `args.envToDelete` runs pre-`buildSpawnEnv` at `:685-687`; daemon twins `pty-subprocess.ts:622`, `:781`) **[code]** — the *only* post-anchor step that can remove a lane key, and the reason guard 1 must sanitize `spawnOptions.envToDelete` itself — at the handoff on both paths, `:4839` on A and the assignment `spawnOptions.envToDelete = combinedEnvToDelete` at `:6522` on B — rather than only the host env; (d) `promoteAgentTeamsShimPath` (`local-pty-provider.ts:163` def, called `:851`; `daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:195` def, called `:840`) **[code]**, re-prepending a client-supplied PATH entry (`env.ORCA_AGENT_TEAMS_TEAM_ID` plus `env[PATH]`, captured at `pty.ts:4635`/`:6293`) — **self-directed, row 12 class**: it redirects which binaries the caller's own pane finds, never which credential it resolves. **The two-part invariant:** **(1)** the lane keys — `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, the auth-env strip, the lane's hook coordinates — are written **last on the host side**, by `computeLaneLaunch` at the anchor, so nothing host-side can precede them; **(2)** **no other post-anchor step writes or deletes a lane key** — a `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, a `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` key or an `ANTHROPIC_*` key — and (a)–(d) are the four classes that could, each **asserted** clean. (2) holds in this tree, with one platform qualifier rev 13 did not carry: `buildPtyHostEnv`'s entire body (`:1689-1922`) contains no such write **[code]**; (c) — the one step that could *delete* a lane key — is closed by guard 1; and (a) — the one step that can *resurrect* one, and only on `win32`, by reinstating a differently-cased `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` out of `process.env` ahead of the lane patch — is closed by the post-merge lane-key **collapse** §2m(5) sites inside the provider and the daemon. Rev 13 asserted (a) clean unconditionally, which was a POSIX reading of an exact-key overwrite; the assertion now names its condition. The remaining post-anchor steps were read line by line and touch no lane key: `removeUnspecifiedPaneIdentityEnv`/`removeAppImageRuntimeEnv`/`removeInheritedNoColor` (`local-pty-provider.ts:686-688`), the `TERM` restores (`:692-694`, `:728-730` — rev 18 wrote `:727-729`; `:727` is the closing brace of the `envToDelete` replay at `:725-726`, corrected here on re-opening, and §2m(5) already cited the construct correctly), the `LANG`/`PYTHONUTF8`/`CHERE_INVOKING` writes (`:696-705`), the win32 `CODEX_HOME`/`ORCA_CODEX_HOME` deletions (`:731+`) and the daemon twins of all of them. **Four further win32 steps run on `finalEnv` — rev 12's enumeration stopped at two of them and rev 13 kept that count; both are corrected here, because the claim is that the invariant is a property of *every* step, and the platform it was under-enumerated on is the one the design pivoted to. Rev 15 said they run “after all of those”, and that ordering is wrong on re-opening: the expansion runs immediately *before* step (d), not after it.** The true `win32` order is `expandWindowsPathEnvironmentVariables(finalEnv)` at `local-pty-provider.ts:847`, then step (d) `promoteAgentTeamsShimPath` at `:851`, then `stripLegacyTerminalShimEnv` at `:856`, then `injectHistoryEnv` at `:868`; the daemon has the same shape with the collapse interleaved — expand `pty-subprocess.ts:831`, collapse `:833`, promote `:840`, strip `:842` **[code]**. The classification is unaffected, because it is per-step and not per-position, but the enumeration is stated in the order the code runs them. The four, then: on the **daemon only**, `collapseWindowsPathEnvKeys(env, requestedEnv)` (`pty-subprocess.ts:833`, def `:163-190`) — which the local provider has **no equivalent of at all**, the asymmetry blocker 3 of round 3 turns into a decision (§2m(5)); `stripLegacyTerminalShimEnv(finalEnv, process.platform)` (`:850`, daemon twin `pty-subprocess.ts:842`); and `injectHistoryEnv(finalEnv, …)` (`:862`) **[code]**. None writes a lane key: the expansion rewrites **only** `path`-cased keys and skips every other name — `if (key.toLowerCase() !== 'path' || typeof value !== 'string') { continue }` (`src/shared/windows-environment-expansion.ts:31-36`) **[code]**; `collapseWindowsPathEnvKeys` filters on `WINDOWS_PATH_ENV_KEY_RE = /^path$/i` and deletes only PATH-cased keys (`pty-subprocess.ts:169-188`) **[code]**; `stripLegacyTerminalShimEnv` deletes only `LEGACY_TERMINAL_SHIM_ENV_KEYS`, every one of which is an `ORCA_*` name (`pty/legacy-terminal-shim-dir.ts:22-31`) **[code]**, and otherwise edits PATH; and the history injection writes shell-history coordinates. So the invariant survives, and it is stated as a property of *every* step rather than as an enumeration a later edit could falsify — but the enumeration is now complete on `win32` as well as on POSIX, which is what made the claim checkable. The control is a test, not a code reading: after the spawn args are built, assert the env the provider actually hands the process carries exactly the lane's `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and none of the auth keys, with a mutation proof on each of (b) and (c).

**`worktree.activate`, the wake, and cold restore — blocker 2, split into the half that computes away and the half that does not.** `worktree.activate` (`rpc/methods/worktree.ts:82-97`) forwards `clientKind` and **no `pairedDeviceId`** to `activateManagedWorktree` (`orca-runtime.ts:22160-22182`) **[code]**, whose mobile-scoped wake runs on the desktop host renderer (comment at `src/renderer/src/lib/sleeping-agent-session-launch.ts:66-67`) and, when the record has no stored `launchConfig`, rebuilds the resume from **host-wide settings** — `resolveTuiAgentLaunchArgs(record.agent, state.settings?.agentDefaultArgs)` (`:89`), the `Env` twin (`:93`) and `agentCmdOverrides` (`:85`) — then spawns through `pty:spawn`. The *argument* half closes at the new anchor: that spawn is path B, the lane comes from the pane record, and `computeLaneLaunch` discards settings-derived args and env for a lane pane, so grant B's ungated `settings.update({ agentDefaultArgs: … })` cannot ride A's resume. The *pane* half does not: the wake does not reuse the sleeping pane — it calls `state.createTab(…)` and then `clearSleepingAgentSession(record.paneKey)` (same file, `:111-137`) **[code]** — so the resumed pane is renderer-minted and carries no binding at all, which under the rule above means `shared`: a silent downgrade onto the other developer's credential. **Decision:** `SleepingAgentSessionRecord` carries the lane of the pane it slept from (persisted with the record, keyed by the same paneKey), and the wake is partitioned host-side — lane-bound records are **excluded from the renderer wake** and resumed only through the host create path, which mints the paneKey and binds the lane before spawning; `worktree.activate` gains `pairedDeviceId`, and a caller the ownership predicate rejects leaves the record **asleep** (`wake_refused_not_owned`) rather than resuming it on anyone's credential. Cold restore is the easy case: `src/renderer/src/components/terminal-pane/pty-connection.ts:5030-5045` (`resolveTuiAgentLaunchArgs` `:5039`, its `Env` twin `:5043`, `agentCmdOverrides` `:5035`) **[code]** respawns *the same pane*, so its persisted lane resolves at `pty:spawn` and its renderer-rebuilt args are sanitized like any other input.

**One structural note, from the grep and not from memory:** `callerSuppliedLaunch` (`:26089-26094`) makes the two families mutually exclusive per launch — a caller-supplied `env`/`launchConfig` returns `opts` untouched (`:26109-26110`) and a `startupAgent` combined with one *throws* (`:26101-26105`) **[code]** — so no single launch is shaped by both a client and host settings today. That is an implementation detail of one function, not a security boundary: the principle is stated over the **effective** configuration and enforced at the single point after both, so it survives that function changing.

**Every surface, enumerated by grep.** The commands below *are* the enumeration; re-run them against this tree to check it.

```
grep -rn "createTerminal(\|createManagedWorktree(\|ptyController.spawn\|ptyController?.spawn" src --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."
grep -rn "resolveTuiAgentLaunchArgs\|resolveTuiAgentLaunchEnv\|buildAgentStartupPlan\|resolveBareAgentLaunchCommand" src --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."
grep -rn "agentDefaultArgs\|agentDefaultEnv\|agentCmdOverrides\|launchPreferences\|startupAgent\|startupCommand\|startupEnv\|startupLaunchConfig\|setupDecision" src/main --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."
grep -rn "name: '" src/main/runtime/rpc/methods/{client-ui,accounts,repo,automations,ai-vault,terminal,session-tabs,worktree}.ts
grep -rn "CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR" src --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."
grep -rn "resumeProviderSession\|ompResumeFilePath\|transcriptPath" src/main src/shared --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."
grep -rn "getConfigPath\|applyAgentStatusHooksEnabled\|agentStatusHooksEnabled" src/main --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."
grep -rn "pty:spawn\|requestTabCreate\|activateManagedWorktree\|sleeping-agent\|resolveTuiAgentLaunch" src --include="*.ts" --include="*.tsx" | grep -v "\.test\."
grep -rn "rendererBacked\|shouldCreateInBackground\|canAdoptPaneIdentity\|persistPtyBinding\|provider.spawn(\|spawnForStablePane\|buildSpawnEnv\|promoteAgentTeamsShimPath\|createRuntimeOwnedMobileSessionTerminal" src/main --include="*.ts" | grep -v "\.test\."
```

This call site is **not a create**: it exists to reattach or respawn the pane the tapped tab already names, through `canAdoptPaneIdentity` → `adoptStablePane` (`:26739`), so §a(i)'s drop is scoped to the two create call sites (`:27420`, `:27545`) and never reaches `:8181`. §a(ii)'s caller-class-independent predicate at `:26704-26711` is then the whole gate: a pane bound to another lane is refused `terminal.lane_not_owned` before any spawn; a pane bound **explicitly** to `{ kind: 'shared' }` (the automation path, row 3's `index.ts:2613`, and row 33) is refused the same way for a lane caller, since §a(ii) excuses only the caller's *own* lane; a respawn runs on the pane's **persisted** lane, never the caller's, and the row is never rewritten to `shared`; an **unbound** pane materialized by a lane caller is refused `terminal.lane_pane_unbound` rather than spawned onto the shared `~/.claude` (fail-closed, §a(i)). Requires `session.tabs.activate` to pass `pairedDeviceId` as *caller identity*, not only as a navigation id (§6) |
| 36 | `pty:spawn`'s per-tab **`shellOverride`** (`ipc/pty.ts:5923`), passed to the Windows resolver at **both** of path B's sites (`:6074`, `:6167`), preferred over the setting by `resolveLocalWindowsTerminalRuntimeOptions` (`shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:41`), supplied by the renderer from the tab (`terminal-pane/pty-connection.ts:3591`, sent `:3831`; minted `terminal/terminal-tab-create.ts:39`; a whole `newTerminalWithShell` entry point at `tab-group/useTabGroupWorkspaceModel.ts:656-672`) **[code]** | the Electron renderer (anonymous, local) — row 24's edge | **allowlisted, same class as row 18 one layer lower**: it selects which shell *binary* runs, never which credential — the lane env is written by `computeLaneLaunch` at the anchor regardless. **Two things it changes and rev 15 did not carry:** row 18's “both developers' lane panes take the same default shell” holds for path A only, so a lane pane can resolve to `pwsh.exe` or `cmd.exe` as well as the two shells of §2m(8); and a `wsl.exe` override is caught **only** by §2a's provider-sited refusal over the *resolved* `shellPath` (`local-pty-provider.ts:733`), never by the setting — which is exactly why that refusal is sited in the provider and not at the anchor. Path B is row 24 — cold-restore respawn, the sleeping-agent wake and the nine renderer launch builders — i.e. the edge every **existing** lane pane re-enters through. **Its sibling on the same args object outranks it and is row 37** (rev 17) |
| 37 | `pty:spawn`'s per-tab **`projectRuntime`** (`ipc/pty.ts:6076`, `:6169` — beside `requestedShellOverride: args.shellOverride` at `:6074`/`:6167` and `settings: getSettings?.()` at `:6075`/`:6168`; rev 17 cited the `settings` line at both sites and rev 19 corrects it on re-opening), supplied by the renderer from the same builder one line below the `shellOverride` spread (`terminal-pane/pty-connection.ts:3831-3832`) **[code]** | the Electron renderer (anonymous, local) — row 24's edge, identical to row 36 | **allowlisted, same disposition as row 36, and enumerated because the completeness claim above is a claim about *this table*.** It is not a second control gap: §2a's WSL refusal is sited on the **resolved** `shellPath` in the provider (`local-pty-provider.ts:733`), which catches every route into the resolver. What it changes is the *ranking* row 36 states — a runtime of `kind: 'wsl'` returns `shellOverride: 'wsl.exe'` and a distro **regardless of the per-tab override** (`shared/local-windows-terminal-runtime.ts:52-57`, against the override-honouring branch at `:41`) **[code]** — so `projectRuntime` is the input that can force a WSL pane past both the host setting (row 18) and a per-tab override (row 36), and a `repair-required` runtime *throws* out of the resolver (`:46-50`) **[code]** before any lane env is computed. §5's path-B fixture drives the resolver through `shellOverride: 'wsl.exe'`; the `projectRuntime: { kind: 'wsl' }` route is the third arm and is asserted there too |

### (a) The lane, and how a launch gets pinned to one

**Principals — the lane key, and the only thing that binds a grant to one (Q1 = YES, overriding rev 10's default).** Rev 10 keyed a lane by `pairedDeviceId`, so the owner's desktop and the owner's phone were two lanes, and L1 forbade them holding the same account; rev 10's own open question 1 defaulted to "no — one grant, one lane". The owner overrides it: *"if we use mobile it should be an extension of our desktops targeting the same shared dev box."* A lane belongs to a **person**. **Decision: the lane key is a host-minted `principalId`** — the lane directory is `<userData>/claude-lanes/<principalId>/` — and a grant reaches a lane only by being **bound** to a principal. Everywhere below, "the caller's lane" means *the lane of the principal the caller's grant is bound to*, and a grant bound to no principal is exactly rev 10's lane-less grant, with rev 10's behaviour byte-for-byte.

**What binds a grant to a principal — and what emphatically does not.** The binding is written by the **same host-side consent action** that provisions a lane (§2a provisioning, below): the local human, at the machine, ticks *"this device belongs to \<person\>"* on that device's row and either picks an existing principal or mints one. That act is the sole writer, it is the same class of act as approving the pairing itself, and **no client can cause it** — the reason is unchanged from rev 10 and now covers a strictly larger blast radius, so it matters more, not less. In particular the pairing **name** is not authority. `DeviceEntry.name` is a free-form string (`device-registry.ts:25-41`, field at `:27`) handed to `addDevice(name, …)` (`:79-85`) and stamped into the row by `createAndPersistDevice` (`:87-110`, `name` at `:96`) **[code]**; it originates from the *local* `--pair-name` flag, one grant per occurrence (`src/cli/specs/serve.ts:14`, `:25`; `src/main/index.ts:1824`) **[code]**, and `mintPendingDevice` mints a fresh row on **every** call and never coalesces (`device-registry.ts:142-160`, and the comment at `:138-141` says why) **[code]** — so two invites both typed `Ana` are two unrelated rows carrying one identical string. It is host-supplied, which makes it a good default label to **propose** at the consent step, and it is unconstrained and non-unique, which makes it useless as a key. **B2's `--pair-name` is a hint for the UI; `principalId` is the authority.** The rev-10 refusal stands and now guards the binding as well as the lane: a device entry with no `pendingExpiresAt` (`:102-103`, minted only on the `mintPendingDevice` path) cannot be bound to a principal at all, because nothing on the host can tell whether a coalesced row is one human or two (`getOrCreatePendingDevice`'s find at `:118-136`, the consequence stated at `:138-141`) **[code]**.

**The consent step is itself ambiguous, and the non-uniqueness proved one paragraph above is what makes it so — rev 11 proved the premise and missed the consequence.** The entire authority model rests on one host-side tick, and the row the human ticks carries nothing that identifies a *device*: `DeviceEntry` is `deviceId`/`name`/`token`/`scope`/`pairedAt`/`lastSeenAt` plus optional relay, connection-mode, reach and `pendingExpiresAt` fields (`device-registry.ts:25-41`) **[code]** — the `deviceId` is a UUID, the timestamps are timestamps, and `name` is the free-form non-unique string just shown to be useless as a key. Two rows both rendering as `Ana` are exactly what the human is asked to choose between. **A mis-tick is not cosmetic:** it binds one developer's phone to the *other* developer's principal — one lane, one credential, silent cross-switching, R1 and R3 failing precisely as they do in the coalesced-QR case this section refuses for the same reason — and rev 11 specified no way to detect it and no way to undo it. **Three rules, shipped with the registry and not as UI polish.** **(i) *Binding* a grant to a principal is offered only at a moment the human is present for one specific device** — at mint, or at the named invite that produced that grant — never as a free-form “bind any row to any person” list assembled afterwards. **Rev 19 states the prohibition's scope exactly, which is the scope this rule's own heading always had, because revs 17 and 18 let it drift onto a second act it was never written for.** Binding answers *whose device is this*: it is the act that decides which person's credential a grant can reach, so it is confined to the moment the human is holding the device and the host already knows the exact row — at mint the row it just minted, at the invite the row that invite produced. **Designation — choosing which of a principal's already-bound grants is the one that pushes — is a different act with a different blast radius, and it is deliberately allowed afterwards, at any time.** It chooses among rows this human has already vouched for; it grants no reach the binding did not already grant; and a wrong choice produces nothing at all (provisioning, below, and §2e). So rule (i) forbids a free-form bind-any-row-to-any-person list and does **not** forbid a designation list, which is by construction the short list of one principal's own grants. **Revs 17 and 18 derived a “lane provisioning is first-connect-only” consequence from the eligibility predicate rev 19 deletes; that consequence is deleted with it** — a lane is provisionable at any time once at least one grant is bound, including for a grant that has never connected, because the designation is a persisted human choice and not an observation of the wire. **(ii) The consent row renders every mint-time fact the host actually holds**, so the human can match it to the act they just performed: the `--pair-name` string as a *label*, the deviceId's short prefix, the scope, `pairedAt`, `pendingExpiresAt`, and whether the grant has ever connected (`lastSeenAt === 0`, the same discriminator `isMintedPendingDevice` uses, `device-registry-pending-grants.ts:14-16`) **[code]**. **Rev 13 attached a *refusal* to that rendering — "when two candidate rows are indistinguishable on all of those, the consent step refuses rather than guessing" — and re-opening the registry shows that refusal can never fire, so it is deleted rather than reworded.** `DeviceEntry.deviceId` is `randomUUID()` (`device-registry.ts:95`) and `pairedAt` is `Date.now()` (`:99`) **[code]**: two rows are unique on those two facts *by construction*, so "indistinguishable on all of them" is unreachable and the rule was a control that looked like one. Worse, it was aimed at a problem rule (i) has already removed: at mint the host knows the exact row it just minted, and at a named invite it knows the row that invite produced — the row is never ambiguous *to the host* at either binding moment, and on any later connection the socket carries `socket.device.deviceId` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1384`) **[code]** anyway. **What (ii) actually is, stated as what it is:** a legibility requirement. The facts are rendered so the **human** can tell two `Ana` rows apart before ticking; they do not gate anything, and no refusal is derived from them. **And the failure this subsection opened by calling "not cosmetic" — the human ticking the *wrong person* for a device that is unambiguously identified — is not addressed by (i) or (ii) at all.** Its only control is (iii): unbind, plus the audit row that makes the mis-tick visible in the first place. That is stated plainly here rather than left implied by a refusal that cannot fire, because a reader who believed the refusal was the control would think the mis-tick was prevented, and it is not — it is *recovered*. **(iii) Bind is reversible and audited, which is what turns a mis-tick from permanent into a five-second correction.** `principal-registry.ts` (§6) ships five operations, not one: **bind** (only onto an *unbound* row), **unbind**, **re-bind as unbind-then-bind — never a rewrite in place** — added in rev 14, because §2e's delegation lease has no other writer once its trap-door is deleted — **designate this principal's `delegatedGrantId`** — since rev 19 a plain tick over the principal's already-bound grants, available at any time and persisted like the bindings themselves — and, added in rev 15 on the orchestrator's answer to §7 question 8, **bind a federated link's home-peer fingerprint to a principal**, without which a federated worktree create fails closed (§2a's per-site derivation) (§2a's pane-binding decision depends on the third of these: an in-place re-bind is the mutation that would silently re-point live panes). **Each of the five** writes a local, host-only audit row, and rev 13's "each of the three — device, principal, direction, timestamp" is stale in both halves (round 5 minor): the count is three revisions old, and two of the five do not have that shape. The row is *the subject the write names, the principal, what happened, and a timestamp*: bind / unbind / re-bind carry the device and a direction; **designate-the-pusher** carries the designated grant and no direction at all; and the **link binding** carries `{ homePeerFingerprint, boundDeviceId, boundAt }` per clause (b) below — no principal of its own, since the create reads `principalOf(boundDeviceId)` (rev 17). All five are surfaced in the consent UI, because without it the failure is invisible and the only remedy on offer is a revoke, which under §2f is not a lane wipe at all. **Effect on already-bound panes, stated because this rule and §2a's pane-binding rule are one design:** a pane persists a `lanePrincipalId` and never a device id, so unbind and re-bind **re-point no live pane** — panes bound to principal P stay on P's lane whoever that device now belongs to. What unbind *does* change is the wipe join (§2f): that grant's sockets stop counting toward P, so if it was P's last connected grant, P's lane wipes at the next close evaluation, exactly as a disconnect would. **And — rev 20, round 8's fourth minor — unbind *also* clears the designation when the row it removes is the designated one, because `delegatedGrantId` is a persisted field naming a **bound** grant and cannot outlive the binding it names.** The principal drops to *no designation*: the surface renders an unticked list rather than a label sourced from a `DeviceEntry` that no longer exists, the next provisioning is refused `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated`, and every push is refused `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated` (§2a). A **revoke** takes the same disposition for the same reason — `deviceRegistry.removeDevice` drops the registry row (`runtime-rpc.ts:648` on the paired path, `:660` on `revokeRuntimeAccess`) **[code]** — so on the case §2f is explicit about, revoking the designated desktop while the phone survives, the **lane survives and the designation does not**: the lane sits at `laneState: 'absent'`, no push arrives, and one re-tick on a surviving bound grant restores it. That is the same inert/recoverable outcome §2a already names for a mis-designation, and stating it here is what keeps a persisted field from dangling past the act that deleted its subject. The clear is itself an audited write (a `designate` row naming no grant), so the audit trail shows *why* the lane went silent. **Rev 32 keeps this rule verbatim and renames the two refusals its last clause names, because the act they guard changed (§2a's provisioning paragraph, §2d(i)):** a principal left with no designation is refused `accounts.lane.no_login_device_designated` rather than `accounts.lane.no_pusher_designated`, and an undesignated bound grant that tries to *start a login* is refused `accounts.lane.login_not_designated` rather than a push being refused `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated`. The clearing behaviour, the audit row, the surviving-lane disposition and the one-tick remedy are all unchanged; what changes with the designation's meaning is that a lane whose designation was cleared **keeps working** — it stays `loaded`, its terminals keep launching and any bound grant can still switch (§2c) — and only *new logins* are blocked, where under push the same clear left the lane unable to receive a credential at all.

**`principalId` is minted like a lane id and validated like one.** It is a fresh `randomUUID()` in the exact shape the registry already mints its *device* ids in (`device-registry.ts:95` is `deviceId: randomUUID()`; no principal mint exists in this tree yet, so the shape is a precedent, not an observation) **[inferred]**, validated **at creation** against the same v4 regex §2a applies to lane ids, so no principal id reaches `path.join` unvalidated and containment stays defence-in-depth rather than the primary check. `DeviceEntry` gains one nullable field, `principalId?: string` — additive, local schema, absent on every legacy row, and an absent value means "no lane", i.e. today.

**Everything rev 10 keyed by `pairedDeviceId` re-keys by `principalId`, and every re-key is a *widening*.** Named one by one, because each has its own site and its own test: **the ownership predicate** — `resolveInheritedLane` case (i) compares `sourcePane.lanePrincipalId === principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` instead of raw device ids — the pane already stores the principal, so the left side is a read and not a second lookup — and case (iii) reads "the caller's principal holds no lane"; case (ii) is unchanged. The normative statement of this predicate is at its own site below, not here; **the adopt/materialize gate** at `orca-runtime.ts:26704-26711` **[code]** compares principals, so the owner's phone may adopt or materialize a pane their own desktop minted — which is the single largest UX gain of Q1, because it makes the documented wake gesture (#11598, row 35) work across one person's devices instead of refusing `terminal.lane_not_owned` at their own pane; **`terminal.split`** and the **worker / federation `inherit`** edges likewise; **the sleeping-agent wake** matches the record's principal; **`openInMyLane`** (§2g) creates under the caller's principal's lane, and its attachment join walks `attachmentsOf(ptyId)` → `connectionId` → participant → `pairedDeviceId` → **principal**; **the presence owner label** (§2h) renders the *principal*, so a person's desktop pane and phone pane show one owner rather than two; ~~**the residency index** (§2e) keys its rows by principal and `assertNotLaneResident` names the holding *principal* in its refusal~~ — **deleted in rev 32 with L1, struck here in rev 33 rather than left in a list of live re-keys** (§2e); **login-session authority** (§2d — "push authorization" through rev 31) resolves the target lane as `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` and refuses a grant with no principal; **revoke** removes the *grant*, and removes the lane only when the revoked grant was that principal's **last** (§2f states it normatively, at the site, with the ordering the two revoke paths force). **The pane binding is the one this list first omitted, and it does not re-key by indirection.** The value **persisted on the pane's binding row is the principal id itself** — `lanePrincipalId`, resolved once at bind time from `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` and never resolved again; the older name `laneGrantId` survives in this document only where a sentence is describing rev 10's shape, and every site listed in §3 rows 4 and 8 carries the principal. **Why storing the grant id and resolving late is not acceptable**, since it is the cheaper version and it fails twice: **(a)** revoke deletes the `DeviceEntry` first — `deviceRegistry.removeDevice(deviceId)` at `runtime-rpc.ts:648` and `:660`, *ahead* of `forgetGrant` at `:653`/`:666` **[code]** — so `principalOf` would return nothing for **every pane that grant created**, and under §2h's own rule those panes would render `credentialLane: 'unknown'` and refuse a split, a recovery *and* a resume while the lane is alive, the person is still working, and the degradation matrix promises “the lane survives”; **(b)** the device→principal binding is mutable by construction — the consent action “picks an existing principal or mints one” — so a re-bind would silently re-point every live pane that device created at a different person's lane. **Two rules close both, and both are asserted in §5:** the persisted value is a principal id, never a grant id; and a *bound* device row is never re-bound in place — re-binding is unbind-then-bind (below), and neither leg touches a pane, because no pane stores a device id to begin with. The **wipe** is the one that cannot simply re-key, below.

**The wipe predicate is the one re-key today's code cannot express, and it must be rebuilt rather than re-keyed.** §2f rests on B2's `hasOtherConnections`, and that value is computed by comparing **device tokens**: `Array.from(this.authenticatedSockets.values()).some((candidate) => candidate.device.deviceToken === socket.device.deviceToken)` (`rpc/mobile-socket-wiring.ts:209-221`, comparison `:215-219` esp. `:218`) **[code]**, then spent as `if (!hasOtherConnections) { this.runtime.onClientDisconnected(socket.device.deviceToken) }` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1408-1410` — rev 13 cited `:1407-1409`; `:1407` is `this.binaryStreamHandlers.delete(socket.connectionId)`, corrected here on re-opening) **[code]**. A token is per **grant**. Left alone, the desktop closing its socket would wipe the lane while the same person's phone is still attached to it, and the phone's next launch would fail closed — the precise opposite of what Q1 asks for. **Decision: the lane wipe is not driven by `hasOtherConnections` at all.** `onClientDisconnected` keeps its signature, its token argument and its existing callers; the lane wipe becomes a **separate** subscriber at the same close site, asking the socket registry directly — *does any authenticated socket remain whose grant is bound to this principal?* — over the same `authenticatedSockets` map `handleClose` already enumerates (`:218-227`) **[code]**, joined through `principalOf`. Rev 10's key-mismatch note survives and gets sharper: the site must read `socket.device.deviceId` — in scope there, and already what `registerConnection` is passed at `runtime-rpc.ts:1382-1387` (esp. `:1384`) **[code]** — resolve it to a principal, and use the token for nothing but B2's own bookkeeping. Control in §5: two devices of one principal connected, the desktop closes and the lane **survives**; the phone then closes and the lane **wipes**. Mutation proof: reusing `hasOtherConnections` as the lane predicate turns that test red.

**What Q1 costs, stated rather than buried.** (1) A principal is a **larger** blast radius than a grant: losing one of a person's devices exposes that person's lane, not that device's lane. That is the owner's explicit choice and it sits inside the same §4 envelope as everything else, but the design does not describe it as free. (2) Revoking **one** of a person's devices does **not** wipe the lane — only revoking the last one does — so the revoke confirmation must say which of the two it is doing, or the local human will believe a revoke removed the credential when it did not. Named residual, UI obligation in S9d. (3) L1 stops biting the case it was written to annoy: one person's desktop and phone now legitimately share one lane holding one account, which is **one** residency and not two. Rev 10's degradation row *"Second desktop/grant, same human … Convenient or wrong depending on Q1"* resolves to convenient and is rewritten in §3. (4) The hard part — **who pushes from the phone** — is not solved by the re-key and gets its own section, §2l.

A **lane** is one directory, `<userData>/claude-lanes/<principalId>/`, used verbatim as that principal's `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` on every grant bound to them. It holds `.credentials.json`, `.claude.json`, `settings.json`, and — as a consequence of relocation **[doc]** — that grant's session transcripts. Directory `0700`, files `0600`. New module `src/main/claude-accounts/principal-credential-lane.ts` reuses the ownership discipline of `managed-auth-path.ts:16-66` (no symlinks, canonical containment under the root, exact relative-segment match, marker file) — re-parameterised for this key, not borrowed as-is: that check hardcodes a two-segment `<accountId>/auth` shape (`:46-53`) **[code]**. Lane ids — now principal ids — are validated **at creation** against the exact `randomUUID` shape the registry mints (`device-registry.ts:95`) **[code]** — `/^[0-9a-f]{8}-[0-9a-f]{4}-4[0-9a-f]{3}-[89ab][0-9a-f]{3}-[0-9a-f]{12}$/` — so no grant id ever reaches `path.join` unvalidated; containment is defence-in-depth, not the primary check. **Rev 32 adds one subdirectory and changes nothing else about this paragraph:** the lane also holds `<lane>/claude-accounts/<laneAccountId>/auth/`, one directory per login the lane has performed, each governed by `managed-auth-path.ts`'s rules re-rooted at the lane — symlink refusal, canonical containment, the exact `<laneAccountId>/auth` two-segment match, the marker file, `0600` atomic writes (`managed-auth-path.ts:16-66`, `:68-93`) **[code]** — with the lane's *active* login materialized into the lane's own `.credentials.json` and `.claude.json` exactly as before (§2c). A `laneAccountId` is a lane-local `randomUUID()` Orca mints at login-start, never a desktop managed-account id, which the host does not have and must not be told. **Rev 34 states the two orderings that decide whether this store can ever hold a grant nothing owns, because rev 33 left both to the implementer and round 2's blocker walked through the gap.** **(1) The marker is written *before* the child is spawned.** Orca mkdirs `<laneAccountId>/auth` and writes its `.orca-managed-claude-auth` marker first, and only then spawns the login child into it — because `resolveOwnedClaudeManagedAuthPath` returns `null` for a directory with no *valid* marker (the gate at `managed-auth-path.ts:54-61`, validity at `:95-108`) **[code]**, so a marker written *after* capture would make a crashed login's directory invisible to `listLaneAccounts` **and** to anything else built over the same resolution, while a complete grant sat inside it at `0600`. The opposite ordering has the opposite defect — a directory that exists holding no credential — and §2c states what that is and how it reads. **(2) The lane's account *index* is the authority on what the store holds; the directory listing is not — and rev 35 puts that index *inside the lane*, which is what makes acting on it safe.** The index is one file, `<lane>/claude-accounts/index.json`, `0600`, written through `writeFileAtomically(path, contents, { mode: 0o600 })` — the same helper, and the same stage-then-rename shape, the store's credential writes already use (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:7-15`, the tmp name at `:12`, the mode at `:14`, the rename at `:15`) **[code]** — holding one row per login: `laneAccountId`, the verified identity, the owner-set label (§2b), which one is active. A row is written **only after I6 passes**. **It is deliberately not a row in Orca's persisted state, and that is rev 35's correction of rev 34.** Rev 34 put it in `principal-lane-store.ts` beside the deleted watermark rows — rows that lane store holds through `persistence` and not on disk of its own (`principal-lane-store.ts:66`, `:135-137`; `getClaudeLaneCredentialWatermarks` at `persistence.ts:7304`) **[code]** — and `Persistence.load` (`persistence.ts:3160`) catches a read or parse failure at `:3866-3868`, tries the backup chain, and on exhaustion assigns `getDefaultPersistedState(homedir())` at `:3888` **[code]**, so **every** persisted collection reads **empty**, silently, in exactly the way `DeviceRegistry.load`'s bare catch yields zero devices. One truncated write, one JSON corruption, one restore from a backup predating the last N logins would then make every login in every lane on the host unindexed *at once*, and the pass below would delete all of them. Inside the lane the blast radius of a bad index is **one lane**, the pass that reads it reads nothing else, and §2f's reconciliation compares the lane against itself in fact rather than only in wording. **A directory with no row in a *successfully parsed* index is an orphan by construction, whatever it contains**, and §2f's startup pass deletes it; a directory that pass cannot judge — because the index is missing or does not parse — is **quarantined and never deleted** (§2f).

**Provisioning is an event, and no client can cause it.** A lane exists for principal P only after a **host-side consent action** — the local human ticks "this device belongs to \<person\>" (minting P, or joining this grant to an existing P) and "give this person their own Claude account lane" — **both on that device's row** in the pairing/accounts UI — **and designates *which of this principal's bound grants may start a login for this lane and see the authorization URL*** (rev 14 introduced this tick because §2e's lease had no other writer; rev 32 deleted the lease and kept the tick with its new meaning, stated in full below), which since rev 19 is a tick on a **list of that principal's grants** rather than a per-device checkbox (the paragraph below states the surface exactly; rev 14's "this device's row" phrasing is corrected here, round 8's fifth minor, because it is true only of the one-human/one-desktop bootstrap and stale for the re-designation the rewrite exists to allow). Or the human runs the local `orca` equivalent over the local socket. On the Windows host that local socket is a **named pipe**, not a Unix socket: the runtime's own endpoint is `\\.\pipe\orca-${pid}-${suffix}` on `win32` and `join(userDataPath, 'o-<pid>-<suffix>.sock')` on POSIX (`createRuntimeTransportMetadata`, `runtime/runtime-rpc.ts:1853-1870`, the win32 branch at `:1864` and the POSIX one at `:1869`) **[code]**. **Rev 13 cited `daemon/daemon-spawner.ts:105-108` for this, and re-opening shows that is the *daemon PTY-host* endpoint (`getDaemonSocketPath`, `:98-110`) — a different pipe, reached by a different client. The citation is corrected here rather than swapped silently, and the substance rev 13 missed is in the correct file:** the in-tree comment reads "named pipes lack the chmod hardening of Unix sockets; a per-runtime suffix avoids a stable, guessable endpoint name" (`:1863`) **[code]** — i.e. on Windows the POSIX socket's mode-bit protection is replaced by obscurity plus the pipe's default DACL, which is a materially different statement from "the Unix socket becomes a named pipe". §2m(6) states what that changes about who counts as "local". It is the same class of act as approving the pairing itself. **The designation is part of that write set, and provisioning fails closed without it.** `delegatedGrantId` is an *authority* value — since rev 32 it decides which grant may **start a login** for this lane and **see the authorization URL** (§2d(i)) — so under this paragraph's own rule ("no client can cause it") it cannot be written by a client action. (Rev 14 reached the same conclusion for the lease it then guarded, deleting §2e's rev-13 trap-door — "pinned from the `delegation` member of the first accepted push" — because it would have let any grant of the principal seize the lease by racing to push first, including the second desktop §2e spent a paragraph closing. The lease is gone in rev 32; the reasoning about who may write an authority value survives it intact.) The consent step therefore also **designates one of that principal's bound grants as its login device**, writing `delegatedGrantId`, and a lane whose principal has **no designation** is **not provisionable**, `accounts.lane.no_login_device_designated`. **Designation is a plain host-side consent tick over already-bound grants, and rev 19 makes that the whole of the mechanism — the orchestrator's decision, 2026-08-22.** The surface lists the principal's own bound grants — the rows this human already vouched for under rule (i) — rendered with the same mint-time facts rule (ii) requires, and the human ticks one. It is the **same** local pairing/accounts UI, and the same local `orca` command, that binds grants, and it is offered at **any** time: in the provisioning tick itself (one human with one desktop is **one tick** — bind, provision, designate), or later, to move the designation to a different bound grant. The value is **persisted in the principal registry** beside the bindings — not memoized for a process lifetime and not read off a socket — so it survives a socket close, a disconnect and an Orca restart; and it writes an **audit row** like the other consent writes (rule (iii)): the designated grant, the principal, what happened, and a timestamp, with no direction. **What makes a grant eligible is nothing whatever except being bound to this principal, and that is a decision to stop inferring rather than a looser inference.** Rev 15 keyed eligibility on `scope: 'runtime'`; rev 16 on a capability the host advertises; revs 17 and 18 on a capability the client declares, plus a runtime-lifetime memo to hold it after the socket died. **All of it is deleted in rev 19, and the reason is one fact about this tree rather than three separate mistakes: every candidate signal is either absent or *client-asserted*, so none of them is evidence.** `export type DeviceScope = 'mobile' | 'runtime'` (`src/shared/runtime-types.ts:56`, the field at `device-registry.ts:29`) **[code]** has no desktop value at all — `'runtime'` covers an `orca` CLI grant exactly as it covers a desktop (`revokeRuntimeAccess` gates on `device?.scope !== 'runtime'`, `runtime-rpc.ts:660`) **[code]**. `clientCapabilities` is copied verbatim out of the client's own `e2ee_auth` payload (`rpc/core.ts:78-79`, set at `rpc/e2ee-channel.ts:256` through `parseRuntimeClientCapabilities`, which allowlists nothing — any array of ≤ 64 non-empty strings of ≤ 128 chars, `rpc/runtime-client-capabilities.ts:3-12`) **[code]**, and `rpc/core.ts:78`'s own comment says the set is *bound to the socket*, i.e. declared at authentication and never observed behaviour **[code]**. **`clientKind` is the other candidate a reader will reach for, and rev 19 refuses it for a *different* reason rather than lumping it in with the handshake — the drafted wording said "client-asserted" and did not survive re-opening.** `ctx.clientKind` (`rpc/core.ts:77`) is set from `device.scope` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1784`) **[code]**, so it is host-stored and not asserted per request. What it is *not* is an observation of the device: `scope` is written once, at **pairing-offer creation**, from `args.scope ?? 'runtime'` (`runtime-rpc.ts:725`, spent at `:733`/`:736`) **[code]** — a label chosen by whoever made the invite — and its type has no desktop value, so the other developer's desktop, an `orca` CLI grant and a script all carry the same `'runtime'`. It answers *what kind of invite was issued*, never *what kind of device is on the other end*. **So the host does not attempt to determine what kind of device a grant is — no signal in this tree can tell it — and no rule in this document depends on one. The human at the machine is the authority, full stop.** That is not a weakening: §4's pinned assumption already says a paired grant is shell-equivalent on this host, so a signal a grant can simply assert about itself was never going to add authority — what a designation costs an attacker is a human tick, and that is unchanged. **Mis-designation is bounded and recoverable — not *inert*, and rev 32 is where that changed, so this bullet is rewritten rather than annotated.** Tick an `orca` CLI grant, or a desktop that has since been wiped, and the failure is still inert: that grant cannot carry a human through a browser round-trip, so no credential byte is written, `laneState` stays `absent`, and every launch into that lane fails closed exactly as it does for a lane that was never provisioned (§2f, §2h). Tick the **wrong device of the right person** — their phone, their other desktop — and it is **not** inert: a login needs a browser and a person, not a credential store, so that grant can load this principal's lane. What still bounds it is this rule's own scope: designation ranges only over grants **already bound** to this principal under rule (i), so the worst case is that person's own other device loading that person's own lane, and **rev 34 drops the I6 clause rev 32 leaned on here**: the mis-designated grant is the *starting caller*, so it supplies `expectedEmail` itself and I6 constrains only its own mistyping, never which account the lane ends up holding (§2b, §4) — rule (i) is the bound, and rule (i) is enough. Nothing leaks, no other principal is touched, and the mis-designated grant gains no authority it did not already hold — it may load **this** principal's lane and nothing else. The honest grade is **bounded to this principal's own devices, and recovered by a re-tick** (§2l, §3's degradation matrix, §4). **The UI names the condition instead of rendering an empty state:** a designated-but-silent lane reads *“lane designated to \<label\>; no login completed”*, using rule (ii)'s label, beside the re-designate control **and beside the “sign this lane in” action** (§6's S9-L2) — the remedy is a login somebody has to run, not a push somebody has to wait for, and the string has to say so. The remedy is one tick, audited like a re-bind, and it is the same remedy this subsection reached two paragraphs above for the consent mis-tick, for the same reason: **prevention is not claimed; recovery is.** A **correctly** designated phone is a **working** configuration, not an inert one: it can display a URL and take a code, holding no secret either way, so it can load the lane, and a phone-only principal is provisionable and *usable* (§2l, §3's degradation matrix). Rev 31's opposite reading — a phone “genuinely cannot push”, so a phone-only principal “simply never loads” — is deleted with the push it depended on. **The residual rev 22 named here is gone with its subject, and rev 33 removes it rather than leaving a deleted mechanism in front of a reader.** It was about a designated grant that is *connected and holding a lane-status subscription* but cannot act, so a phone's `requestSwitch` was emitted rather than refused and ran to `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out`, while a designated `orca` CLI grant — connected, but with no subscribe entry point — was refused `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable` before any frame. Rev 32 deletes `requestSwitch`, the liveness precondition and both codes: any bound grant switches in its own lane directly and no other device is consulted (§2l, §3 row 9). What replaces it is smaller and is stated above — an undesignated bound grant may **switch** but may not **start a login**, refused `accounts.lane.login_not_designated`, and that refusal is immediate rather than a timeout. **And the refusals read for what they are:** `accounts.lane.no_login_device_designated` says *no grant has been designated as this principal's login device*, never “no `runtime`-scoped grant” and never “no grant declared a capability”, so the wording cannot drift back onto an enum or onto a claim of observation the host cannot make. Moving the designation later is that same consent surface and that same audit row as a re-bind (rule (iii) above); §7 question 4 asks only whether it should also move *automatically*, and the answer there is still no. **What this buys, and it is the whole of round 3's first blocker:** §3 row 1 and §5's arm can keep the flat rule — a **login start** whose caller is not the principal's `delegatedGrantId` is refused `accounts.lane.login_not_designated`, with no "unless the principal has no designation yet" carve-out — because provisioning writes one and a lane is never *created* without one. (Through rev 31 that same sentence read `accounts.lane.push_not_delegated` over `accounts.lane.push`; the method and the code are both deleted, §3 row 1, and the rule transfers unchanged to the writer that replaced it.) **Rev 20 states the one way a provisioned lane can later hold none, and it is a refusal rather than a carve-out:** rule (iii)'s unbind/revoke clear (above) leaves an existing lane with no designation, and that case is refused `accounts.lane.no_login_device_designated` on both the login-start and the re-provisioning path — never permitted, never auto-designated. **Rev 34 states that this holds on the host's own shell too, because rev 33's §2d(iv) read as an exemption from it and three sections then said opposite things about a headless box whose designated grant was just revoked.** The host-inline `orca lane login` is exempt from binding (i)'s *caller test* — there is no `pairedDeviceId` to compare against `delegatedGrantId` — and **not** from its *precondition*: a principal holding no designation is refused the same code at the same site on that path too, and the operator's remedy is one more command on the same shell, `orca lane designate --person <p> --device <d>`, an audited consent write rather than a silent auto-designation. That keeps a headless box self-sufficient — the CLI both designates and signs in — without giving the lane a second door that opens on a state this paragraph calls fail-closed. **The refusals on the login-start path carry codes too, and rev 20's rule survives the re-basing with its subject swapped (round 8's sixth minor).** An `accounts.lane.loginStart` from a grant bound to no principal, or against a principal with no provisioned lane, is **refused** — `accounts.lane.no_login_device_designated` whenever the named principal has no designation (evaluated at the same site as §3 row 1's `delegatedGrantId` comparison and **before any spawn**), and `accounts.lane.login_not_designated` when the principal *has* a designation and the caller is not it. Both carry a complete human sentence under §3's Rule-3 row, and both are in §3's old-client list. (Rev 20 wrote this over `accounts.lane.push`, `no_pusher_designated` and `push_not_delegated`; all three are deleted in rev 32, and "before any write" becomes "before any spawn" because the login start's first side effect is a child process.) It is never auto-provisioning: otherwise any paired grant — including the other developer's phone — could flip host state that changes what *other* clients are allowed to do (§2d), which is exactly the cross-grant influence R1 exists to close. Deprovisioning is the same UI, and wipes (§f). **Rev 32 re-means the designation without moving it, and states the one thing that gets worse.** `delegatedGrantId` is still written by exactly this tick, still ranges over exactly this principal's already-bound grants, still persists in the principal registry, still writes the same audit row, and is still moved by the same one-tick re-designation. What it *names* changes: from "the one grant that may push credentials into this lane" to **"the one grant that may start a login for this lane, and the one grant that sees the authorization URL"** (§2d(i), §2l) — a Rule-3 meaning change on a value old clients render, with the wire name kept for the same reason §2h keeps `credentialLane: 'grant'`. **Rev 33 applies that re-meaning *throughout this paragraph* rather than appending it, which is what rev 32 did and what the review round found.** The designation's meaning, the provisioning refusal's name, the mis-designation grade, the correctly-designated-phone case, the deleted switch-path residual, the UI string and both push-arm refusals are all rewritten above, so the sentences a reader meets first are the current rule and not a push-model statement with a correction bolted on the end. One thing is worth restating once here because it is the whole of why the loss is a loss and not a hole: designation ranges only over grants **already bound** to this principal under rule (i), so a mis-tick reaches that person's own other device and that person's own lane, never another principal and never another lane — but *not* “never an account the human did not name”, which was I6's advertised scope through rev 32 and is not this rule's (§2b, §4, rev 34). Prevention is still not claimed; recovery is, and the thing being recovered from is no longer nothing. The rule that made §3 row 1's refusal *flat* — provisioning writes a designation, so a lane is never created without one, and the only later way to hold none is rule (iii)'s clear — is unchanged and is what keeps the login-start refusal flat in the same way.

**Orphan reconciliation, gated on a registry read that actually succeeded.** At startup, walk `<userData>/claude-lanes/` and delete any lane directory whose principal has no surviving bound device entry — revoke-time wiping does not cover registry rebuilds or restore-from-backup. But `DeviceRegistry.load` wraps the whole read/normalize in a bare `try { … } catch { this.devices = [] }` (`device-registry.ts:350-385`, catch at `:382-385`) **[code]**, so a JSON parse error or a truncated write at `:359` — on **every** platform — yields **zero** devices. **The mode rev 11 named here is withdrawn, in the same breath as §2i withdraws it, rather than left standing in one section and retracted in the other:** the `hardenExistingSecureFile(this.registryPath)` at `:358` **[code]** cannot throw on `win32` at all — both of its arms end in an asynchronous, error-swallowing best-effort ACL call (§2i, re-verified at `secure-file.ts:167-175` → `:69-89` → `:201-209` → `secure-path-windows-acl.ts:24-41`, callback `:37-39`) **[code]**. It *can* throw on POSIX, where the same dispatch ends at a synchronous `chmodSync` (`secure-file.ts:210`) **[code]**. So the zero-device modes are corruption everywhere plus a POSIX permission failure — and Windows, the platform this design pivoted to, is the one platform on which this particular call is structurally incapable of producing them. Unconditional reconciliation would then delete *every* lane on the host, including the transcripts §f deliberately preserves and — after rev 32, which deletes the watermark (§2c) — the lane's own login store, `<lane>/claude-accounts/<laneAccountId>/auth/`, whose loss costs a browser round-trip per account rather than a re-push. **Decision:** `load` records a `loadSucceeded` flag; reconciliation runs only when it is true **and** the registry is non-empty, and otherwise logs and deletes nothing. Negative control in §5. **And rev 34 states what this gate does *not* cover, because the review round came looking here for round 2's blocker and found the wrong pass; rev 35 states what that other pass is gated on instead, because "ungated" was not a safe answer.** This one reconciles *lane directories against principals* and is gated on a registry read. The **lane account store** is reconciled by a second, independent pass that compares the lane's own account index — `<lane>/claude-accounts/index.json`, which as of rev 35 lives *in the lane* (rule (2) above) — against that same lane's own `<laneAccountId>` subdirectories (§2f). That pass reads no registry row at all, so it is **not** gated on `loadSucceeded`: gating it would leave a crashed login's grant at rest on precisely the corrupt-registry boot where nothing else runs either. **It is not ungated, though, and rev 34's version was.** Its gate is this paragraph's gate applied to the input it actually reads: it deletes only against an index it **read and parsed successfully**, and when a lane's index is missing or unparseable it **quarantines** that lane's unindexed directories — renaming each `<laneAccountId>` to `<laneAccountId>.quarantined-<ts>` — logs, and deletes nothing (§2f). Per lane, never host-wide: one unreadable index costs one lane its automatic hygiene, not every lane its logins. **And the two passes are ordered, which rev 34 left unstated:** this lane-level pass runs **first**, and the store pass runs over the lanes that survive it — reconciling the store of a lane that is about to be deleted wholesale is work with no reader.

**A grant is not automatically one human, and *binding* must refuse the ones that are not — this is now a principal-binding rule, not only a lane rule.** The whole authority model keys on `pairedDeviceId`, but the ordinary regenerate-QR link *coalesces*: `getOrCreatePendingDevice` returns the first never-scanned entry of that scope instead of minting (`device-registry.ts:118-136`, the `find(...)` call at `:125-127`; `:128` opens the `if (existing)` — rev 13 cited `:125-129`, corrected here on re-opening) **[code]**, and the registry states the consequence itself — "two humans handed one coalesced link land on one deviceId and one token, so nothing downstream can tell them apart. A grant can only be split per person at offer time" (`:138-141`) **[code]**. If the two developers paired through that link they share one `deviceId`, therefore one lane; either one's switch moves the other's terminals, **R3 fails silently**. B2's per-person path is `mintPendingDevice`, which never reuses a pending row and stamps a deadline (`:142-160`; `pendingExpiresAt` written at `:102-103`) **[code]**. **Decision:** principal **binding** — and therefore lane provisioning — **refuses** a device entry that carries no `pendingExpiresAt`. That field is the durable discriminator — written at mint, surviving scanning, whereas `isMintedPendingDevice` additionally requires `lastSeenAt === 0` and stops matching the moment the person connects (`device-registry-pending-grants.ts:14-16`) **[code]**. Consequence, stated: a pre-B2 or coalesced grant cannot be bound to a principal, and therefore cannot hold a lane, until it is re-paired per person — the correct failure, because nothing on the host can tell whether such a row is one human or two.

**The lane env must NOT be gated on the launch command. This is the correction rev 5 most needed.** Rev 5 threaded `laneGrantId` into `prepareClaudeAuth` and stopped — but `prepareClaudeAuth` is reached only when `isClaudeLaunch` is true (`pty.ts:4589-4592`, `:6205-6208`, predicate `:4517-4518`/`:6153-6154` over the regex at `:1924-1931`) **[code]**. So a lane-pinned pane created as a plain shell — bare `terminal.create` with no `command`, `session.tabs.createTerminal`, a worktree default tab, any non-Claude `startupAgent` — would receive **no** lane env, and the human typing `claude` at that prompt would resolve `~/.claude` (`runtime-paths.ts:14-25`) **[code]**: on this box, the other developer's credential. Meanwhile §h would still render the pane `credentialLane: 'grant'` from its `lanePrincipalId` and §k would attribute its statusline usage to the lane owner — silent misattribution, the exact mode the funnel exists to prevent. **Decision: decouple.** A lane-pinned spawn (`credentialLane.kind === 'principal'`, no `args.connectionId`, normalized target runtime `host`) gets the lane `envPatch` **regardless of `args.command`**; `isClaudeLaunchCommand` keeps its existing jobs and gains a lane arm. Three consequences, all deliberate:
1. `stripAuthEnv: true` now arms on **every** lane pane, so `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` (`environment.ts:59-72`) **[code]** refuses a lane pane that defines Anthropic auth vars and `authEnvToDelete` (`pty.ts:4742-4744`, `:6467-6469`) **[code]** deletes them from it. That is a behavior change for plain shells in a lane, and the right one.
2. `markClaudePtySpawned` (`pty.ts:5363-5365`, `:6998-7000`) **[code]** keeps its command-detected arm **and** additionally registers every lane-pinned pane into that lane's "may hold lane credentials" set, because the human can type `claude` at any prompt. Deliberately conservative: it over-defers rotation (§e) rather than risk a double rotation. The cost — a lane whose panes never run `claude` never gets a *host* rotation — is absorbed by the CLI's own refresh plus §c's `cli-observed` receipt, and it is what makes §f's "a lane launch fails closed when the lane is unloaded" true for ordinary panes.
3. The second Claude-compatible runtime, **OpenClaude**, is missed by the command regex entirely (`openclaude` has no path separator before `claude`, so `:1924-1931` cannot match), and decoupling gives it the lane *env* where a command regex never could. **But rev 7's “covers it for free” claimed isolation, and rev 8 grades that claim: it is unsupported.** Orca's own OpenClaude integration hardcodes `configDirName: '.openclaude'` (`claude/hook-settings.ts:28-31`), resolved as `join(homedir(), configDirName, 'settings.json')` (`:72-74`) **[code]**, and the tree contains no `OPENCLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and no config-dir env for it at all — `grep -rn "OPENCLAUDE_" src` returns **nine** hits (rev 13 said three, and the count is corrected here on re-opening rather than the claim quietly restated): `claude/hook-settings.ts:28`, `openclaude/hook-service.ts:2` and `:7`, and six in `claude/hook-service.test.ts` — none of them a config-dir env, which is the substance the sentence rests on and which re-opening confirms **[code]**. Whether the binary honors `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is **[inferred] and unverified**, so the design refuses the assumption instead of resting on it: if it does not, an OpenClaude pane in a lane shares one credential store across grants while §h renders `credentialLane: 'grant'` and §k bills the usage to the lane owner — the worst class in this design. **Decision, fail closed:** the host **refuses to build an OpenClaude launch for a lane pane** (`terminal.lane_agent_unsupported`), and lane provisioning marks OpenClaude unavailable *in that lane* — a lane-scoped, host-computed version of row 15's disable. What that cannot cover is a human typing `openclaude` at a lane prompt; there the managed OpenClaude hook reports the session per pane — the reporting is in the **shared** `ClaudeHookService`, whose hook script posts `--data-urlencode "paneKey=${ORCA_PANE_KEY}"` and exits early when that variable is unset (`claude/hook-service.ts:103-118`, the paneKey field at `:112`) **[code]**; `openclaude/hook-service.ts:4-8` merely constructs that service with `OPENCLAUDE_HOOK_SETTINGS`, and rev 13 cited the construction as if it were the reporting — so an observed OpenClaude session in a lane pane **downgrades that row to `credentialLane: 'shared-runtime'` and drops its usage** rather than misattributing it. Named residual in §4, control in §5, and a §5 live step settles the question on the pinned binary — if OpenClaude does honor `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, this item collapses to one allowlist line.

**Lane derivation *and binding* live at the `createTerminal` funnel, not at one handler.** `runtime.createTerminal` (`orca-runtime.ts:26639-26641`) **[code]** has **twenty** production call sites — fourteen `this.createTerminal(...)` inside `orca-runtime.ts` (`:17328`, `:22595`, `:22657`, `:22694`, `:22985`, `:23027`, `:23743`, `:23867`, `:24094`, `:24219`, `:26322`, `:26515`, `:27257`, `:27715`) plus `terminal.ts:1542`, `coordinator.ts:382`, `orchestration-worker-topology.ts:66`, `orchestration-federation.ts:171` and `ipc/worktree-remote.ts:315`/`:367` **[code]** — and several are reachable by a paired grant. **S9a makes `credentialLane` a required field of `TerminalCreateOptions`** (`orca-runtime.ts:1457-1507`) **[code]**: `{ kind: 'principal', principalId } | { kind: 'shared' } | { kind: 'inherit', fromPtyId }` — rev 10's `{ kind: 'grant', grantId }` re-keyed, not merely renamed (§2a). Required, not optional — the compiler enumerates every call site and a new spawner cannot be added without deciding. `createTerminal` asserts it at runtime and **throws for any caller — identified, anonymous, or host-internal — that supplies neither a lane nor an explicit `{ kind: 'shared' }`**. Fail-closed is a property of the field being required, not of the caller having an identity; rev 5's "throws for an identified caller" left the host-internal path (below) silently sharing. The resolved value is not passed onward as a launch option: it is **bound to the pane** the funnel is minting (§2 preamble), and every spawn edge reads it back from there.

**Per-site derivation** — the §2 table's rows 1–3 expanded only where a site needs its own rule. `terminal.create`, `session.tabs.createTerminal` and `worktree.create` derive the lane as `principalOf(socket.pairedDeviceId)` (`terminal.ts:1535`, `session-tabs.ts:60`, `worktree.ts:100`) **[code]** — the funnel resolver is `deviceId → principalId → lane` (§6), not `deviceId → lane`; `shared` for an anonymous socket **and for a socket whose grant is bound to no principal**, which is rev 10's lane-less grant byte-for-byte. `terminal.createAgentSession`/`ensureAgentSession` (`orca-runtime.ts:26322`, `:26515`) need a **distinct field**: `callerContext` collapses identity into one string, `pairedDeviceId ?? clientId` (`agent-session.ts:247`, `:253`) **[code]**, so `RuntimeAgentSessionRpcCaller` (`shared/agent-session-host-authority.ts:142`) **[code]** gains its own `pairedDeviceId`. `orchestration.workerStart` on both topology paths (`orchestration-worker-topology.ts:66`, `:137`), federated worker start (`orchestration-federation.ts:171`), the coordinator loop (`coordinator.ts:382`), `terminal.split` and relay-lease pane recovery (`orca-runtime.ts:17328`) take `inherit` through the ownership predicate below — recovery failing closed when the rehydrated record carries no lane (§h). `launchAgentTerminal` (`:27257`, local IPC), `ipc/worktree-remote.ts:315`/`:367` and the automation path (`index.ts:2613`) pass `shared` **explicitly**.

**The worker path that does not go through `:64`.** For `worktree: 'new-child' | 'new-top-level'` the worker's terminal and setup pane are created inside `runtime.createManagedWorktree({ … startupAgent: args.agent … })` (`orchestration-worker-topology.ts:137-160`) **[code]**, which reaches the nine `orca-runtime.ts` worktree sites. `createManagedWorktree`'s args carry no caller identity at all — only `lineage.callerTerminalHandle: params.from` (`orca-runtime.ts:22822-22867`; `lineage` at `:22866`; the call's `callerTerminalHandle` at `orchestration-worker-topology.ts:158`) **[code]**. Under the §2 table's row 3 that would resolve to **shared**, on the *primary* orchestration path, while the narrative says the worker runs on the coordinator's authority — a worker silently on the host/other developer's credential, mixed-lane with its own worktree, and no throw, because rev 5's rule only fired for an identified caller. **Decision:** (i) `createManagedWorktree` gains a required `credentialLane` in its args, resolved at `orchestration-worker-topology.ts:137` from `lineage.callerTerminalHandle` (already `params.from`) through the same `resolveInheritedLane` predicate as `:66`; (ii) the funnel's throw covers host-internal callers, per the rule stated above. `worktree.create` and the renderer path pass their own value explicitly, the automation path (`index.ts:2613`) passes `shared`. **The federated create is the one site whose lane rev 12 could not state, and saying "the link's grant" was not a shorthand for a principal — it named a key the required field has no case for.** `credentialLane` is `{ kind: 'principal', principalId } | { kind: 'shared' } | { kind: 'inherit', fromPtyId }` (above), and §2a's pane-binding decision says normatively that the value persisted on a pane's binding row **is a principal id, never a grant id**, for the two reasons stated there. There is no grant case to bind to. Re-opened, the site has neither of §2a's two derivations available: `orchestration-federation.ts:90` **[code]** carries `lineage: { noParent: true }` (`:106`) **[code]** so `inherit` has no source pane, and the handler's only caller identity is `orchestrationMutation.callerFingerprint`, stamped into the dispatch row as `homePeerFingerprint` (`:63`) **[code]** — a **home-peer fingerprint**, not a `pairedDeviceId`, so `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` has nothing to read. **Decision: the federated create binds `{ kind: 'principal', principalId }` when — and only when — the federated *link* carries a host-side principal binding, written by the same consent action and into the same registry that binds a device (`principal-registry.ts`, §6); otherwise it *fails closed* (`terminal.lane_link_unbound`).** It never binds a grant id, because the union has no such case and the pane row must not hold one; and it never falls through to `{ kind: 'shared' }`, because an implicit shared lane on a federated worker is the silent downgrade onto the other developer's credential that this whole subsection exists to prevent. **Consequence, stated rather than assumed away: no link→principal binding exists in this tree today**, so until the registry write below lands a federated worktree create fails closed for **every** caller — the correct failure, and also a real functional cut, since it is the primary federated-dispatch path. After it lands, the same refusal is what an **unticked** link still gets. **Decision, rev 15 — §7 question 8 answered (b) by the orchestrator, 2026-08-22: that binding ships, as one more consent write rather than as a new mechanism.** `principal-registry.ts` gains a **fifth** operation beside bind / unbind / re-bind / designate-the-pusher (rule (iii) above): **bind a federated link to a principal**, keyed by the link's **home-peer fingerprint** — the only stable link identity this tree has, stamped onto the dispatch row as `home_peer_fingerprint` (`runtime/orchestration/types.ts:205`, table column `runtime/orchestration/db.ts:504`) and already the value the federation paths authorize against (`orchestration-federation-relay.ts:227` and `orchestration-federation-control.ts:175`, both `attachment.home_peer_fingerprint !== callerFingerprint`) **[code]** — written by the **local human** at the same consent surface, audited with the same row as a device bind, and read at `orchestration-federation.ts:63`, where that fingerprint is already in scope. **The cost is one extra human tick per link and nothing else,** which is the whole of what option (b) buys over option (a): an unticked link still fails closed `terminal.lane_link_unbound`, so the conservative behaviour stays the default and the human opts in per link rather than per create. It remains a *host-side consent write*, which is what keeps it inside this section's provisioning rule — no client and no home peer can cause it. **But the key needs a rule, and rev 15 shipped the binding without one — round 4's blocker, and it is a fail-*open* one.** Rev 15 called the home-peer fingerprint “the only stable link identity this tree has” and cited only where the value is *stored* and *compared*. Re-opening the function that **produces** it changes the decision. `orchestration-federation.ts:63` stamps `homePeerFingerprint: orchestrationMutation.callerFingerprint`; that identity is built at `rpc/orchestration-mutation-executor.ts:32` from `authenticatedCallerFingerprint(request)`, whose entire body is `createHash('sha256').update(request.authToken || request.deviceToken || 'authenticated_transport').digest('hex')` (`:128-134`) **[code]**; `dispatcher.ts:100` passes it through, and no production caller supplies `callerFingerprintOverride` — the parameter at `:26` is its only other occurrence in `src` **[code]**. So the fingerprint is a hash of *whatever credential the request carried*, and that is two different things. **(1) Every local-socket caller collapses onto one value.** `RuntimeRpcServer` mints a single `authToken = randomBytes(24).toString('hex')` per runtime (`runtime-rpc.ts:507`), publishes it in the transport metadata (`:1807`), and `parseAndAuth` refuses any request whose `authToken !== this.authToken` (`:1664-1669`) **[code]** — so the `orca` CLI, the Electron renderer and, per §2m(6), **either developer** on this one-Windows-account box all present the same token and hash to the same fingerprint. Bound as written, a `principalId` attached to that value would make `orchestration.federationAttachStart` — which requires only a durable `orchestrationRequestId` (`orchestration-federation.ts:27-33`) **[code]** — create a worktree, its startup terminal and its setup pane **in that principal's lane for the other developer**. That is the exact fail-open twin of the silent downgrade `terminal.lane_link_unbound` exists to prevent, arriving through the one authority write rev 15 added, and R1 failing through it. **(2) For a paired peer the key is a *grant*, not a link.** Mobile and desktop clients put `deviceToken` on every request (`mobile/src/transport/rpc-client.ts:1057`, `:1092`; the relay twin at `mobile/src/transport/mobile-relay-rpc-session.ts:202`) **[code]**, so the fingerprint is `sha256(deviceToken)` — per grant, and **mutable by construction**, since a re-pair mints a fresh `token: randomBytes(24).toString('hex')` on the row (`device-registry.ts:97`) **[code]**. Rev 15's closing clause — “it never introduces a grant-keyed lane, because what it writes is a `principalId`” — confused the value with the key. What it *writes* is a principalId; what it is **keyed by** is a hash of one grant's device token, which has the two properties §2a rejected when it refused to persist a grant id on a pane and refused to bind a coalesced `getOrCreatePendingDevice` row (`device-registry.ts:118-136`, `:138-141`) **[code]**: non-unique across the local class, and mutable. **Decision, rev 16 — the binding refuses any fingerprint that is not derived from a per-link credential, and the rule is three clauses.** **(a) Two values are refused by name.** The host knows its own `authToken` (`runtime-rpc.ts:507`), so at tick time it computes `sha256(authToken)` and `sha256('authenticated_transport')` and refuses to bind either — the first is the shared local-socket identity of clause (1), the second is the fallback literal at `orchestration-mutation-executor.ts:132` **[code]** that any authenticated transport carrying neither token lands on. Neither names a link. **(b) A bindable fingerprint must resolve to exactly one paired grant.** The consent surface offers the tick only for a fingerprint that equals `sha256(device.token)` for exactly one row in the device registry — the host holds every `token` (`device-registry.ts:97`) **[code]**, so this is a local computation over the rows it already has, not a new protocol — and the binding row records `{ homePeerFingerprint, principalId, boundDeviceId, boundAt }` so the audit row names the grant the human actually ticked rather than an opaque hash — **the `principalId` of which rev 17 drops, for the reason stated two sentences on**. Zero matches, or more than one, is not offered and not bindable. **And clause (b) collapses the fifth consent write into a lookup, which rev 16 did not notice and round 5 did — so the disposition is stated rather than leaving two bindings whose disagreement has no defined winner.** A federated home peer *does* reach this host as an ordinary paired grant: `callOrchestrationWorkerServer` runs over the environment transport (`orca-runtime.ts:4946-4994`) **[code]** and paired clients put `deviceToken` on every request (`shared/remote-runtime-client.ts:140`; mobile twins `mobile/src/transport/rpc-client.ts:1057`/`:1092`, `mobile/src/transport/mobile-relay-rpc-session.ts:202`) **[code]**, which is exactly why clause (b) is satisfiable at all.
