But the `boundDeviceId` clause (b) resolves **already carries a `principalId`**, written by the *first* consent write — so a second `principalId` on the link row is a duplicate that can disagree with it, with no rule saying which wins. **Decision, rev 17: the link row does not carry a principal of its own.** It persists `{ homePeerFingerprint, boundDeviceId, boundAt }`, and the federated create reads `principalOf(boundDeviceId)` — one authority, one revocation point, and no second place for a mis-tick to hide. **What the fifth write still is, because option (b)'s cost does not change:** it is the tick that authorizes *this link* to create in that grant's lane at all — clause (a)'s two refusals by name, clause (b)'s exactly-one-row check, and the audit row are its whole content, and a federated create from an unticked link fails closed `terminal.lane_link_unbound` even when its grant is bound to a principal. One extra human tick per link stands; what does not stand is rev 15–16's implication that the tick names a principal. **One consequence, named rather than discovered later:** unbinding `boundDeviceId` from its principal leaves the link resolving to nothing and its next create refused `terminal.lane_link_unbound` — the same join §2f's wipe walks, and the correct direction. **(c) A re-pair unbinds, silently and correctly.** Because clause (b) keys on the token, a re-paired link's fingerprint no longer matches its stored row, and a federated create from it gets `terminal.lane_link_unbound` — the conservative refusal — until the human re-ticks. That is stated as a **cost**, not as an elegance: re-pairing a federated link costs one tick, and the failure between the two is a refusal rather than a create in the wrong lane. **What this leaves true and what it gives up:** it gives up federated creates from an anonymous local caller (clause (a)), which is correct, because that caller is not a link and §2a's `resolveInheritedLane` case (ii) already covers the in-pane ergonomic path; it keeps the whole of option (b)'s benefit for a real paired home peer; and the lane is still keyed by a `principalId` — what changed is that the *route* to that principalId is now required to be per-link, which is what rev 15 asserted and did not enforce.

Anonymous local launches (Unix-socket CLI, Electron renderer bridge) keep today's shared `~/.claude` lane, as do Orca's own host-side `claude` probes, which take the other env path entirely (`applyClaudeEnvPatch` is called only from `text-generation/commit-message-agent-environment.ts:131` and `rate-limits/claude-pty.ts:247`) **[code]**. Threading below the funnel: `lanePrincipalId` on `RuntimePtyWorktreeRecord` (beside `connectionId`, `:1399-1427` esp. `:1403`) **[code]** and on the pane's persisted binding row (§h), carrying the **principal id** resolved at bind time and never a device id → resolved at the spawn edge by pane identity — on path A it arrives as a host-computed field of the in-process `ptyController.spawn` args (never IPC JSON), on path B it is *looked up*, because that caller is the renderer — → `prepareClaudeAuth`, whose type grows from `(target)` to `(target, lanePrincipalId?)` (`ipc/pty.ts:1264-1268`, call sites `:4637-4638`, `:6205-6208`) **[code]** and whose *call condition* becomes `isClaudeLaunch || lanePinned`. `getPreparation` gains a branch returning `{ configDir: lanePath, envPatch: { CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR: lanePath }, stripAuthEnv: true, managedRefreshDeferredByLivePty, provenance: 'lane:<opaqueLabel>' }` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:660-743`) **[code]**, structurally like the WSL branch at `:683-697`. `managedRefreshDeferredByLivePty` is **not** dropped: the host branch returns it (`:733-737`) **[code]** and it drives the "Waiting for Claude session" UI state; the lane branch computes it from §e's account-scoped query.

**`provenance` must not carry the grant id either.** §k argues the posted `configDir` has to become an opaque label before reaching any peer-readable row, because after the re-key it embeds another **principal's** host-minted `principalId` (rev 13 still said "another grant's `pairedDeviceId`" here and in §2k; the *decision* is unaffected, but the premise was stale and a `principalId` is a different identifier with different properties — host-minted per person, never a device-row key) — and `provenance` is a second carrier of the same identifier: it is copied into `lastClaudeAuthSnapshot.provenance` (`rate-limits/claude-usage-attribution.ts:68-71`) and thence into `usageMetadata.authProvenance` (`:1523`) **[code]**, a *published* field (`shared/rate-limit-types.ts:41`) **[code]** already rendered in settings/stats surfaces. **Decision:** the lane's provenance is `lane:<opaqueLabel>`, where the label is a random token minted with the lane and stored beside it — never the `pairedDeviceId`. **What that buys, stated exactly:** it bounds *incidental* disclosure in published rows (usage metadata, status surfaces, logs). It is **not** a confidentiality property — R4 gives the other developer typing access to a lane pane, so `env | grep CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or `/proc/<pid>/environ` — on the Windows host, the process environment block (§2m(6)) — discloses the **principal id** directly, and §h renders the owner label on the row anyway. No later slice may rely on the id staying host-private. And the predicate it must satisfy is a negative one: `isManagedClaudeAuth` keys on `provenance.startsWith('managed:')` (`rate-limits/claude-fetcher.ts:495`) **[code]** and gates CLI usage supplementation; a lane provenance must **not** match it, because §k scopes the usage *pull* to the shared lane and lane usage arrives by the statusline path instead.

**`terminal.split` inherits.** The split handler destructures only `{ runtime }` (`terminal.ts:1569-1579`, `defineMethod` at `:1569`, `name` at `:1570`, handler destructure at **`:1614`**) **[code]**, and `splitPtyBackedTerminal` (`orca-runtime.ts:28554-28568`) reaches `ptyController.spawn` with no lane **[code]**. The lane is a property of the pane, not of the request: the split reads `lanePrincipalId` off the parent record. The renderer-notified branch (`notifier.splitTerminal`, declared `:2037-2045`, called `:28544-28548`) **[code]** carries no env and re-enters through the anonymous renderer path — it gains a `lanePrincipalId` field in those opts, and if a build cannot carry it the split **fails closed**.

**`inherit` needs an owner check.** "The worker runs on the coordinator's authority" says nothing about whether the caller has any relationship to the pane it names. `orchestration.workerStart` destructures no identity (`orchestration-workers.ts:29`) **[code]** and resolves the coordinator pane from the client-supplied selector `params.from` (`:31`; schema `orchestration-worker-start-schema.ts:27`) **[code]**; the only gate is that the pane is the Run's current consumer (`:32-38`, `consumer_fenced` at `:35`) **[code]**. So grant B names any pane bound to a Run — including grant A's — and mints a worker terminal, worktree and setup pane on **A's credential**. `terminal.split` is the same shape (`terminal.ts:1276`) **[code]**, **and its handler is not the only door into a split**: `agentTeams.tmuxCompat` (`terminal.ts:1663-1670`, handler destructuring only `{ runtime }` at `:1682`) reaches `this.splitTerminal` through `claudeAgentTeams.handleTmuxCompat` (`orca-runtime.ts:28829-28845`, `splitTerminal` passed at `:28839`) **[code]** with no `pairedDeviceId` at all — so the predicate below lives inside `splitPtyBackedTerminal`, over the parent pane's record, not in the `terminal.split` handler. This is **not** the §4 shell-equivalence concession — that concession is about bytes a co-tenant can read anyway; this is Orca's own supported RPC *granting B use of A's Claude account*.

**Decision — one ownership predicate, applied to every `inherit` edge.** `resolveInheritedLane(sourcePtyId, ctx)` returns the source pane's lane only when one of: **(i)** the source pane's `lanePrincipalId === principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)`, **both defined** — a *principal* comparison, not a device-id one, which is the whole of Q1's gain here and is stated at this site because this paragraph, not §2a's re-key list, is what an implementer reads when writing `terminal-inherited-lane-authority.ts` (§6). Rev 10 compared `laneGrantId === ctx.pairedDeviceId`; left standing, one person's phone could not split, worker-start from, or inherit their own desktop's lane pane — refused `terminal.lane_not_owned` at their own pane, the exact refusal Q1 exists to remove; **(ii)** the caller is anonymous (Unix socket or renderer) — today's behavior, and the path an agent *inside* A's pane takes when it runs `orca worker-start --from <its own pane>`, so the ergonomic case is untouched; **(iii)** the source pane is in the shared lane **and** the caller's principal holds no lane (a grant bound to no principal is included, since it holds none by definition). Anything else is refused with `terminal.lane_not_owned` — no fallback to `shared`, no silent downgrade. Attachment is deliberately **not** the predicate here: R4 gives B an attachment on A's pane, and §g's attachment check is right for `openInMyLane` only because that operation spends the *caller's* credential. Applies to `orchestration.workerStart` (whose handler gains `pairedDeviceId`) on **both** its topology paths (`:64` and `:133`), federated worker start, `coordinator.ts:382` (always case (ii)), `terminal.split` (gains `pairedDeviceId`) and pane recovery at `orca-runtime.ts:17328` — **not** host-internal: its client route is `terminal.recoverPane` (`terminal.ts:1245-1255`, handler destructuring only `{ runtime }`, client-supplied `paneKey`/`worktreeId`), which re-enters `createTerminal` with the client's own `tabId`/`leafId`. It does not escalate today only because `getRecentExpiredSshLease` (`:6893`, gate at `:17322`) bounds it to relay-lease panes, which are `credentialLane: 'remote'` and never lane-bound; §a(ii)'s adopt rule covers it if that bound ever widens, and it fails closed when the rehydrated record carries no lane (§h). Residual, named: an anonymous local caller can still inherit any pane's lane — same Unix user, inside §4's conceded envelope, and the reason (ii) is safe to keep.

**Branch order — the lane is *not* unconditionally first.** `getPreparation`'s WSL branches (`:612-626`, `:627-654`) run for a WSL target **[code]**. A lane path is a host-side `<userData>\claude-lanes\<id>` path; handing it to a Linux-side `claude` either hard-fails or silently creates a fresh empty config dir that prompts for login. The WSL branch is not a counter-precedent: it deliberately emits `wslLinuxAuthPath`, *a path visible from the far side* (`:622`) **[code]**. **Decision:** the lane branch is evaluated first only when `normalizeClaudeAccountSelectionTarget` (`runtime-selection.ts:17-26`) **[code]** yields runtime `host`; a WSL target falls through and the terminal is labelled `credentialLane: 'wsl'`. **That classifier is not the one that selects the WSL *shell* branch**, and the gap matters: the WSL export keys on the variable's mere **presence** — `if (finalEnv.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR) { addWslEnvKeys(finalEnv, ['CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR']) }` (`providers/local-pty-provider.ts:762-764`, mirrored `daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:762-764`) **[code]** — while that branch is chosen by `resolveWslSessionContext`/`getCodexSelectionTargetForPty`. On a Windows host a pane the two classify differently would export a Windows lane path into the distro through WSLENV, producing exactly the empty-config-dir failure above. **Invariant, asserted and controlled (§5):** a lane path is never present in a spawn env that takes the WSL branch. A WSL-visible lane is **S9e** (§2n), which narrows this invariant rather than deleting it — a *Windows* lane path is still never present, a *Linux* one under that distro's lane root is. **And the invariant needs an enforcement *site*. It is not `computeLaneLaunch`, and this is a correction to rev 12 rather than a preference: the input type says that function cannot see what the rule turns on.** `PtySpawnOptions` (`src/main/providers/pty-provider-contract.ts:41-108`, read in full) carries `shellOverride`, `terminalWindowsWslDistro` and `terminalWindowsPowerShellImplementation` and **no resolved shell and no `isWsl`** **[code]**. The value is computed *inside* the provider: `shellPath` is resolved at `local-pty-provider.ts:578-668` from cwd-derived `wslInfo`, the worktree WSL context, `this.opts.getWindowsShell?.()`, `COMSPEC`, Git-Bash resolution and an async pwsh probe, and the export gate reads exactly that resolved value (`:733`, export at `:764`) **[code]**. A refusal written at the anchor is therefore unimplementable there, and implementing it there would mean synthesizing a **fourth** shell classifier beside the two this section already warns are 22 lines apart (§2m(5)). **Decision: the assertion lives in the lane-aware `buildSpawnEnv` arm this design already created for exactly this class of problem.** The §2 preamble threads the pane's lane into that ctx (`local-pty-provider.ts:712-722`) — and that ctx is precisely where `shellPath` and `isWsl` already sit (`:718-719`) **[code]** — so the rule is evaluated over the *same* value the export gate reads, two steps later in the same function. It reuses the export gate's own predicate, `pathWin32.basename(ctx.shellPath).toLowerCase() === 'wsl.exe'` (`:733`), and deliberately **not** the broader `isWslShell` of `:707`, so it refuses exactly the panes the export would have touched and no others: **no new classifier is introduced.** The daemon twin resolves its own `shellPath` at the same gate (`daemon/pty-subprocess.ts:723`, export at `:762-764`) over an env it rebuilds in its own process (`:610`) **[code]**, so the assertion is stated over that value too, and a lane spawn on the daemon path that cannot carry the lane into it **fails closed**. `computeLaneLaunch` keeps its job unchanged — it computes the lane env at the anchor; it simply never sees the shell, and the design no longer pretends it does. The refusal is `terminal.lane_wsl_shell_unsupported` and carries a complete human sentence, because it surfaces from inside the provider rather than from a pre-spawn RPC check (§3's Rule-3 row). **What that mis-siting cost, pre-S9e, stated because it is the harm and not the tidiness:** a Windows lane path reaches `addWslEnvKeys` and crosses WSLENV into a distro that cannot use it, while the pane still renders `credentialLane: 'grant'` (§h) and its statusline usage joins to the lane's account (§k) — a pane labelled isolated, billed to its owner, and sitting at a login prompt in an empty config dir.

**SSH panes must never receive a lane path — and after the decoupling above, that guard becomes real.** Correction to rev 5: *no leak exists today*. `isClaudeLaunch` already requires `!args.connectionId` (`pty.ts:4517-4518`, `:6153-6154`) **[code]**, so for an SSH pane `claudeAuth` is null and the merge yields `env = sshScopedEnv` with nothing added (`:4632-4634`, `:6221-6223`) **[code]**. The exclusion is incidental behavior, not a fix. But decoupling the lane from `isClaudeLaunch` removes that incidental guard, so `args.connectionId` must become an **explicit** condition of the lane resolver, and the two-clause invariant of guard 3(a) becomes an asserted property with **both** a positive control and a mutation proof — deleting the `connectionId` condition must turn a named test red, since this is exactly the moment its accidental protection is withdrawn. Such a pane is labelled `credentialLane: 'remote'`.

**`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is a client-supplied key today, and that is a hole S9 must close first.** The merge is `{ ...sshScopedEnv, ...claudeAuth.envPatch }` (`pty.ts:4632-4634`) **[code]**; for a caller with no lane the host branch returns `envPatch: paths.envPatch` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:731`) **[code]**, which `runtime-paths.ts:23` **[code]** makes `{}` unless the Orca process itself inherited the variable. An empty patch lets the client's own `env.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` survive the spread verbatim. The only launch-time guard, `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` (`environment.ts:59-72`) **[code]**, does not list `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, and it runs only when `claudeAuth.stripAuthEnv` is true — itself false unless a managed account is selected (`:732`) **[code]**. **This is exploitable today, before any lane exists:** the injection target that exists right now is `<userData>/claude-accounts/<id>/auth`, a real managed-account credential store (`managed-auth-path.ts:12-14`) **[code]**, reachable by any identified grant. `terminal.split` carries an `env` record too (`terminal.ts:1054`, passthrough `:1577`) **[code]**, and `pty.ts:7015`'s own comment already flags `args.env` as "untrusted IPC JSON" **[code]**.

**And `terminal.*` is not the only door.** `session.tabs.createTerminal` accepts `env` (`session-tabs-schemas.ts:147`), `envToDelete` (`:148`) and `launchConfig` (`:150` → `shared/workspace-session-sleeping-agents.ts:66-73`: `agentCommand`, `agentArgs`, `agentEnv`, `ompResumeFilePath`) **[code]** and forwards every one (`session-tabs.ts:67-68`, `:72`) into `createMobileSessionTerminal` → `createTerminal` (`orca-runtime.ts:27720-27722`) **[code]**; `worktree.create` accepts `startupCommand`/`startupEnv`/`startupLaunchConfig` (`worktree-schemas.ts:172-174`) **[code]**. Both are lane-derived rows (§2 rows 1 and 3), and their payloads are §2 rows 5–9. Three escapes follow: **(a)** `envToDelete: ['CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR']` merges into the spawn's deletion list and is applied *after* the patch merge, so the launch silently falls back to the shared `~/.claude` — on this box, the other developer's credential — while the pane still carries `lanePrincipalId`, presence (§h) and usage (§k) still name the lane owner: R1/R3 fail silently, and an *override* check does not see a *deletion*. **(b)** An `agentArgs` flag that redirects settings resolution reaches the CLI unfiltered, so §j's allowlist inspects files the CLI was told to ignore — and `apiKeyHelper`/`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` from such a file **outrank** stored logins **[doc]**. **(c)** `ompResumeFilePath` and `agentEnv` escape §g's containment check for the same reason.

**Fix, in S9a, before any lane is provisionable — as the closure principle's single computation point.** `computeLaneLaunch` runs for every spawn whose **pane record** carries a lane, at the convergence point named in the §2 preamble — `spawnForStablePane` (`pty.ts:907-919`) plus the agent-session-ensure spawn (`:5043`), i.e. over the `spawnOptions` both assembly points hand to `provider.spawn`. That is downstream of all twenty `createTerminal` call sites, of `splitPtyBackedTerminal`'s own spawn (guard `:28569`, spawn `:28602`) **[code]**, of the renderer-backed create, of the mobile-session create, of the wake, of cold restore and of the `agentTeamsPlan` re-derivation alike — one anchor instead of rev 7's two, under the two-part post-anchor invariant stated in the §2 preamble rather than rev 7's “nothing may re-derive” blanket. Three guards over four vectors:

1. **Env and deletions.** Strip `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, every `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` key (`environment.ts:9-14`) and `ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` — absent from that tuple but auth-bearing by the tree's own model, deleted under `stripAuthEnv` when `isAuthLikeCustomHeaders()` says so (`:23-24`) and tested as the second arm of `hasClaudeAuthEnvConflict` (`:44`) **[code]** — from `options.env`, from `options.envToDelete`, and from `launchConfig.agentEnv` — silently for `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, loudly (typed refusal) for the auth vars. Deletion counts as much as override, and it must be stripped from **`spawnOptions.envToDelete` itself**, not merely from the host env: the provider replays the list after its own env build (`local-pty-provider.ts:725-726`, and once before it at `:689-691`; daemon twins `pty-subprocess.ts:622`/`:781`) **[code]**, post-anchor. Path B hands the merged list over as `spawnOptions.envToDelete = combinedEnvToDelete` (`pty.ts:6522`) **[code]**, so the sanitation must sit on that object on both paths, not only on the host env applied at `:4839`/`:6498`. Sources: `terminal.ts:1009`/`:1010`/`:1015` and split `:1054`/`:1577`; `session-tabs-schemas.ts:147`/`:148`/`:150`; `worktree-schemas.ts:173`/`:174` **[code]**.
2. **Launch strings.** `launchConfig.agentCommand`/`agentArgs` (`workspace-session-sleeping-agents.ts:66-67`) **[code]** are parsed and matched against an allowlist for a lane launch; a settings-, config-dir- or auth-redirecting flag is refused (`terminal.agent_args_refused`). `ompResumeFilePath` (`:71-77`) and `resumeProviderSession.transcriptPath` (`terminal.ts:1027`) **[code]** are containment-checked against the caller's own lane root and the workspace (§g).
3. **A post-merge assertion in `pty.ts`, on *both* spawn paths, stated as a condition rather than a blanket.** The only place all three sources converge is the spawn env itself. Path A builds `env` at `:4632-4634`, merges deletions at `:4770-4783` and applies them at `:4839`; path B builds at `:6221-6223`, merges at `:6470-6482` and applies at `:6498` **[code]**. The invariant is *"no lane path and no client-supplied `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` enters an env carrying `args.connectionId`"* — two clauses with different scopes, so the assertion branches on `args.connectionId`: **(a) `connectionId` set (SSH pane):** assert `claudeAuth === null` and that **no value in the merged env canonicalizes under `<userData>/claude-lanes/`**; the client's own `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is **left alone**, because it names a config dir on the *remote* host, has nothing to do with host lanes, and the env is `sshScopedEnv` verbatim (`:4632-4634`, `:6221-6223`; `stripRemotePaneEnvWhenHooksDisabled` removes only `ORCA_*`, `pty.ts:972-994`) **[code]**. Deleting it would be a silent, undocumented break of a legitimate remote setting, which `AGENTS.md:40-42` requires us to consider. **(b) `connectionId` absent:** when the auth patch carries a lane path, assert `env.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` equals it **and** `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` is absent from the merged deletion list; when it does not, assert no client-supplied `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` survives. Clause (b) is **not** behind `stripAuthEnv` and **not** conditioned on a lane existing, because the injection is exploitable in the zero-lane state, where the target is a real managed-account store (`managed-auth-path.ts:12-14`) **[code]**.

Each guard carries its own mutation proof, and §5 runs the four vectors through **all three** spawning RPCs.

**A lane loses Orca's managed hooks unless we mirror them.** `getConfigPath` is `join(homedir(), settings.configDirName, 'settings.json')`, with no `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` awareness (`claude/hook-settings.ts:78-80`) **[code]**, so a lane launch reads a `settings.json` that has never been written and loses status/notification hooks and the statusline. S9a writes the managed hook block into each lane's `settings.json` at lane creation and on hook refresh, alongside the mirrored allowlist below — and never `env` or `apiKeyHelper`.

**What else a lane loses — and the recorded decision this reverses.** `global-settings-types.ts:253` records "persist only per-account auth (not a `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` swap) so switching accounts doesn't fork Claude's shared chat/session context" **[code]**. That is right for the problem it was written for — *one human*, several of their own accounts, one machine, where forking history is pure loss. S9's problem is the inverse: *two humans*, one machine, where a shared history is the thing to remove. The reversal is deliberate, scoped to grants, and the shared lane keeps today's behavior byte-for-byte. **The loss list, complete and with a decision per item**, because §1's own [doc] bullet concedes `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` moves `settings.json` and `.claude.json` as a unit: **(i) sessions/transcripts** — `--resume`/`/resume` sees only that lane's sessions; **not mirrored** either way (they are the other developer's content, and copying them into a credential directory defeats the wipe promise); **(ii) user-level memory** (`~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, `memories/`) — **mirrored** host→lane at creation and on hook refresh (workspace guidance, not secrets; silent loss would be the worst regression here); **(iii)** `resumeProviderSession.transcriptPath` (`terminal.ts:1027`) **[code]** points outside the lane for any pre-lane session — refused by §g containment, the message naming the shared-lane pane as the place to do it; **(iv) permission allowlists** — `permissions.allow`/`deny`/`ask`/`additionalDirectories` **mirrored** (without them every agent re-prompts for every tool, on both lanes, permanently), but `permissions.defaultMode` **not** (a host-wide `bypassPermissions` must not silently widen a lane); **(v) `model`/`outputStyle`** — mirrored (preferences, no credential influence); **(vi) the user's own `hooks` and `statusLine`, and `env`/`apiKeyHelper`/`awsAuthRefresh`/`awsCredentialExport`** — **never** mirrored: the first two are owned by the lane's managed block (§k depends on it), the last four are exactly the credential-redirecting keys §j exists to keep out; **(vii) `.claude.json` `mcpServers` and per-project `mcpServers`/`enabledMcpjsonServers`** — **mirrored**, minus any entry whose `env` names `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or a `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` key; the security reasoning is that an MCP entry names a *subprocess*, cannot redirect Claude's own credential resolution, and is reachable by a co-tenant per §4 anyway, whereas zero MCP servers in every lane is a larger regression than either Q9 ask (**Q9 = confirmed**: the mirror list and the forking both ship as written); **(viii) per-project trust map** — **not** copied (a trust decision belongs to the grant that runs the agent), so each lane re-prompts once per workspace; **(ix) onboarding-completion and theme keys** — copied by allowlist (§ fresh-lane bootstrap); **(x) project prompt `history`** — not copied (the other developer's prompts); **(xi) plugins/marketplace state** — **not** mirrored in S9, named as an accepted loss because plugin state is coupled to caches inside the config dir, Q9. **(xii) user-level `agents/`, `commands/`, `skills/` and custom `output-styles/`** — all inside the config dir and therefore relocated as a unit **[doc]**, so an unmirrored lane starts with zero custom slash commands, zero user subagents, zero user skills and zero custom output styles: the same silent-regression class as (iv), and the omission round 7 caught. **Mirrored** host→lane, one-way, on memory's schedule and for memory's reason — authored workspace tooling, not secrets. Two caveats stated: such a file can carry arbitrary shell, which §4’s shell-equivalence already concedes for this Unix user’s own files; and a lane’s *own* additions are not mirrored back (Q9). So the lane `settings.json` rule is not "the managed block and nothing else" but **"the managed block plus the mirrored allowlist above, all computed host-side, and nothing client-supplied"** — clause (ii) of the closure principle, item by item. The mirror is one-way host→lane, recomputed at creation and on hook refresh; the only lane-scoped *override* path is that grant's own push (§b), which is clause (i). §5 asserts each item individually — "reaches a usable prompt" would pass while most of them were broken.

**Fresh-lane bootstrap, with the trust question answered.** A lane's `.claude.json` carries first-run state as well as identity, so a lane's first terminal may sit at an onboarding prompt instead of running **[inferred]**. S9a seeds it with `oauthAccount` plus an **explicit allowlist** copied from the host's own `.claude.json` (`runtime-paths.ts:27-33` resolves which file that is) **[code]**: onboarding-completion and theme/appearance keys, plus the MCP entries item (vii) mirrors, and nothing else. The per-project trust map is **not** copied — that would inherit the host's trust acceptances for workspaces the grant never approved. Consequence: each lane's first launch in a workspace re-prompts for trust, once per lane. Correct, because a trust decision belongs to the grant that runs the agent.

### (b) What crosses the wire — a URL and a code, and nothing else

**The desktop sends nothing, and rev 32 is where that sentence replaces this section rather than qualifying it.** Every revision through 31 opened here with "the owner's desktop pushes **the selected account only**: the byte contents of that account's `.credentials.json` and `oauth-account.json`". That is deleted, along with the envelope, its three members, its bounds, its freshness fields and its refusals (§3). **A lane's credential is minted inside the lane**, by `claude auth login --claudeai` run with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR = <lane>/claude-accounts/<laneAccountId>/auth`, and the only two values that ever cross the Orca wire are:

1. **The authorization URL**, host → the requesting person's device. It is the URL the CLI **prints to stdout**, and that qualifier is load-bearing rather than incidental — see the next paragraph.
2. **The code**, that person's device → the host, written to the stdin of the *same* `claude` process that printed the URL.

**Neither is a credential, and the asymmetry between them is the whole of this section's security content.** The request carries `code_challenge` and `code_challenge_method=S256` **[code, observed on 2.1.247]**, so the `code_verifier` is generated inside the lane's `claude` process and never enters the relay at all. An attacker who reads the code in transit — another paired grant, the co-tenant reading a pane's stream, the shared Windows user — **cannot redeem it**: the token endpoint refuses it without the matching verifier, and an authorization code is single-use besides. So the relay's confidentiality requirement on the **code** is weak. The requirement on the **URL** is strong, and it runs the other way: whoever chooses *which* URL a person authenticates against chooses *whose lane* receives the resulting grant. §2d states the three bindings that close it and §4 states the attack they close.

**Relay the printed URL, never the browser-opener URL — a rule, with a test, because the two differ in one query parameter.** A single `claude auth login --claudeai` invocation emits **two** `redirect_uri` values for the same `code_challenge` and `state`: the URL handed to the browser opener uses `http://localhost:<ephemeral>/callback`, while the URL printed to stdout uses `https://platform.claude.com/oauth/code/callback` **[code, instrumented probe on 2.1.247 — an `xdg-open` shim captured the first while the same run's stdout printed the second]**. Relaying the localhost one would deliver the code as a plaintext GET query string to an unauthenticated loopback port **on the person's own desktop**, where any process holding that port receives it — not a takeover, because the verifier is on the host, but a disclosure and a guaranteed login failure. S9-L1 asserts the relayed URL's `redirect_uri` host is `platform.claude.com` and nothing else (§5).

**Parsing the URL is a scraped contract, and the design says so rather than treating it as an API.** Three facts, all observed on 2.1.247 and all version-dependent: the printed URL is ~900 characters and is wrapped in OSC-8 hyperlink escapes, so it appears **twice** in the byte stream — strip OSC-8 and take the first match, never "last URL wins"; `NO_COLOR=1 TERM=dumb` does not disable that wrapping; and the readiness signal is the literal prompt `Paste code here if prompted > ` with **no trailing newline**, so a line-buffered reader hangs. One further trap is in Orca's own helper rather than in the CLI: `runClaudeCommand` accumulates output and keeps only the **last** `MAX_COMMAND_OUTPUT_CHARS = 4_000` characters (`service.ts:54`, `:1133-1134`) **[code]**, so a chatty CLI or a re-prompt loop can truncate the URL out of the buffer before Orca reads it — the login session must read from the live stream, not from that buffer. A parse failure is a **named refusal** (`accounts.lane.login_url_unparsed`) that surfaces as a lane state, never a hang.

**Identity is verified before the lane loads — invariant I6, and it is the cheapest control in the whole model.** The login-start RPC takes a caller-named expected account and passes it through as `claude auth login --claudeai --email <expected>` (`--email <email>  Pre-populate email address on the login page`, `claude auth login --help` on 2.1.247 **[code]**). After the CLI exits, the host runs `['auth','status','--json']` in the same dir — exactly the call `service.ts:615-620` and `:232-237` already make **[code]** — and compares the resulting identity against the expectation **before** the lane transitions to `loaded`; a mismatch is refused `accounts.lane.login_identity_mismatch` and the half-written login directory is swept. **Rev 34 states the *unverifiable* case, which rev 32 left to the implementer on the one check that gates `loaded`.** If `auth status --json` yields no identity at all — a parse failure, an unexpected shape, a CLI inside the tested range that still emitted no account block — the login **fails closed on the same path**: refused, swept, `laneState` never reaching `loaded`. “Unverifiable” is not “mismatched”, but it carries the *same* code, whose sentence names both causes (“we could not confirm which account this lane was signed into”), because the human's remedy is identical — run the login again — and a second code would buy a distinction nobody acts on. What is **not** permitted is the fall-back the shipped resolver would otherwise make: `resolveIdentity` prefers `auth status --json` and then `oauth-account.json` (`service.ts:787`) **[code]**, and I6 may **not** take the second when the first is silent (§5's mutation proof). That turns "a lane may hold any Claude account" into "a lane holds the account **the starting human named**" — and the scope of that is exactly what it says. **I6 is a control against mis-authentication, not against substitution, and rev 33 withdraws rev 32's claim that it was both.** `expectedEmail` is a parameter of `accounts.lane.loginStart`, supplied by whoever starts the session, and the host holds no allowlist to constrain it against: R5 keeps the account inventory on the owner's desktop and this section deletes the delegable list, so the host does not know which accounts are a given principal's own. In §4's substitution attack the attacker starts the login in **their own** lane and simply declares the *victim's* email; the victim authenticates as that account, `auth status --json` in the attacker's lane reports that account, and the comparison **passes**. What I6 does close is the honest-caller case, which is not small: a wrong-device-of-the-right-person login (§2l), a person who mistypes or misremembers which account they are signing a lane into, and any URL substitution by something that cannot also choose `expectedEmail`. §4 therefore counts **four** bindings against URL substitution rather than five, and the labelled human confirmation is the last one standing on that path. **Rev 35 states where this check sits relative to the lane's write queue, because the capture step is one of that queue's three members (§2c).** `auth status --json` is a read of the login's own `<laneAccountId>/auth` directory and runs **before** the capture step takes `serializeLaneWrite` — a subprocess is not awaited inside a lane's write turn. Everything that *mutates* the lane then happens inside **one** turn: the post-queue re-check (§2c), the `.credentials.json` rewrite and the `oauthAccount` merge that make this login active, and the index row that records it. The session is **`captured`** at the moment that row is written and not before, and that boundary is exactly what §2f's `cancel` acts on (§2d rule (2), §2f).

**What the host stores, and what it does not.** The lane keeps what the CLI wrote: `.credentials.json` and the `oauthAccount` block in `.claude.json`, plus the `oauth-account.json` the capture path already reads (`readOauthAccountFromConfigDir`, `service.ts:770`; identity resolved by `resolveIdentity` at `:787`, preferring `auth status --json`, then `oauth-account.json`, then the blob) **[code]**. **The account identity is no longer client-asserted, and that is a strict improvement over §2b as it stood through rev 31.** Rev 25's sentence — "everything the host knows about *whose* account this is comes from the pushed `oauth-account.json`; a pusher can put any email in it" — is retired: the host now watches its own child process write that file into a directory the host owns, and cross-checks it against `auth status --json` from the same dir. **Q3's friendly per-account name loses its carrier and gains a better one:** it was the push envelope's third member, and with the envelope gone it becomes an **owner-set label written at the host's own consent surface** (`orca lane accounts label`, and the desktop equivalent), persisted beside the lane account row, ≤64 characters, control characters rejected — one more host-side consent write rather than a wire member, so nothing client-asserted reaches a peer-visible row at all.

**And the delegable-account list is deleted outright, which closes §7's **host-pivot** question 10 and §8 item 5 by removing their subject.** §2l step 1 required the desktop to publish a list of ticked accounts so the phone had something to tap; §3 row 12 gave it a wire method and `runtime/lane-delegation-directory.ts` gave it a host-side store. Under per-lane login the phone taps **the lane's own account list**, which the host derives from the lane's own managed store (§2c) and has therefore always held. `accounts.lane.setDelegableAccounts`, `ClaudeLaneDelegableAccount`, `claudeLaneDelegationRows`, `MAX_LANE_DELEGABLE_ACCOUNTS` and both `delegable_*` refusals go with it. **The privacy widening rev 22 shipped ahead of the owner's decision therefore un-ships**: the host no longer holds the owner's ticked account names or any opted-in email, and §2b's original normative sentence — "the host never receives the owner's account list" — becomes true again, for a different reason than it was first written for.

### (c) A switch is a choice among the lane's own logins — plus one new verb, "log this lane into another account"

**A lane is a self-contained Claude installation, and under rev 32 it may hold several logins.** The lane directory keeps its **active** `.credentials.json` and `.claude.json` exactly as before, and gains `<lane>/claude-accounts/<laneAccountId>/auth/` holding the lane's **own** logins — `managed-auth-path.ts` re-rooted at the lane, not borrowed as-is. That module already enforces everything a lane store needs and enforces it in the right shape: `getClaudeManagedAccountsRoot()` = `<userData>/claude-accounts` (`:12-14`), `resolveOwnedClaudeManagedAuthPath` requires an exact `<accountId>/auth` two-segment relative match under a canonicalized root with the shared containment helper's symlink refusal ordered first (`:16-66`, esp. `:30-42` and `:43-53`), a `.orca-managed-claude-auth` marker whose contents must equal the id (`:54-61`, `:95-108`), and reads/writes exactly the two filenames a login produces through `writeFileAtomically(path, contents, { mode: 0o600 })` (`:68-93`) **[code]**. S9-L1 parameterises that root so `<userData>/claude-accounts` and `<lane>/claude-accounts` share one implementation; the lane root helper already exists (`claude-lanes-root.ts:12-14`) **[code]**. **The one shape change is the id:** a lane account id is a lane-local UUID Orca mints at login-start, never the desktop's managed account id, which the host does not have and must not be told. **Three rules rev 34 adds, because “N logins” left N unbounded, let the directory listing stand in for the store, and left the marker's ordering unstated (§2a).** **(1) The index is the store's authority.** `listLaneAccounts` returns the lane's **index rows** joined to their directories, never a directory walk: a directory with no index row is an orphan (§2a) and is never offered, and an index row whose directory or credential is missing is dropped on read and removed. So a half-written login is never a phantom entry a human can select, and `accounts.lane.selectAccount` against its id is refused `accounts.lane.account_unknown` — the code that already means “this lane holds no such login”, so nothing is minted for it. **Rev 35 names the file and the read failure.** The index is `<lane>/claude-accounts/index.json` at `0600` (§2a rule (2)), read per lane and never out of Orca's persisted state, so a lane whose index cannot be read costs **that** lane its listing and every other lane nothing. `listLaneAccounts` on an unreadable index returns **empty** and does **not** fall back to a directory walk — a walk would offer a human a directory whose identity and label the host cannot vouch for, which is the phantom entry rule (1) exists to refuse. What is lost is the *choice* among logins, not the lane: the active credential lives in `<lane>/.credentials.json` and not in the store, so the lane stays `loaded` and keeps working until the index is restored or a fresh login writes one, and the directories the reconciliation could not judge are quarantined rather than deleted (§2f). **(2) Deletion is directory-scoped and is *not* marker-gated.** `purgeLaneAccountStore` removes every `<laneAccountId>` directory under `<lane>/claude-accounts/`, marker or no marker, valid marker or corrupt one — the canonical-containment and symlink refusals still run first and still refuse a path that escapes the lane root, but the marker resolution is a precondition for **reads and writes only**. Gating deletion on it would make the one directory that most needs deleting — the crashed login's, whose marker may be absent — the one the purge cannot see, which is exactly the failure §2f's normative scope exists to prevent. **Rev 35 adds two members to what that purge removes:** the index file itself, with any `index.json.<pid>.<uuid>.tmp` sibling a crashed write staged (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:12`) **[code]**, because a logout that left the index behind would leave rows naming directories it had just deleted; and every `<laneAccountId>.quarantined-<ts>` directory a startup pass renamed but could not judge (§2f), because a quarantined directory is a complete grant at rest and class (3) means *every* directory under this root, not every well-named one. **(3) The store is capped at eight.** `MAX_LANE_LOGINS = 8`; a `loginStart` that would exceed it is refused `accounts.lane.login_store_full` **before any spawn**, with `accounts.lane.removeAccount` named in the sentence as the remedy. The push model carried `MAX_LANE_DELEGABLE_ACCOUNTS` and rev 32 deleted it with the delegable list (§2b) leaving nothing in its place; every entry in this store is an independently durable grant at rest under R-32a's unbounded window (§4), so “a human has to complete a browser round-trip each time” is a rate, not a bound.

**Two verbs, and separating them is what makes the model legible.** **(i) *Switch* — choose among the logins this lane already holds.** No browser, no network, no person: an atomic `0600` rewrite of the lane's `.credentials.json` plus an `oauthAccount` merge into the lane's `.claude.json`, which is exactly what `LaneCredentialWriter` already does (`lane-credential-writer.ts:39-49`, `:72-88`, `writeFileAtomically` at `:39-47`, `ensureOwnerOnlyMode` at `:208-217`) **[code]**, run under the existing per-lane switch gate `beginClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` / `endClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` / `isClaudeAuthSwitchInProgress(laneId)` (`live-pty-gate.ts:205`, `:212`, `:216`) **[code]**, whose lane keying S9c already shipped (§10 item 33). **And rev 34 states how the gate's callers are serialized, because rev 32 gave it a second one and left the contention undefined.** The gate is not a queue: `beginClaudeAuthSwitch` throws a bare `Error('A Claude account switch is already in progress.')` when the lane is already gated (`live-pty-gate.ts:205-210`) **[code]**. In the shipped tree that throw is unreachable only because the single writer takes the per-lane write queue **first** — `authState.serializeLaneWrite(principalId, …)` wrapping `beginLaneSwitch` (`runtime/lane-wire-authority.ts:160-163`, `:296`; the queue at `lane-auth-state.ts:102-109`) **[code]**. **Decision, corrected in rev 35 to name every member of that queue and the precedence among them: the queue has *three* members, and the wipe outranks the other two.** They are `accounts.lane.selectAccount`, which *any* grant bound to the principal may call including the phone (§3 row 9); the login session's capture step (§2b); and **§2f's wipe**, which is already inside this queue in the shipped tree — `attemptWipe` runs inside `deps.serializeLaneWrite` (`principal-lane-lifecycle.ts:210`, wired at `lane-credential-coordinator.ts:87`) **[code]**. Rev 34 named two members and stopped there, and that is what left round 3's blocker open: **queue order is not a fence.** A member that entered the queue *before* a wipe and takes its turn *after* it writes into a lane the wipe has just certified empty, and waiting politely in line is precisely how it gets there. **So neither non-wipe member may act on a precondition it checked before its turn.** After acquiring the queue and before touching a byte, the capture step re-checks **both** that the lane's wipe is not pending (`lane-wipe-pending.ts:65`, `:70`) **[code]** **and** that its own session is still live, and is refused `accounts.lane.wipe_in_progress` if **either** changed while it waited; `selectAccount` re-checks the wipe mark under the same rule and takes the same refusal. Precedence is what those re-checks implement: the wipe sets its mark **before** it enters the queue — `markLaneWipePending` at `principal-lane-lifecycle.ts:157`, under a comment that already says "Set BEFORE anything is aborted", with the queued `attemptWipe` called from the loop at `:160` and taking `serializeLaneWrite` at `:210` **[code]**, so every member arriving after the mark loses regardless of what the queue order says. **The shipped tree holds this exact defect in the analogous writer, and it is the pattern S9-L1 must not repeat:** the push writer checks `isLaneWipeInFlight` at `lane-wire-authority.ts:112`, **outside** the `serializeLaneWrite` it then enters at `:120-122` **[code]**, so a caller that passes the guard and waits behind a wipe writes after it. With the re-checks in place a phone tapping an account while a capture lands **waits** and then applies, in the order the queue took them; a capture or a switch that waited behind a logout is **refused**, and resurrects nothing. The capture holds its turn for the credential rewrite, the `oauthAccount` merge and the index row — not for the browser round-trip and not for `auth status --json`, both of which run outside it (§2b) — so the wait is one file rewrite long. **The bare throw becomes a named refusal on the defensive arm**, `accounts.lane.switch_in_progress`, carrying a complete sentence under §3's Rule-3 row: with the queue in place no caller reaches it, and §5 asserts both halves — that the two writers serialize, and that a caller who ever does reach it sees the refusal rather than an untyped throw. **(ii) *Log in* — add a login this lane does not hold.** A browser round-trip through §2b's relay, ending in a new `<laneAccountId>/auth` directory and, on success, an automatic switch to it. §2c through rev 31 said "a live switch **is** a push"; it now says a live switch is neither a push nor a login — it is a local file rewrite, and only (ii) costs a human anything.
**Precedence between a queued writer and the wipe is a property of the *session*, not of the wipe-pending mark — rev 36, the chair's closure of rev 35's last blocker.** Rev 35 argued that "the wipe sets its mark before it enters the queue, so every member arriving after the mark loses". That argument does not hold by construction: the mark is cleared by `clearLaneWipePending` in the wipe's **own continuation after its queue turn resolves** (`principal-lane-lifecycle.ts:157` set, `:210` turn) **[code]**, so a capture admitted to the queue *after* the wipe can find the mark already cleared and write a complete grant into a lane that was just swept, re-read clean and published `laneState: 'absent'`. **Decision:** the closure is the login session's own state. §2f's `cancel` step runs *before* the wipe takes its queue turn and moves every in-flight session of that lane to `cancelled` — a per-session, monotonic state that nothing later flips back — and the capture step re-checks **its own session state** after acquiring the queue, refusing `accounts.lane.login_cancelled` (complete sentence; §3 Rule-3 row) unless the session is still `live`. The wipe-pending mark stays as a second, *advisory* refusal (`accounts.lane.wipe_in_progress`) and never carries correctness weight. The in-lane switch gets the same shape: `selectAccount` re-reads `laneState` under the queue and refuses when it is `absent`. Consequence, stated so the queue's admission order is provably irrelevant: a capture admitted *before* the wipe writes, and the wipe then cancels, sweeps and re-reads it away; a capture admitted *after* the wipe finds its session `cancelled` and writes nothing; and a capture whose child exited into a successful `auth status --json` but whose queue turn has not come is in exactly the second case. §5's fence fixture therefore scripts the capture acquiring the queue **after** the wipe's continuation has already cleared the mark, and asserts refusal on session state alone (mutation proof: making the re-check read the mark instead of the session turns it red). The shipped guard-outside-the-queue defect (`lane-wire-authority.ts:112` vs `:120-122`) **[code]** is the pattern this paragraph exists to make unrepeatable in S9-L1.

**R2 and R3 are unchanged and still rest on the same single assumption.** The write is scoped to the directory the launch was pinned to, so every live `claude` in that lane re-resolves on its next credential read and every terminal in another lane is untouched. That is the one behaviour §4 pins and §5 probes; if the probe fails, this degrades exactly as it always did — `laneState: 'restart-required'`, new terminals in that lane get the new account — and no further.

**What the switch no longer needs, and it is most of §2c as written through rev 31.** The **lane sync driver** existed because "a writer alone is not enough": the desktop's copy went stale when the lane's own `claude` rotated, and the ordinary post-wipe re-push would then restore an already-revoked blob. There is no desktop copy, no re-push, and no staleness — **the lane *is* the store**, so a rotation performed by the lane's CLI is simply the store being updated by its owner, exactly as on any laptop. So: the four triggers, the **watermark** and its three writers, `basedOnRefreshTokenSha256`, the `reauthenticated` flag, the strictly-older-push comparison, the observe-only startup sync, the `foreign-rotation` receipt cause and `accounts.lane.push_stale` are all **deleted** (`shared/claude-lane-watermark.ts`, `claude-accounts/lane-sync-driver.ts`, and the watermark rows in `principal-lane-store.ts`) **[code, by deletion]**.

**One small thing does survive from the sync driver, and it is a read rather than a sync.** Presence (§2h) and usage (§2k) both need to know *which account the lane is on right now*. That is one read of the lane's `oauthAccount` and, where available, the identity `auth status --json` reported at capture — recomputed on lane open, on a switch, on a completed login, and on a status subscribe. It writes nothing, compares nothing across machines, and cannot fail in a way that strands a lane. `lane-sync-driver.ts` therefore shrinks to a **lane identity resolver**, and the name changes with it (S9-L3).

**And the `cli-observed` rotation receipt loses its purpose along with the watermark.** Its whole job was telling the desktop that the lane's CLI had rotated the shared token so the desktop could `pullRotated` before its own copy went stale. With no shared token and no desktop copy there is nothing to tell and nothing to pull: the rotation receipt shrinks to the two states a *person* still needs — `loaded` and `reauth-required` — and `accounts.lane.pullRotated` is deleted (§3).

### (d) Authorization

Lane RPCs derive their target lane from `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` **only** — never from a parameter, and the params schema has no lane or principal field to spoof. The derivation is two host-side lookups, both host-written: socket → `device.deviceId` (`runtime-rpc.ts:1384`) **[code]** → the registry row's `principalId` (§2a). A grant bound to no principal addresses no lane at all, and is refused exactly as an anonymous caller is. This is the pattern the Run mailbox already argues explicitly (`rpc/methods/orchestration-remote-run-mailbox.ts:11-22`) **[code]**. Anonymous callers are refused **on the lane RPCs**: a Unix-socket caller — on `win32`, a named-pipe caller — cannot address a lane *through them* at all. **That is a statement about the RPC surface and not about the host, and rev 33 states the exception rather than leaving it to be discovered in §9.** The host-only consent/CLI surface, the same local surface that binds and designates (§2a), addresses lanes by `--person`, and `orca lane login --person <p>` runs the login flow **inline on the host** (§6's S9-L2, §9 step 0 item 8) — which is what makes a headless serve box self-sufficient. That caller is the local human, who is the authority this whole design rests on. What it must **not** be is a second, unfenced way into a lane, and binding (iv) below is what stops it from being one.

**Every refusal below is conditioned on the *caller's own* lane state, never on "some lane exists on this host."** A host-global dormancy switch would let one grant's single RPC downgrade what every other client may do — the cross-grant influence §a's provisioning rule closes.

**The four login-session RPCs take that derivation unchanged and add bindings of their own — three in rev 32, and a fourth in rev 33 that scopes them to the session rather than to this RPC surface; together they are the whole of what stands between the relay and a durable grant on someone else's account (§4).** `accounts.lane.loginStart`, `loginSubmitCode`, `loginCancel` and `loginStatus` each resolve the lane as `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)` and carry no lane or principal field, exactly like every other lane RPC. On top of that:

- **(i) The session is created only by a grant bound to the requesting principal *and* holding that principal's designation — and rev 34 splits it in two, because binding (iv) has to speak about the halves separately.** **(i-a) is a precondition on the *lane*:** the principal must hold a designation at all. **(i-b) is a test on *this caller*:** the grant asking must be the designated one. **Every entry point that mints a session takes (i-a); only a caller that *is* a grant can be measured against (i-b)** — the distinction rev 33's “exempt from (i) and (ii)” collapsed, contradicting §2a, §3's revoke row and §5 all at once. `delegatedGrantId` survives rev 32 with a **new meaning** — from "the one grant that may push credentials into this lane" to "the one grant that may *start a login* for this lane and *see the URL*" (§2a, §2e) — so a bound-but-undesignated grant is refused `accounts.lane.login_not_designated`, and a principal with no designation is refused `accounts.lane.no_login_device_designated`. That is a **Rule 3** meaning change on a value old clients already read; the wire name is kept because renaming it would buy nothing and cost every client that renders it (the same reasoning §2h applies to `credentialLane: 'grant'`).
- **(ii) The URL is delivered only on the requesting grant's own connection**, through §2d's existing per-connection projection and the lane status stream's per-grant subscriber filing (`runtime/lane-status-stream.ts:38-40` — "The grant, never the principal: the binding it resolves through can move under the socket") **[code]**. It is never broadcast to the principal's other grants and never rides a peer-visible row.
- **(iii) The code is accepted only from the same grant, only for a session that grant started, and only while the host process that minted the verifier is alive.** PKCE makes the last clause mechanically true — a code redeemed against a different `code_verifier` simply fails at the token endpoint — but it is also enforced *at the RPC*, so a cross-principal or cross-session submit is a **named refusal** (`accounts.lane.login_session_unknown`) rather than a silent verifier mismatch a person cannot diagnose. **At most one login is in flight per lane**, and a second start is refused `accounts.lane.login_already_in_flight` rather than queued, so there is never an ambiguous "which URL is this" moment for the human to resolve. **Rev 34 states the cross-surface half of this binding, which rev 33 specified for `loginCancel` and left open for the submit.** A **CLI-started** session has **no owning grant**, so **no** grant may submit into it from any surface — the code is printed to, and pasted at, the operator's own terminal, and a `loginSubmitCode` naming that session id is refused `accounts.lane.login_session_unknown` for every caller, the designated grant included. A **grant-started** session is symmetrical: the submit belongs to the starting grant alone, and the host's own consent/CLI surface may **cancel** it (the schema facts below) but may not complete it. The asymmetry is deliberate — cancelling destroys, completing mints — and it is one comparison either way.
- **(iv) Every property of the session belongs to the *session*, not to this RPC surface — new in rev 33, and it is what closes the second entry point.** `orca lane login --person <p>`, run at the host's own shell, mints a real grant into a named lane **without passing through `loginStart`** (§6's S9-L2, §9 step 0 item 8), and the §9 runbook makes that path mandatory on a headless serve box. A rule stated only over "the four login-session RPCs" therefore leaves a second writer unfenced: two `claude` children, two verifiers, two authorization URLs and two captures racing one `<lane>/claude-accounts` root and one automatic switch — precisely the "which URL is this" ambiguity binding (iii) says is never created. **So the per-lane single-in-flight lock, the TTL, the retry cap, the I6 identity check, the half-initialized-lane sweep and the lifecycle rules below are properties of the lane's login session, taken by every entry point that mints one.** Concretely: the CLI's inline start is refused `accounts.lane.login_already_in_flight` against a grant-started session, a grant's `loginStart` is refused the same way against a CLI-started one, and both register in the same per-lane session map. What the inline path is exempt from is **(i-b) and (ii) alone — corrected in rev 34, because rev 33 wrote “(i) and (ii)” and that flatly contradicted §2a's refusal, §3's revoke row and §5's authorization arm, none of which carries an entry-point carve-out.** There is no grant to measure against `delegatedGrantId` and no connection to scope a URL to, because the URL is printed to the operator's own terminal, and that operator is the local human §2a already treats as the authority. **But (i-a) is a precondition on the *lane*, and the inline path takes it:** a principal holding no designation — the state rule (iii)'s unbind/revoke clear leaves behind — is refused `accounts.lane.no_login_device_designated` here exactly as over the wire, and the operator's remedy is `orca lane designate --person <p> --device <d>` at the same shell, one audited consent write, before the login. That is what keeps a headless box self-sufficient *and* keeps §2a's rule flat: the box recovers, and it recovers **through** the consent surface rather than around it. **`--email` is required on this path too, not optional (rev 34):** an inline start without it is a CLI usage error before any session is minted, because an optional expectation makes I6 skippable at the caller's own choice on precisely the entry point §9 makes mandatory. §5 asserts the mutual refusal in both directions.

A session that outlives its TTL is refused `accounts.lane.login_session_expired` and its process group is killed; the CLI does **not** bound the window itself — a bad code makes it print `Invalid code. Please make sure the full code was copied.` and **re-prompt** rather than exit **[code, observed on 2.1.247]** — so `LOGIN_TIMEOUT_MS = 180_000` (`service.ts:52`) plus the existing `AbortController` and win32 `taskkill` teardown (`service.ts:1103`, `:1114`, `:1153-1157`) **[code]** are what close it, together with a cap on retry attempts per session so a co-tenant cannot pin a lane in "logging in" indefinitely.

**Session lifecycle under an unexpected child exit, a host restart and a designation change — three rules, stated because §3's Rule-3 row forbids leaving them to the implementer (rev 33).** **(1) Sessions are process-local.** The session map is in memory and is **never persisted**, so an Orca restart ends every in-flight login; a `loginSubmitCode` after a restart is refused `accounts.lane.login_session_unknown`, which is a named refusal carrying its own sentence and not a lookup miss of unspecified shape. **Rev 34 states what that does *not* end, and it is the whole of round 2's blocker: a restart ends the session *record*, not the *directory* and not the *child*.** On an ordinary quit the host **cancels every in-flight session before it exits** — the same `cancel` §2f's fence uses: kill the process group, sweep the half-written `<laneAccountId>/auth`. On a `kill -9`, a crash or a power loss there is no such path, and on POSIX the login child is `detached` and therefore its own process group by construction (the spawn at `claude-accounts/service.ts:1099`, stdin a pipe at `:1103`, `detached` at `:1109`) **[code]** — the shape §6's S9-L1 says to reuse — so it can outlive the host and write a complete grant into a directory no session owns. Whether it instead tears itself down on stdin EOF is the same scraped CLI behaviour `:1100-1102` documents as load-bearing, and nothing here rests on it. **Two decisions follow.** *(a) No pid is persisted and no child is killed across a restart* — pid reuse makes a stored pid an unsafe kill target, and persisting one would re-open exactly the session state this rule keeps out of storage. *(b) The reaping is done at the **directory** level, at startup, by §2f's account-store reconciliation* — every `<laneAccountId>` directory with no index row is deleted, the store is re-read, and a directory that reappears means a surviving child is still writing and is **surfaced** rather than absorbed. That pass is the backstop, and §2f states it normatively. **(2) A child exit reaps its session.** When the `claude` process exits before the TTL and before a successful capture — a crash, an operator `kill`, a CLI that gave up — the host removes the session on the exit event and sweeps the half-written login directory, and every later submit for that id is `accounts.lane.login_session_unknown`. Without this rule the natural implementation writes the code into a dead pipe and surfaces an untyped `EPIPE`, which is exactly the class §3's Rule-3 row forbids. **Rev 35 states the case this rule does *not* cover, because §2f's `cancel` has to reach it.** A child that exits *into* a successful capture has not ended the work: the capture tail — `auth status --json`, then a turn on the lane's write queue for the credential rewrite, the `oauthAccount` merge and the index row (§2b, §2c) — runs **after** the exit event. A session in that state is neither reaped by this rule nor `captured` yet, and it stays **cancellable**: `cancel` invalidates a session in **any** state before `captured`, child-exited-but-capture-pending included, and the capture step's post-queue re-check is what makes that invalidation bite (§2c, §2f). "Confirm the child dead" is therefore not sufficient on its own for the login writer — a child that exited a second ago may still have a complete grant landing behind it — which is why §2f's fence cancels first and the capture re-checks second. **(3) A designation clear, a designation *move*, or a revoke of the grant that owns the session, cancels it — the move is rev 34's addition.** §2a rule (iii)'s unbind/revoke clear leaves a lane with no designation; that clear, and a revoke of the starting grant itself, both **cancel** the session — kill the process group, sweep the login directory, emit `login-failed`. §2a specifies the revoke ordering for the lane wipe (capture `principalOf(deviceId)` before `removeDevice` drops the row); this is that same ordering applied to the session. **Why the *move* belongs here, and why rev 33's two cancellers were not enough:** §2a offers re-designation at **any** time as the remedy for a mis-designation, and §2a, §2l and §3's degradation row all grade a mis-tick as “recovered by a re-tick”. A move is neither a clear nor a revoke, so without this rule the mis-designated grant's in-flight session survives the very act meant to stop it — the designation is checked at **start**, and binding (iii) then accepts that grant's `loginSubmitCode` because it is still the grant that started the session. The operator notices the wrong device holds the designation, re-ticks, and the wrong device completes anyway. One canceller, symmetrical with the two already stated, closes it, and §5 asserts it. **And §2f's lifecycle fence is the fourth canceller**, on logout / last-grant revoke / deprovision, with its own start-side precondition.

**Four schema facts that touch authority, stated because leaving them to the implementer re-opens a binding — three in rev 33, a fourth in rev 34.** **`expectedEmail` is required and non-empty** on `loginStart` **and on every other entry point that mints a session, the host-inline `orca lane login` included (rev 34)**: if it were optional, I6 would be skippable at the caller's own choice, and the mis-authentication case §2b names would go unchecked — which is exactly what rev 33's own CLI spelling, `orca lane login --person <p> [--email <e>]`, permitted on the one path §9 makes mandatory on a headless box. The bracket is removed here, in §6's S9-L2 and in §9 step 0 item 8. **`loginStatus({ loginSessionId })` never re-serves `authorizeUrl`** — its reply carries the session's state, `expiresAt`, the attempt count and, only on completion, the verified identity, because re-serving the URL would defeat binding (ii) by letting the principal's *other* grants fetch what was delivered to one connection. **`loginCancel` is accepted only from the grant that started the session**, or from the host's own consent/CLI surface; letting any bound grant cancel would let an undesignated grant of the same principal pin its own person's lane out of a login — small, within-principal, and closed for the cost of one comparison. **`loginStatus({ loginSessionId })` is accepted only from the grant that started the session, or from the host's own consent/CLI surface — new in rev 34, and it is the fourth fact because the other three left the one RPC whose key is *published* without a caller rule.** `loginSessionId` rides the `login-started` frame to every bound grant of the principal (§3 row 2), the undesignated ones (i-b) refuses at start included, and the stream files subscribers by grant with the principal re-resolved on delivery (`runtime/lane-status-stream.ts:38-43`) **[code]** — so “look the id up in the in-memory map and answer” is the obvious implementation and it answers the wrong audience. The comparison is the one binding (iii) already insists on for the submit — the session's owning grant against `ctx.pairedDeviceId` — and any other caller is refused `accounts.lane.login_session_unknown`, the same code and the same sentence, so nothing is minted for it. The harm this closes is bounded (the verified identity already rides `login-completed` to that same audience) and so is the cost. The frame keeps carrying the id, because that is what lets the principal's other grants render “a login is in progress” and correlate the `login-completed` / `login-failed` that follows.

- `accounts.selectClaude` (`accounts.ts:118-127`) materializes the chosen account into the shared `~/.claude` (`runtime-auth-service.ts:477-478`) **[code]** and, after rev 32, is gated on **one** condition rather than two. *Caller scope, unchanged:* **anonymous local callers** unchanged and host-wide (this is `orca account select` and the host's own renderer); **identified callers with no provisioned lane** unchanged and host-wide; **identified callers that hold a lane** — **refused** `accounts.selection_out_of_scope`, telling them to switch **in their own lane**, which is now a real verb (§2c) rather than a pointer at a push. *Target residency — **deleted**.* L1 is retired (§2e), so `accounts.lane.account_resident_elsewhere` and `accounts.lane.residency_unverifiable` are deleted, `assertManagedClaudeAccountNotLaneResident` is deleted from both of its call sites (`claude-accounts/service.ts:415` in `doRemoveAccount` and `:472` in `doSelectAccount`) **[code]**, and `assertClaudeAccountNotDelegatedToLane` (`service.ts:475`) is deleted with the lease it reads. **Those two lines are the 2026-08-27 incident**, and they are removed rather than relaxed: selecting account X locally while X is logged into a lane is now harmless, because the two locations hold two grants with two refresh-token chains and neither can rotate the other's.
- `accounts.removeClaude` (`:147-151`): the residency refusal is **deleted** for the same reason. Removing a managed desktop account can no longer strand a lane, because the lane never held that account's blob — it holds its own grant, in its own store, which `removeClaude` does not touch.
- `accounts.addClaudeFromConfigDir` (`:197-212`) and `accounts.addCodexFromHome` (`:213-225`) keep their containment refusal **unchanged, and it matters more rather than less.** `assertCaptureSourceOutsideClaudeLanes(trimmed, 'claude-config-dir')` is already the first statement of `addAccountFromConfigDir` (`claude-accounts/service.ts:216`) **[code]**, canonicalizing through the shared helper the way `managed-auth-path.ts:30-42` does. Under push, the prize inside a lane was a *copy* of a credential the desktop already held; under per-lane login it is an **independent, durable grant that no lease will ever clear**, so laundering it into a permanent managed account is a strictly larger harm than it was.
- `accounts.list` (`:102-115`) and `accounts.subscribe` (`:182-217`) keep rev 25's per-connection projection unchanged in shape. The peer branch is still exactly `scope`/`occupied`/`ownerLabel`/`displayName` (§10 item 26), with `displayName` now the **owner-set label of §2b** rather than a string the pushing desktop asserted — the same field, a better provenance.

Compatibility consequence, in full: a paired client **loses** host-wide Claude switching exactly when it has been given its own lane — i.e. only after the local human granted it one — and it gains an in-lane switch and an in-lane login in exchange. A grant with no lane keeps everything it has today. **Q5 = RESOLVED: NO — the shared lane stays open, and rev 32 makes the residual *simpler* rather than larger.** Through rev 31 the residual carried a carve-out — such a grant could move the shared lane "for accounts that are **not** resident in a lane". That carve-out is deleted with L1: **no account is ever lane-resident** in a sense any refusal can read, so a grant bound to no principal keeps host-wide `accounts.selectClaude` over **every** account, for every caller class, exactly as before S9. What it can do is move the **default** under every other lane-less terminal on the box; it cannot touch a provisioned lane and cannot make a lane pane resolve anywhere but its lane. The mitigation stays procedural and stays stated: **bind and provision both developers before either relies on the box.**

### (e) Rotation — the lane's own CLI rotates its own chain, and Orca's managed refresh is not applied to a lane

**Decision, inverted from every prior revision: for a lane, the CLI owns refresh.** Rev 5 through rev 31 gave rotation to the host, and the reason was stated at the site: Orca owns refresh so that "a single-use refresh token is rotated and persisted atomically, instead of being scraped back after the CLI rotates it (the lossy path that strands stale tokens)" (`oauth-refresh.ts:4-8`) **[code]**. That reason is exactly right **wherever a second store has to be kept in sync** — the desktop's managed store, and the shared `~/.claude` — and it has no application inside a lane, because **there is no second store**: the lane *is* the store, and the `claude` running in it rotating its own token is simply correct, as on any laptop. Orca's managed refresh keeps running, unchanged, for managed desktop accounts and for the shared lane. It is not run against a lane.

**That is a re-keying of one gate, not the deletion of one, and the distinction is worth stating because the mechanics brief proposed the deletion.** `claude-fetcher.ts:1215-1230` refreshes an expiring credential and writes it back while fetching usage, today gated by `!isClaudeAccountDelegatedToLane(account.id)` (`:1216`) **[code]**. The *delegation* predicate is deleted with the lease. What replaces it is **not nothing**: the per-lane usage pull hands that fetcher a **lane** `authPreparation` (§2k Fact 1, `claude-fetcher.ts:236-248`), and Orca must not rotate a lane's chain out from under the lane's own live `claude` any more than it may rotate a delegated one. So the gate becomes a **lane-preparation** predicate — Orca-side refresh is refused for any preparation whose config dir is a lane, always, not only when some lease says so — and the reason changes from "L1 says this account lives elsewhere" to "this chain belongs to that lane's CLI". The site is the same line; the predicate and its justification are both new.

**Invariant L1 is retired, and its machinery is deleted rather than relaxed.** L1 said "one account, at most one lane", and its entire justification was that a single-use refresh token shared between two copies gets rotated out from under one of them. Two independent grants have two independent refresh tokens, so the premise is void (§ preamble). Deleted: `claude-accounts/account-residency-index.ts` (171 lines), `claude-accounts/managed-account-lane-residency.ts` (112), `assertNotLaneResident` / `assertManagedClaudeAccountNotLaneResident` / `assertAccountNotResidentElsewhere` and their three call sites, the dual `accountUuid` + `sha256(refreshToken)` keying, the re-derived `host` row, and the refusal codes `accounts.lane.account_resident_elsewhere` and `accounts.lane.residency_unverifiable` **[code, by deletion]**. **The index is not kept as a display-only fact**, and the reason is that it would have nothing true to display: it compared identity and refresh-token shas to decide whether two locations held *the same credential*, and after rev 32 two locations on one account hold two chains **by construction**, so every honest answer is "different". A UI that wants to say "this account is also signed in on lane X" needs an *account-identity* join, which §2k's row already performs, not a residency index.

**The delegation lease is deleted entire, and with it the rule that fired on 2026-08-27.** Deleted: `claude-accounts/lane-delegation-lease.ts` (229), `claude-accounts/lane-delegation-push-client.ts`, `lane-delegation-host-client.ts`, `lane-delegation-desktop-service.ts`, `lane-push-envelope.ts`, `runtime-credential-lane-clearing.ts` (109), `shared/claude-lane-lease.ts`, `CLAUDE_LANE_LEASE_TTL_MS`, and rules (i)–(iv) in full **[code, by deletion]**. Rule (iv) is the one to name explicitly, because it is the incident: `clearRuntimeCredentialsForDelegatedAccount` removed `~/.claude/.credentials.json` and nulled `oauthAccount` in the co-located `.claude.json` whenever the resident identity was not a *known different* account (`runtime-credential-lane-clearing.ts:11-22` states the intent; the clearing runs at `:41-74`) **[code]** — note that an *unknown* identity was cleared too, deliberately, because "the failure this rule prevents is a plain `claude` rotating a token Orca cannot prove is not the lane's". Under per-lane login Orca *can* prove it: the lane's chain is not the desktop's chain, so there is nothing to protect and the clearing is deleted rather than narrowed. The `delegatedGrantId` **field** survives with §2d's new meaning — which grant may start a login and see the URL — and it is still written by exactly one act, the host-side consent tick over that principal's already-bound grants (§2a).

**R-DR2 is retired as a class, and that is the single largest thing rev 32 removes.** R-DR2 was *"two Orca installs holding the same account rotate the single-use refresh token out from under each other"*, and it was the largest accepted residual in §4 through rev 31: unfixable host-side, detection-only, with an operating rule stated in the UI — *"an account delegated to a host must not be live in another Orca install"* — which is verbatim what the owner objected to. There are no two copies of one grant anywhere in the shipped model, so the class has no members — **with one bounded, dated exception rev 33 names rather than leaving to the migration paragraph:** the lane that is loaded *today* by a push keeps a byte copy of the desktop's grant for the length of the cutover, and S9-L3 deletes the very lease and rule (iv) that suppressed the desktop's rotator against it. For that window R-DR2 has exactly **one** member, on the one box that matters, and it ends the way R-DR2 always ended — `invalid_grant` at the desktop's next rotation, recovered by one lane login (§3's migration row, §4, §6's S9-L3). Deleted with it: the `delegation` envelope member, the `foreign-rotation` receipt cause, `laneState: 'reauth-required'` **as a foreign-rotation signal**, and the operating rule in the desktop UI. **What replaces it is ordinary revocation, and it is not R-DR2 wearing a new name:** an account-wide credential reset — a password change, or revoking sessions from claude.ai — still ends *every* grant on that account, the lane's included, and the lane then fails `invalid_grant` on its next refresh. That surfaces as `laneState: 'reauth-required'` and the remedy is one lane login. It is visible, it is the person's own deliberate act, and no other grant caused it.

**Rotation serialization re-keys from account to lane, which is the mirror image of rev 5's decision and is now the sound one.** §2e through rev 31 said flatly that "keying rotation by *lane* is unsound", and the whole of that argument was L1's premise. With two lanes holding two chains, **lane is the correct key**: `lane-auth-state.ts`'s per-lane write queue is kept, its per-ACCOUNT rotation queue (`:111-120`) is re-keyed per lane, and `managedRefreshDeferredByLivePtyAccountId` follows it. **What that queue actually serializes, stated precisely because rev 32 over-claimed it:** `serializeAccountRotation` (`lane-auth-state.ts:111-120`) is **Orca's own** rotation queue **[code]**, and after this section's decision the only credentials Orca still rotates are the managed desktop store and the **shared** lane.
