So the re-key removes a false constraint — two lanes' Orca-side work no longer blocks on one account key — rather than adding a control over the lanes themselves. **The per-lane *write* queue does gain a job in rev 34, and rev 35 corrects its membership to three:** §2c's two surviving writers — the in-lane switch and the login session's capture — enter through `serializeLaneWrite(principalId, …)` **before** taking the switch gate, which is what makes that gate's bare `Error` unreachable by construction rather than merely unlikely; and **§2f's wipe is the third member**, already inside the queue in the shipped tree (`principal-lane-lifecycle.ts:210`, wired at `lane-credential-coordinator.ts:87`) **[code]**, holding **precedence** over both. Precedence, not order: the wipe marks the lane before entering, and each of the other two re-checks that mark — the capture also re-checking that its own session is still live — **after** acquiring the queue rather than before it, refused `accounts.lane.wipe_in_progress` if either changed (§2c, §2f, §3 row 9). It says nothing about two `claude` processes rotating inside one lane; that is §2k's first bullet, and Orca does not mediate it. `lane-auth-state.ts` also drops the watermark, the `basedOnRefreshTokenSha256` freshness comparison, the rotation receipts' `input-superseded` / `lane-write-lost` outcomes' push arms, and the eight-field re-key's push-specific members (§2c).

**What survives untouched, stated so the deletion slice does not overreach.** The live-PTY gate (`live-pty-gate.ts`) survives in full, including `markClaudePtySpawned` / `markClaudePtyExited`, the lane-scoped `hasLiveClaudePtysInLane` (`:169`), the ephemeral (non-persisting) arm the usage probe registers through (`:136-151`), and the per-lane switch gate (`:205`/`:212`/`:216`) **[code]** — a lane's `.credentials.json` can still be rewritten *by a switch* while a live `claude` holds it, which is a real hazard independent of how the credential arrived. The seeded-live-PTY reconciliation (`index.ts:2241-2250`, `daemon/daemon-init.ts:1043-1061`) survives; what it no longer protects against is a rotating startup pass, because there is no startup pass. `refreshClaudeOauthCredentials` itself survives, unchanged, for the managed desktop store and the shared lane.

### (f) Lifecycle — the lane's grant is resident like a laptop's login

**Decision, and it is the one owner default that makes this model *weaker* than push on one axis while making it usable on the axis the owner actually complained about: the disconnect wipe and the startup wipe are deleted.** Under push, wiping on last-close was nearly free — recovery was "re-push on reconnect", invisible to the human. Under per-lane login the recovery is a **browser round-trip**, so keeping wipe-on-disconnect would turn every dropped Wi-Fi, every idle timeout and every Orca restart into a re-authorization. That is the owner's own objection reappearing in a new place, and it would make this model worse than push on the seamlessness axis. It is removed, not tuned. **Owner-pending: this is a default applied ahead of explicit ratification (§7 question 12).**

**The lane's credential ends on exactly four events, and on nothing else.** (a) An explicit `accounts.lane.logout`. (b) A **grant revoke where the revoked grant was that principal's last** — the revoke arm S9c shipped, kept verbatim, including the ordering the two revoke sites force: capture `principalOf(deviceId)` **before** `removeDevice(deviceId)` drops the row (`runtime-rpc.ts:648` then `:653`; `:660` then `:666`) **[code]** and evaluate the survivor query after it returns. (c) A deliberate lane **deprovision** (the same consent UI and the same `orca lane deprovision` verb). (d) **Upstream expiry or revocation**, which is not a wipe at all — the credential simply stops working and the lane reads `reauth-required` until someone logs it in again. Disconnect, idle timeout, Orca restart and host reboot no longer end anything. `principal-lane-lifecycle.ts:24`'s `LaneWipeReason = 'last-connection-close' | 'startup' | 'grant-revoked'` **[code]** therefore becomes `'explicit-logout' | 'grant-revoked' | 'deprovision'`: the first two of the shipped three are deleted, the third survives with its survivor query intact. **And the *scope* of what those events end is stated normatively here, because rev 32 introduced a second place a lane's credentials live and left "the lane's credential" in the singular everywhere it was already written (rev 33).** A lane's credential is not one file. It is **(1)** `<lane>/.credentials.json`, **(2)** the `oauthAccount` block in `<lane>/.claude.json`, **(3)** **every `<lane>/claude-accounts/<laneAccountId>/auth` directory** — one complete, independently usable OAuth grant per login the lane has performed (§2a, §2c) — **(4)** every staged `*.tmp` sibling of any of them, under both temp-name shapes (§2m(2)), and — **new in rev 35** — **(5)** the lane's own account index, `<lane>/claude-accounts/index.json`, with any `index.json.<pid>.<uuid>.tmp` sibling. Member (5) is not a secret and is not why it is here: it is the store's authority (§2a rule (2)), and a logout that swept the directories and left the index behind would leave rows naming grants that no longer exist, which is the phantom entry §2c rule (1) refuses in the other direction. **All five are in scope for logout, for the last-grant revoke and for deprovision, and the post-sweep re-read covers all five.** **Rev 34 adds one word to class (3) and it is load-bearing: *every* `<laneAccountId>` directory, indexed or not, marker-valid or not — and rev 35 adds one more shape to it: every `<laneAccountId>.quarantined-<ts>` directory a startup pass renamed but could not judge (§2f below).** A quarantined directory is a complete grant at rest under a name nothing resolves; leaving it out of the purge would mean a lane could be logged out, report `absent`, and still hold a usable credential — the identical failure this paragraph exists to state, arriving through the one arm rev 35 added. The purge is a directory walk under the lane's own `claude-accounts` root, with canonical containment and symlink refusal ordered first — **not** a loop over `listLaneAccounts` and **not** gated on `resolveOwnedClaudeManagedAuthPath` returning non-null (§2c rule (2)). A crashed login's directory is exactly the one whose index row was never written and whose marker may be missing, so a marker-gated purge would certify a lane empty while that directory held a complete grant, which is the failure this paragraph exists to state rather than a new one. A wipe that empties (1) and (2) and leaves (3) is a lane that reports its credential gone while N−1 durable grants sit at rest at `0600`, inside a directory whose Windows DACL grants FullControl to local Administrators by construction (§2m(6)) — and that is what ships today: `listLaneCredentialArtifacts` and `sweepOneLanePass` read the lane directory's own top level and match `.credentials.json` plus `*.tmp` and nothing else (`principal-lane-credential-sweep.ts:48-58`, `:109-117`) **[code]**. Extending the sweep is therefore an S9-L1 obligation, not a restatement of shipped behaviour. **Logout and deprovision do not differ in this scope** — both mean *no grant of this lane's remains on this box*; what differs is what survives around them: deprovision also removes the lane directory, its registry row and its designation, while logout leaves the lane provisioned and empty at `laneState: 'absent'`, ready for the next login.

**Startup sweeps tmp artifacts and reconciles the lane account store, and nothing else — rev 34 adds the second half; through rev 33 this heading read "and nothing else" over the sweep alone, which is what left round 2's blocker open.** The crash hazard rev 5 identified is real and is unchanged: `writeFileAtomically` stages at `${targetPath}.${pid}.${randomUUID()}.tmp` and unlinks only on a *thrown* error, and `writeSecureFile` stages under a second shape (§2m(2)), so a crash mid-write leaves a full-credential blob at `0600` under a name no filename-scoped wipe touches. `sweepLaneCredentialTempArtifacts` already matches the **pattern** rather than either literal (`principal-lane-credential-sweep.ts:9-28`) **[code]**, and it runs at startup and at lane open, exactly as before. What no longer runs at startup is the **credential** wipe, the observe-only `syncLane` that preceded it and the watermark it recorded (§2c) — so the startup ordering collapses from "observe-only sync → watermark → wipe → seed → listen" to "**sweep tmp → reconcile the lane account store → seed → listen**" (the middle step is rev 34's; rev 33's ordering read "sweep tmp → seed → listen", and that is what left round 2's blocker open), and the reason the sync had to be observe-only (it ran before `seedLiveClaudePtysFromPersistence`, so a rotating pass would hand the lane `invalid_grant` across every live session; `index.ts:2241` states that ordering invariant in so many words) **[code]** is retired with the pass itself. **The account-store reconciliation, new in rev 34 and re-gated in rev 35, and it replaces the deleted credential wipe rather than reviving it.** Sessions are process-local and are never persisted (§2d rule (1)), so after any restart **no** session owns **any** directory in **any** lane's store: the lane's own index (§2a rule (2)) is the only record of what a completed login produced. **For each lane independently**, the pass reads that lane's `<lane>/claude-accounts/index.json`, walks that lane's `<lane>/claude-accounts/` — **directory entries only**; the index file and its staged `.tmp` siblings are not accounts — and then takes one of two arms. **Arm A — the index was read and parsed.** Delete every `<laneAccountId>` directory with no matching row, drop every row with no directory or no credential, and then **re-read**: the same post-sweep re-read the wipe uses, under the sweep's existing pass cap (`LANE_SWEEP_PASSES = 3`, `principal-lane-credential-sweep.ts:45`) **[code]**. **Arm B — the index is missing, does not parse, or parses empty over a store that is not.** **Quarantine; delete nothing.** Every unindexed `<laneAccountId>` directory is **renamed** `<laneAccountId>.quarantined-<ts>`, and the pass logs the lane, the count and the reason. A quarantined name is not the `randomUUID` shape lane account ids are validated against (§2a), so no later pass mistakes it for an account, `listLaneAccounts` never offers it, no login resumes into it and it does not count against `MAX_LANE_LOGINS` — which is itself a named cost: the cap counts index rows, so a lane that quarantined N directories can go on to hold eight *more* logins, and up to N+8 grants sit at rest until something sweeps them (R-34a, §7 question 19). It **is** in class (3)'s scope, so the lane's next logout, last-grant revoke or deprovision deletes it. The empty-parse case is arm B for the same reason §2a's other pass requires a **non-empty** registry: a zero-row index over a store holding directories is the shape a truncated write takes, and it is indistinguishable from the real thing at the moment it matters. **An absent index over an absent or empty `claude-accounts/` root is not arm B at all** — it is the ordinary state of a lane that has never logged in, or has just been logged out — and the pass does nothing. **Why two arms, when rev 34 had none:** rev 34 read the index out of Orca's persisted state, where one truncated write, one JSON corruption or one restore from a backup predating the last N logins sends `Persistence.load` down its backup chain and then to `getDefaultPersistedState(homedir())` (`persistence.ts:3160`, catch at `:3866-3868`, assignment at `:3888`) **[code]**, every collection reading empty and silently — under which "delete every unindexed directory" means *delete every login in every lane on this host*. Rev 35 removes the mechanism (the index is in the lane) **and** the consequence (an unreadable index quarantines); the two together are what make this pass safe to run without a registry gate, and "it compares the lane against itself" a fact rather than a premise. **Ordering:** it runs **before the seed** and before any lane is served, and **after** §2a's lane-level orphan reconciliation, over the lanes that survive it; it is **not** gated on the device registry's `loadSucceeded` (§2a), because it reads no registry row and arm B is its own fail-safe rather than a borrowed gate. **Four consequences, stated rather than discovered.** *(1) Arm A can delete a complete grant.* A crash between the CLI's capture write and the index write leaves a usable credential with no row, and this pass destroys it. That is the fail-closed direction and it is the deliberate trade: the cost is one browser round-trip, and the alternative — keeping unindexed directories — is a durable grant at rest that no wipe rule in this document reaches (§7 question 19). *(2) Destroying it locally does not revoke it upstream.* The grant stays valid at its source until it expires or the person revokes it, so this is a hygiene pass and not a revocation, and §4 carries it as **R-34a**. *(3) A directory that reappears across the re-read means a login child from the previous process is still alive and still writing.* The host does not kill a child it did not spawn (§2d rule (1)), so it publishes that lane `laneState: 'absent'` with `laneWipePending: true` and logs, rather than reporting a clean store it cannot vouch for — the same shape this section already uses for a probe that cannot be confirmed dead. *(4) Arm B leaves complete grants at rest, under a quarantined name — new in rev 35, and it is consequence (1) deliberately inverted for the one input this pass cannot trust.* A parse error is evidence about the *index*, not about the directories, and destroying N working logins on it is the larger harm; but the grants are still there, the signal is a log line and not a published field, and the operator remedy is that log plus `ls <lane>/claude-accounts` — or a logout, which sweeps them. §4's R-34a and §7 question 19 both carry it.

**Every piece of fencing §2f built survives, retargeted from "disconnect" to "logout / revoke / deprovision" — the trigger set shrinks and no logic changes.** The wipe-pending mark, the abort of every in-flight usage probe through `fetchViaPty`'s `options.signal` → `settleAborted` → `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, termDisposables, { kill: true })`, the confirm-dead-before-sweep rule with its bounded window, the post-sweep re-read before the wipe is reported done, the additive `laneWipePending: true` published beside `laneState: 'absent'`, and §2k's two **start-side** preconditions (no probe starts into a lane whose wipe is pending or whose `laneState` is `absent`) are all kept exactly as S9c shipped them (`principal-lane-lifecycle.ts:9-19`, `:39`, `:70`, `:76-77`; `lane-wipe-pending.ts`) **[code]**. A probe's `claude` can still rotate and write `.credentials.json` back after a sweep, so the fence is as load-bearing after rev 32 as before it. **And rev 32 introduced a second post-sweep writer that rev 33 puts inside the same fence: the lane login session.** A login child holds `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` pointed at the lane and writes a complete grant into it at capture time — seconds or minutes after it was started, on a longer clock than a probe but with exactly the shape the fence exists to stop. The arm is the probe's arm with one substitution: **mark wipe-pending → cancel every in-flight login session in that lane → confirm the child dead on the same bounded window the probe uses → sweep → re-read → clear the mark**, with the mark set **before** the wipe takes the lane's write queue, so that every other member of that queue arrives after it (§2c). **Rev 35 restates what `cancel` covers, because rev 34's three actions did not reach the writer that lands last.** `lane-login-session.ts`'s `cancel` kills the process group, sweeps the half-written `<laneAccountId>/auth`, refuses every later `loginSubmitCode` for that id `accounts.lane.login_session_unknown` — **and invalidates the session in any state before `captured`, which includes a session whose child has already exited and whose capture tail has not completed** (§2b, §2d rule (2)). That last clause is the load-bearing one, because **the capture step is not the child**: it runs after the exit — `auth status --json`, then a turn on the lane's write queue for the credential rewrite, the `oauthAccount` merge and the index row — so "confirm the child dead" is satisfied *trivially* by a child that exited a second earlier with a complete grant still landing behind it. A capture whose session `cancel` invalidated discovers that **after** it acquires the queue, where it re-checks wipe-pending and its own liveness and is refused `accounts.lane.wipe_in_progress`, having written nothing (§2c). Without both halves the defeating sequence is not exotic: the capture tail and the logout both enter the queue, the wipe sweeps and re-reads clean, the mark clears, `laneState: 'absent'` is published, and the capture's turn then writes a full grant into the lane — verbatim the failure this fence exists to prevent, arriving through the queue rev 34 added to close a different one. The shipped tree shows the shape is not hypothetical: the push writer's `isLaneWipeInFlight` guard sits at `lane-wire-authority.ts:112`, **outside** the `serializeLaneWrite` it enters at `:120-122` **[code]**. **And rev 34 gives the login arm the probe's *outcome* for that window, not only its duration:** a login child that cannot be confirmed dead leaves the lane published `laneState: 'absent'` **and** `laneWipePending: true`, retried rather than reported done, exactly as the probe's arm does. The shipped `accounts.lane.probe_not_confirmed_dead` (`shared/claude-lane-refusals.ts:43`) **[code]** is **reused rather than twinned**, with its sentence generalized from “the usage probe” to “a process holding this lane's credential”, because the code is not client-mapped and the human's remedy is identical. `accounts.lane.logout_incomplete` stays what it is — a sweep that did not finish — which is a different failure and keeps a different sentence. The **start-side** precondition mirrors §2k's exactly: **no `loginStart` — from a grant *or* from the host-inline CLI (binding (iv), §2d) — into a lane whose wipe is pending or whose `laneState` is `absent` because a wipe is running**, refused `accounts.lane.wipe_in_progress`. §5 asserts the cancel and the start-side refusal **separately**, for the reason it already does for the probe: a suite that tests only the kill passes while the very next call re-loads the lane it just watched being wiped. Without this arm a logout racing a login started moments earlier completes its sweep, the CLI then writes a full grant back into the lane, and §2a consequence 2's fail-closed promise and `laneState: 'absent'` are both false while a live credential sits at rest — the S9c hazard, arriving on the one new writer the re-basing added. **Rev 36:** the fence's precedence over a queued capture rests on the session's `cancelled` state, re-checked by the capture under the queue — not on the mark's timing (§2c).

**`accounts.lane.logout` runs `claude auth logout` inside the lane before sweeping, and that is a capability push never had — but the design does not yet promise what it means.** Under push, deleting the lane's copy left the grant alive on the desktop, so "clear" could only ever mean *removed*, never *revoked*. Under per-lane login the lane's grant is separately revocable at its source, and logout can plausibly mean *actually revoked*. **Whether `claude auth logout` performs a server-side revoke or only clears local state is [inferred] and unverified**, and the design refuses to promise it until it is probed (§5, §7 question 15): until then `logout` means *every credential of this lane's is gone from this box* — all four artifact classes the paragraph above enumerates, the per-lane account store included, and not merely the active blob — which is what `accounts.lane.clear` meant back when a lane could hold only one login, and the UI says that and no more. The refusal on an incomplete sweep keeps its shape and its name changes with the verb: `accounts.lane.logout_incomplete`.

**The residency window is now unbounded, and that is the price of the decision at the top of this section.** Through rev 31 this section could say the credential was resident "from push until the earliest of last-close, idle/keepalive timeout, or the next Orca start", and §4 conceded a co-tenant could read it at any resident moment. After rev 32 the window is **from login until logout**. On Linux the co-tenant is the same Unix user and `0600` protected nothing from them at any moment anyway (§4); on the Windows Server host the lane's own DACL grants FullControl to the local Administrators group by construction (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:71`, `:85-95`) **[code], §2m(6)**, so "unbounded" there means "as long as the lane is signed in". This is a **real confidentiality regression against the co-tenant**, it is inside §4's already-accepted shell-equivalence envelope rather than outside it, and §4 argues the trade is net-positive because what now sits at rest is an **independently revocable grant** rather than a copy of the desktop's own credential — but it is a regression, it is stated as one, and §7 question 12 asks the owner to ratify it rather than absorbing it silently.

**The per-lane switch gate is unchanged and keeps its second caller, renamed.** `beginClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` / `endClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` (`live-pty-gate.ts:205`/`:212`) already have two callers: `syncRuntimeAuthWithLivePtyGate` (`claude-accounts/service.ts:576-582`) **[code]** for the host switch, and the push handler for the lane write. The second becomes the **in-lane switch handler** and the login-session's capture step — both write the lane's `.credentials.json`, so both must fence spawns in that lane and only that lane. The control is unchanged: a spawn in lane A during a switch in lane A is refused, including a plain-shell lane pane; a spawn in lane B is not.

### (g) Handoff — narrowly defined, mostly scoped out

**Q4 = RESOLVED, and the answer splits the question in two — the half the owner asked for is already native.** The owner: *"if we're switching accounts for ourselves we want to continue the conversation, just with a different claude account."* **That needs no feature and S9 must not build one.** A self-switch happens inside the *same* lane (§2c): same `principalId`, therefore the same lane directory, therefore the same `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, therefore — because `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` relocates credentials, `settings.json`, `.claude.json`, transcripts and memory **as a unit** **[doc]** — the same `.claude.json`, the same session transcripts and the same `--resume`/`/resume` surface. The switch replaces exactly two things: the bytes of `.credentials.json`, and the `oauthAccount` key inside the lane's `.claude.json`. Nothing forks, nothing is copied, and no transcript crosses a lane boundary. **The running session re-resolves the new account on its next credential read** — the one assumption §4 pins and §5 probes — so the conversation continues in place, in the same terminal, with a different Claude account. **Rev 32 makes this strictly cheaper rather than changing it:** through rev 31 a self-switch was a *push*, so it required the desktop to hold that account and to be the designated pusher; it is now a **local rewrite among logins the lane already holds** (§2c(i)), so it completes with no other device involved, and a switch *requested from the phone* is the phone calling `accounts.lane.selectAccount` for itself rather than asking a desktop to push (§2l). If the §5 probe fails, this degrades exactly as R2 degrades and no further: `laneState: 'restart-required'`, new terminals in that lane get the new account, and the AccountsPane hedge (`AccountsPane.tsx:802`) **[code]** becomes accurate.

**The half that stays out is the cross-*person* one, and §2g's scope-out stands unchanged.** Transcript-seeded "continue *your* session in *my* account" is not built, for exactly the reason rev 10 gave and Q1 does not touch: the source JSONL lives inside the **other person's** lane, and copying it into a different principal's credential directory defeats the wipe promise and makes a data-mixing decision the owner has not made. Under principals the boundary is *between people*, which is the boundary the owner drew. The containment refusal below is what enforces it, and it is unchanged.

**Shipped (S9d):** `terminal.openInMyLane` — grant-scoped, creates a **new** terminal in the same workspace (git worktree *or* folder workspace, `AGENTS.md:43-45`) **[code]**, under the caller's lane, optionally seeded with a plain-text prompt. It moves no process and copies no files. A caller with **no** lane is refused `terminal.lane_open_no_lane`, never silently served from the shared lane; a caller not attached to the source terminal — or attached from another principal's grant — is refused `terminal.lane_open_forbidden`; the seed prompt is bounded (≤4096 bytes, control characters other than newline rejected) and refused `terminal.lane_seed_too_long`/`terminal.lane_seed_control_char`; an unrecognized source pty reuses `terminal.lane_source_unknown`. Named here because this design gave the behaviors and not the codes; §10(d) item 36 records the naming as a deviation and §3's degradation row carries all four (`shared/claude-lane-refusals.ts:56-59`) **[code]**. It is delivered as terminal input, never interpolated into a shell command line, and never auto-submitted — the prompt lands on the shell's line for the person to read and send (§10(d) item 38).

**The authorization check is a real join.** `registerConnection` (`terminal-presence-registry.ts:111-124`) stores into `participants`, a map keyed by **connectionId** (`:49`, set at `:121`), and it is called for *any* authenticated socket (`runtime-rpc.ts:1376-1387`, comment `:1377-1381`) **[code]** — so "is the caller a registered participant" is true for every connected grant on every terminal and authorizes nothing. Per-terminal membership lives in `attachments`, ptyId → subscriptionKey → attachment (`:51`, `attachmentsFor` `:226-233`, `attach` `:235-241`, public reader `attachmentsOf` `:272`) **[code]**. **The check S9d ships — and this is its normative statement, re-keyed here because rev 12 left it one hop short of §2a's re-key list:** `attachmentsOf(ptyId)` (`:272`) → each attachment's `connectionId` (`:23-27`) → `participants.get(connectionId)` (`:50`, written by `registerConnection` at `:122`) → `.pairedDeviceId` → **`principalOf(…)`**, which must equal `principalOf(ctx.pairedDeviceId)`. **Not `.pairedDeviceId === ctx.pairedDeviceId`, which is what rev 12 wrote here and what §5 tested.** Both forms are implementable against the registry exactly as cited — the shape was re-opened and is unchanged **[code]** — so this is a choice, not a constraint, and the grant-keyed form gets Q1 wrong in the one place Q1 is most visible: a person attached from their **phone** would be refused `openInMyLane` on a pane their **desktop** is attached to, while the same person from the desktop is allowed. One person, two answers to "may I open this in my lane" — the precise class Q1 exists to remove (§2a: the adopt gate, the ownership predicate and the owner label all compare principals; this join is the last one that did not). The registry itself stays principal-blind: the new accessor is still `grantsAttachedTo(ptyId)` — it exposes the `pairedDeviceId`s the registry actually holds, because `participants` is private — and `principalOf` is applied in the handler, so `terminal-presence-registry.ts` gains no dependency on the principal registry.

**Scoped out — not built, and not the same as not reachable.** Transcript-seeded "continue this session in my account" is not built: session transcripts relocate with `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` **[doc]**, so the source JSONL lives inside the *other* developer's lane, and `--resume` in the caller's lane would copy one person's transcript into another person's credential directory — defeating the wipe guarantee and making a data-mixing decision the owner has not made. **The other half, plainly:** `resumeProviderSession.transcriptPath` (`terminal.ts:1027`) and `launchConfig.ompResumeFilePath` (`workspace-session-sleeping-agents.ts:71-77`, reachable on `terminal.create`, `session.tabs.createTerminal` and `worktree.create`) are client-supplied paths on the very RPCs that derive the lane **[code]**. §a's `computeLaneLaunch` containment-checks both against **only** the caller's own lane root and the workspace — which also refuses a path under the shared `~/.claude`, so a pre-S9 session cannot be resumed from inside a lane (§a; the refusal names the shared-lane pane as the place to do it). Residual is the §4 one: a paired grant can read those bytes with a shell anyway.

### (h) Presence integration

Two additive fields on terminal/presence rows: `credentialLane: 'grant' | 'host' | 'remote' | 'wsl' | 'shared-runtime' | 'unknown'` — a union of **display strings**, and a different thing from §2a's host-internal `credentialLane` *create option*, which rev 12 re-keys to `{ kind: 'principal', principalId }`. The published string `'grant'` deliberately keeps its rev-10 name and now reads "pinned to a principal's lane": it is a value old clients render, so renaming it would be a Rule-3 meaning change bought for nothing (`'shared-runtime'` is the OpenClaude downgrade of §a consequence 3 — a lane pane observed running a runtime the lane cannot isolate) and `laneState: 'loaded' | 'absent' | 'reauth-required'` (plus `'restart-required'` if the §4 live probe fails), **and — rev 16 — an additive optional `laneWipePending: true` beside it**, set while a wipe is marked, aborting or retrying its sweep and cleared only on §2f's clean post-sweep read-back. It exists because `'absent'` is what *launches* key on and is therefore the right fail-closed value in that window, while the same string would otherwise assert a residency claim the host cannot yet make; a client with no string for the flag simply renders `absent` (Rule 1). **These are properties of the terminal, not of a participant** — they come from the pane's `lanePrincipalId` (§a), so a shared-lane terminal never renders as owned by a person and a person's desktop pane and phone pane render one owner, not two and a wiped lane says `absent` rather than looking healthy. Because §a's decoupling pins the lane env to *every* lane pane, `credentialLane: 'grant'` now means what the row claims it means for plain shells too.

**The owner label is the authority label — but it is a join, not a shared key, and rev 11 makes it a three-hop join.** `participants` is keyed by connectionId with `pairedDeviceId` as a *field* (`terminal-presence-registry.ts:49`, `:14-16`), `attachments` by subscriptionKey (`:50`) **[code]**; the lane is keyed by `principalId`, which neither map carries. The row projection resolves the terminal's bound lane to its **principal**, resolves each participant's `pairedDeviceId` to a principal, and marks the participants whose principal matches as the credential owner — plural, deliberately: one person connected from a desktop and a phone is **one** owner label over two participants, which is what Q1 means on screen. The label itself is the principal's name, seeded from the `--pair-name` string the registry already carries (`runtime-rpc.ts:1385` reads `deviceRegistry.getDevice(...)?.name ?? ''`) **[code]** and editable at the consent step; it is host-observed and not spoofable. Written once, in the projection, it cannot drift. The account **email** on that row, if Q3 says to show one, is **host-observed** as of rev 32 — the host's own child process wrote it into a directory the host owns and `auth status --json` from that same dir cross-checks it (§2b) — so the reason it stays **off by default is privacy, not provenance** (§7 row 3). Rev 31's "client-asserted (§b)" is retired with the push envelope it described. The owner label was never client-asserted and is unchanged.

**The pane's lane must survive a restart — after rev 8 it *is* the binding, not an attribute of it.** `lanePrincipalId` (rev 10's `laneGrantId`, re-keyed) — carrying the **principal id**, resolved once at bind time, immutable for the pane's life, so that neither a revoke (which drops the `DeviceEntry` at `runtime-rpc.ts:648`/`:660` **[code]**) nor a re-bind can orphan or re-point a live pane — lives on the in-memory `RuntimePtyWorktreeRecord` (`orca-runtime.ts:1399-1427`, beside `connectionId` at `:1403`) **[code]** and is persisted on the pane's binding row, which both spawn paths already sync-flush before `pty:spawn` returns — `persistPtyBinding` (`src/main/persistence.ts:6993-7027`, paneKey composed at `:7008`, beside `terminalPtyIncarnationsByPaneKey`) — which has **four** writers, not the two rev 8 named: `ipc/pty.ts:5299`/`:5303` (path A), `:6875`/`:6877` (path B), and two *reattach* writers, `ipc/pty.ts:820` (inside the stable-pane persistence fence, `:814-824`) and `ssh/ssh-relay-session.ts:2558` **[code]**. The reattach writers rewrite an existing row from an owner record, so each must **preserve** the row's lane field rather than re-emit the row without it — a dropped lane on reattach is a silent downgrade to `shared`, and §5 asserts it on both; §3 row 6 additionally carries the lane alongside each `claudeLivePtySessionIds` entry (`persistence.ts:7228-7282`) **[code]** so the live-PTY seed can attribute accounts, and the sleeping-agent record carries it too (§a). Rehydrated onto the record at restore **[inferred — the field is new; the two store shapes it joins are those]**, and the reattach writers above are the ones that can silently drop it. Without it, cold restore, the wake, `terminal.split` and relay-lease recovery (`orca-runtime.ts:17328`) **[code]** all read no lane. A pane restored from an *older* state carries none: it renders `credentialLane: 'unknown'` (never attributed to a person), and a split, a recovery **and a resume** of it fail closed.

### (i) macOS and Linux — and where Windows and WSL now live

**macOS — a hard refusal, not a degradation.** Per-lane isolation is nearly free: the Keychain service is already suffixed by `sha256(CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR)` (`keychain.ts:85-93`) **[code]**. What breaks it is the legacy fallback: `getActiveClaudeServices` returns `[scoped, unsuffixed]` and readers/writers walk both (`:95-100`) **[code]**, and `runtime-auth-service.ts:480`'s comment explains why — "Claude Code 2.1+ reads the scoped service, older builds the legacy unsuffixed one" **[code]**. With a pre-2.1 CLI every lane collapses onto the single unsuffixed item: all grants share one credential and **R1/R3 fail silently**. **Decision:** on macOS the lane path never touches the unsuffixed service, and lane provisioning **refuses** below the pinned CLI version rather than degrading. The shared lane keeps the fallback. Wipe must also delete the lane's scoped Keychain item. **The fail-closed claim for a *wiped* lane needs its own probe:** `getActiveClaudeServices` returning `[scoped, unsuffixed]` (`keychain.ts:95-100`) **[code]** is *Orca's reader* behavior, not documented CLI behavior. If the CLI does fall back, a wiped lane silently runs on the shared credential. §5 adds a macOS probe on the pinned version; until it passes, macOS lane provisioning stays off — **a hard gate restated in §6, not a note**. And because a live probe on a real Mac cannot protect the reader from regressing on CI, S9a also ships an **in-repo control with a mutation proof**: a unit test asserts that the lane read/write path resolves the *scoped* service only, and making any lane path reach the unsuffixed service (e.g. restoring the `[scoped, unsuffixed]` walk of `keychain.ts:95-100` **[code]** on the lane branch) must turn a named test red. This failure class — every lane silently collapsing onto one credential — is the worst in the design, so it must fail loudly in CI, not only in a manual step.

**Linux.** Unchanged, and still the reference platform for the file-mode reasoning: `.credentials.json` is a plain file at `0600` **[doc]**, `ensureOwnerOnlyMode` chmods it (`lane-credential-writer.ts:208-217`) **[code]**, and every §4 residual in this document was written against a Linux co-tenant. It is no longer the *shared host*, but the owner's VPS is Linux and single-owner, so nothing here is demoted.

**Windows Server — promoted out of this subsection.** Rev 10 answered Windows in one paragraph. It is now the shared host, so it gets its own first-class section: **§2m**. The one claim rev 10 made here that survives verbatim, because §2a already depends on it, is the second-order effect: a `DeviceRegistry.load` that throws anywhere inside its `try` returns **zero** devices (`device-registry.ts:350-385`, `hardenExistingSecureFile` at `:358`, `JSON.parse` at `:359`, catch at `:382-385`) **[code]**, which is why orphan reconciliation is gated on a successful load. **Rev 11 gave that sentence a *Windows* justification and it was wrong; it is withdrawn here rather than quietly patched.** `hardenExistingSecureFile` (`src/shared/secure-file.ts:167-175`) hardens the directory (`:170`) and the file (`:173`) through `hardenSecurePathOnce`, and on `win32` **both** arms end at `applySecurePathRestriction(…, sync=false)` — the file arm at `:84`, and the directory arm at `:64` inside `hardenSecureDirectoryOnce` (`:59-67`, cached at `:66` whether or not the ACL landed), which `:70-73` is merely the win32 short-circuit into (rev 12 cited `:70-73` as the call site; corrected here on re-opening); the dispatch is at `:201-209`, which fires `bestEffortRestrictWindowsPath` and returns `true` optimistically with an empty `execFile` error callback (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:24-41`, callback `:37-39`) **[code]** — asynchronous, best-effort, and structurally unable to throw. That is the *same* property §2m(1) leans on to argue the opposite about the ordinary directory-hardening path, so the two paragraphs contradicted each other. The two synchronous PowerShell ACL spawns the tree does name (`device-registry.ts:62-63`, `:286-288`) **[code]** belong to the **save** path — `writeSecureJsonFile` → `writeSecureFile` → `applySecurePathRestriction(…, sync=true)` (`secure-file.ts:120-126`) — not to load's harden. **The `loadSucceeded` gate is unchanged and still load-bearing**; what changes is the evidence under it: the real zero-device modes are a JSON parse error or a truncated write at `:359`, on every platform, plus a POSIX `chmodSync` failure at `secure-file.ts:210`. Windows is not the platform that makes it real; corruption is.

**WSL.** For S9a–S9d, unchanged and unchanged deliberately: lanes do not apply (§a), a WSL-target launch keeps today's per-account WSL config dir labelled `credentialLane: 'wsl'`, and §a's WSLENV export invariant is asserted so no host lane path ever crosses into a distro. On the Windows host a principal's WSL panes are therefore **not** isolated from another principal's WSL panes — the gap rev 10 named. Rev 11 stops deferring it indefinitely and designs it as an independently shippable slice: **§2n, S9e**. Whether S9e ships *with* S9a or after it was rev 11's open question for the owner; **rev 15 records the answer — native shells, so S9e ships neither: it is deferred** (§2m(8), §7 question 1), and a WSL lane pane is refused rather than un-isolated until it is revived.

### (j) Launch scrub — unchanged, plus new negative controls

The strip and the refusal stay as they are: the `authEnvToDelete`/`envToDelete` construction gated on `claudeAuth?.stripAuthEnv` (`ipc/pty.ts:4742-4744`, `:6467-6469`) over `CLAUDE_AUTH_ENV_VARS` (`environment.ts:9-14`), with the refusal at `:4594-4598`/`:6211-6215` **[code]** — the lane branch's `stripAuthEnv: true` is what arms them, now on every lane pane (§a). (`applyClaudeEnvPatch`'s strip path, `environment.ts:29-57`, is never invoked from `pty.ts` **[code]**; it serves Orca's two own probes.) These are joined by the `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and `agentArgs` guards from §a.

Because `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`/`ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`/`apiKeyHelper` **outrank** stored logins **[doc]**, a lane is only as strong as the settings files the CLI reads *and the arguments that choose them*. Rev 6 answered "three surfaces" one at a time and missed a fourth (host-wide `agentDefaultArgs`); rev 7 answers them from §2's enumeration instead, and only one row needs an answer §2 cannot give structurally: **row 22**, a project-level `.claude/settings.json`, resolved from the cwd rather than the config dir **[doc]** — and that cwd is a folder workspace as often as a git worktree (`AGENTS.md:44-46`) **[code]**. The host does not own that file, so the answer is a **refusal**: S9a refuses a lane launch when resolved project settings define `apiKeyHelper` or auth `env` keys. Rows 5–14 (client `env`/`envToDelete`/`agentEnv`/`agentArgs`/`agentCommand`, and host-wide `agentDefaultArgs`/`agentDefaultEnv`) are computed away by `computeLaneLaunch`; row 20 (the host's own `settings.json`) is ignored except the §a mirror. Two neighbours are **named rather than refused**: enterprise **managed policy** (`/etc/claude-code/managed-settings.json`) outranks every lane `settings.json` and is not relocated **[doc]** — root-only, so §4 class and invisible to this refusal; and the **base-URL class** env keys (row 5), which redirect only the lane owner's own pane. Controls: a lane launch in a workspace whose `.claude/settings.json` defines `apiKeyHelper` must throw (once for a worktree, once for a folder workspace); a lane whose own `settings.json` is mutated on disk to add `apiKeyHelper` must be rewritten or refused at next launch; a lane launch whose `agentArgs` contains a settings-redirecting flag must be refused, once per spawning RPC; and a lane launch made while a peer's `settings.update` has set `agentDefaultArgs`/`agentDefaultEnv` must carry neither; and a lane launch made while a peer has set `agentStatusHooksEnabled: false` still carries the hook coordinates (row 16).

### (k) Usage and rate-limit attribution

Relocating `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` breaks Claude usage telemetry. The managed statusline posts `configDir=$CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`claude/statusline-script.ts:160`; Windows branch `:57-58`) **[code]** — and §a requires every lane's `settings.json` to carry that hook block. `rate-limits/claude-usage-attribution.ts:68-71` snapshots **one** host-wide `configDir` from `authPreparation?.envPatch.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` and drops any event whose `configDir` does not match (`:1476-1482`) **[code]**. Rev 8 adds the dependency row 16 now carries: the post exists only if the hook env was injected, and that injection is gated at spawn by `isAgentStatusHooksEnabled(ptySettings)` at three sites — `pty.ts:4730`, `:6455` (daemon) and `:2508` (non-daemon, via `local-pty-provider.ts:713`) → `agentHookServer.buildPtyEnv()` `:1770` **[code]** — a host-wide, ungated, client-writable flag, which is why the lane read is lane-scoped and always on. Without a change, every lane terminal's usage post is silently discarded while §d promises the projection carries "the caller's own lane row".

**S9b makes the snapshot a map.** `lastClaudeAuthSnapshot` becomes `Map<normalizedConfigDir, { provenance, laneId, identity }>`, populated by the shared-lane fetch as today plus one entry per loaded lane from §c's `syncLane`; `ingestLiveClaudeRateLimits` (`:1467-1484`) looks the event's `configDir` up in that map and attributes to the matching lane, still dropping unknown dirs. Two consequences: (1) the posted `configDir` embeds another **principal's** host-minted `principalId` after the re-key (rev 13 still said "another grant's `pairedDeviceId`" here; the decision is unchanged, the premise was stale — §2a), and although it travels only a loopback POST to Orca's own agent-hook server (`statusline-script.ts:155-163`) **[code]**, it must be replaced by the opaque lane label before it reaches any presence or status row a peer reads — and per §a the same is true of `provenance`, which surfaces as the published `usageMetadata.authProvenance` (`claude-usage-attribution.ts:118`, `shared/rate-limit-types.ts:41`) **[code]**. (2) The *pull* path is not fixed by this, and **Q7 moves it into S9b** — the owner wants "usage bars per-person, per-account and depending on the account attached to the terminal/agent", which is two asks: a per-lane pull, and a per-row join. Three code facts set the design.

**Fact 1 — the pull is already parameterised by a config dir.** `resolveOAuthCredentialReadOptions(authPreparation)` returns `{ credentialsFileConfigDir: authPreparation.configDir, keychainConfigDir: authPreparation.configDir }` (`rate-limits/claude-fetcher.ts:236-248`, the two fields at `:244-245` — `:243` opens the object literal and `:246` closes it; rev 15 cited `:243-246`, corrected here), consumed at `:705` and `:779` **[code]**. **Correction to rev 10, which named this function `buildClaudeUsageReadOptions`:** no such symbol exists in this tree; the name was wrong, the behaviour rev 10 described was right, and the line range `:239-247` was off by a line at each end. Because the fetcher already takes a preparation, giving the pull a lane is a question of *which preparation it is handed*, not of rewriting it.

**Fact 2 — the per-account preparation already exists, and it returns `null` on Windows.** `getManagedUsagePanelAuthPreparation(account, location)` (`rate-limits/claude-fetcher.ts:1058-1088`) builds exactly the shape a lane needs — `configDir`, `envPatch: { CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR }`, `stripAuthEnv: true`, `provenance: 'managed:<id>:inactive-preview'` — in a WSL arm (`:1069-1077`) and a host arm (`:1079-1087`) **[code]**. **But its first statement is `if (process.platform === 'win32') { return null }` (`:1062-1064`) **[code]**.** On the shared **Windows Server** host, the very precedent this slice is modelled on is switched off, and the code carries no comment saying why. **Stated rather than assumed away:** the per-lane pull is a **Linux/macOS capability in this tree**, and shipping it on the Windows host requires first establishing what `:1062-1064` is protecting against — a §5 **Windows-only** probe and a named gate in §6. Until that probe passes, a lane on the Windows host has **statusline-derived usage only**, which is exactly what rev 10 shipped, so the failure mode is "usage bars cover live sessions only", not "no bars".

**Fact 3 — row 32 needs a lane, not a new spawn shape.** `rate-limits/claude-pty.ts` assembles its env with `applyClaudeEnvPatch({ ...process.env, TERM }, options?.authPreparation?.envPatch ?? {}, { stripAuthEnv })` (`:245-249`) **[code]**, overlays the configured proxy (`:254-255`), then branches: a WSL target writes `export CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR=${shellQuote(wslConfig.linuxConfigDir)}` into its `bash -lc` line (`:280`) and a host target rides the env patch alone; the spawn is `pty.spawn(spawnFile, spawnArgs, { …, env: spawnEnv })` (`:289-297`), with `spawnFile` resolved to `wsl.exe` / `cmd.exe` / `claude` at `:266` **[code]**. So the lane arm of row 32 is **one more `authPreparation` handed in** — the same object the fetcher already threads — and nothing about the spawn changes, Windows `cmd.exe /c` arm (`:285-287`) **[code]** included. **Two things do have to change about the env it hands in, and both are `win32`-only.** The strip is exact-case, so an inherited lowercase `anthropic_api_key` survives it (§2m(5)); and the **write** is exact-key too — `baseEnv.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR = patch.CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` (`claude-accounts/environment.ts:44-46`) **[code]** — over a plain spread of `process.env`, so an Orca process carrying `Claude_Config_Dir` (row 19, `runtime-paths.ts:14-25`) leaves **two** keys in the object and Win32 resolves the inherited one. **This path is the exception §2m(5)'s collapse decision had no site for**, because that collapse is sited post-merge inside the provider and the probe never reaches a provider. §2m(5) now sites the third collapse here, over the object `applyClaudeEnvPatch` returns and before `pty.spawn` (`:289-297`); the three consequences of getting it wrong are §2k's own three mechanisms — the paneKey join, the synthetic live-PTY id and the post-probe `syncLane` — each writing the **lane's** row while the probe holds the **shared** lane's token.

**The critical interaction, and rev 32 shrinks it from four mechanisms to three: a usage pull that runs `claude` under a lane's config dir *is* a live Claude process holding that lane's own refresh token.** Rev 13 enumerated three and missed §2f's close-wipe; rev 15 added it as the fourth; **rev 32 deletes the watermark bullet outright, because the watermark is gone (§2c); rev 33 carries out the re-statement rev 32's preamble announced and its bullets did not perform** — the first bullet's surviving hazard is re-graded honestly (Orca cannot serialize two CLIs in one lane), and the second is rewritten from push verbs to the two writers that actually remain, the in-lane switch and the login capture.
- **Rotation deferral (§2e), re-stated because its hazard changed shape.** `markClaudePtySpawned` is called only from `pty.ts:5364` and `:6999` **[code]**, and row 32 never touches `PtyProvider`, so the usage PTY is invisible to the live-PTY gate unless it registers itself. Through rev 31 the harm was a *host* rotation landing on the lane while the probe held the token. After rev 32 Orca performs no rotation on a lane at all (§2e), so that particular double-rotation is gone — **and the registration is kept anyway, for the two hazards that remain and are not smaller.** First, and stated as an accepted consequence rather than as something a control closes: the lane's **own** `claude` — the probe's — can rotate the lane's chain while another `claude` in that lane also holds it, and **Orca cannot serialize that**. Re-opened for rev 33: `markEphemeralClaudePtySpawned` only records the pty in `liveClaudePtyIds` / `ephemeralPtyIds` / `lanePrincipalIdByPtyId` (`live-pty-gate.ts:136-140`) **[code]**, and `hasLiveClaudePtysInLane` is documented in the tree as "the query the lane's rotation gate asks" (`:168-176`) **[code]** — the gate defers **Orca's** refresh and intercepts nothing a CLI does. Rev 32 said "the gate is what serializes them"; it does not, and since §2e removes Orca-side rotation from lanes entirely, that deferral has no consumer left on the lane axis either. Two live `claude` processes in one lane can double-rotate that lane's chain exactly as two `claude` processes in one config dir on a laptop can. That is a direct consequence of "the CLI owns refresh" (§2e), it is accepted, and the recovery is the ordinary one: `laneState: 'reauth-required'` and one lane login. **The registration is kept for the second hazard, which is real and is Orca's own:** an **in-lane switch** (§2c) rewrites the very `.credentials.json` the probe is reading, and a login-session capture does the same at the end of a successful login — both are Orca writes, so the gate does fence them. So the probe still **mints a synthetic, lane-scoped gate id** and registers and releases it through `markEphemeralClaudePtySpawned` / `markEphemeralClaudePtyExited` (`live-pty-gate.ts:136`, `:142`) **[code]** — the **non-persisting** arm, which exists precisely so a synthetic id never reaches `persistence.addClaudeLivePtySessionId` and never gets seeded back at the next startup — wired into the probe's own termination path so no exit route leaks a permanent deferral. Deliberately conservative, and the same trade §2a consequence 2 already made.
- **The per-lane switch gate (§2c, §2f) — rewritten in rev 33, because rev 32 left this bullet written end-to-end in push verbs while §2c and §3 row 9 re-sited the same gate on the writers that survived.** A probe is a read, but it reads the file an **in-lane switch or a login capture** is about to replace — the two writers left after §2c (§2f's wipe is the third member of that lane's write queue, but it removes rather than writes, and it holds precedence over both — §2c). **Decision: the probe takes the lane gate the way a spawn does** — `isClaudeAuthSwitchInProgress(laneId)` (`live-pty-gate.ts:216`) **[code]**, re-cited here because rev 32's `:178-191` names `listLanesWithLiveClaudePtys` instead — and a probe that finds the gate closed is **skipped, not queued**. Usage is sampled on a tick and a missed tick costs nothing; a queued probe would run against a credential the human has already switched away from and post usage attributed to the wrong account, which is the misattribution class this whole design exists to remove. **The reverse ordering produces the same harm and needs its own rule, because the gate is a *spawn* gate and not a barrier.** `beginClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` / `endClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` (`live-pty-gate.ts:205`, `:212`) **[code]** are lane-scoped at the four **spawn-side** reads (`ipc/pty.ts:4527`, `:4593`, `:6163`, `:6210`) **[code]** — **re-derived against the implementation tree in rev 34: rev 32's numbers named the wrong symbol, rev 33's replacement (`:4639`, `:6174`, `:6223`) is wrong at three of four, and §6's S9c bullet carried a third set (`:4619`, `:6154`, `:6203`) that is wrong at the same three; these four are the actual call sites of `assertClaudeAuthSwitchNotInProgress`, and §6 is corrected to match**; they stop a new spawn and do nothing to a PTY already running. **A switch or a login capture landing *after* the probe spawned therefore replaces `.credentials.json` underneath it** — and on Windows it **succeeds** in doing so, inside `renameFileWithWindowsRetry`'s ~750 ms window (`codex-accounts/fs-utils.ts:200-202` → `:208-223`, six attempts at `:209` over `EPERM`/`EACCES`/`EBUSY` at `:216`) **[code]**, by §2m(2)'s own design. Two harms, not one: the probe posts usage for the **pre-switch** account under a row that now names the post-switch one, and it still holds the **pre-switch** single-use refresh token, so any rotation it performs revokes a credential the lane no longer holds. **Decision: the writer does not wait for an in-flight probe; it invalidates it.** `beginClaudeAuthSwitch(laneId)` — taken by the in-lane switch handler (§2c(i)) and by the login session's capture step (§2b) alike — marks every probe in flight in that lane **stale**; a stale probe's result is **dropped, not attributed** — no usage row, and no lane identity read carried forward from it — and its PTY is **killed** rather than left to finish. **The carrier rev 12 named does not do that, and the difference is the second of the two harms above, so it is corrected rather than adjusted:** `registerHiddenRateLimitPty(term)` (`rate-limits/hidden-pty-cleanup.ts:12-19`) does `activeHiddenRateLimitPtys.add(term)` and returns a disposable whose `dispose()` is a bare `.delete(term)` (`:15-18`) **[code]** — it is bookkeeping, and it terminates nothing. Termination is `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, disposables, { kill: true })` (`:25-58`, the disposable drain at `:30-32`, `term.kill()` at `:36`) **[code]**, which the probe already calls on each of its own exit paths (`claude-pty.ts:345`, `:364`, `:419`, and with `kill: false` at `:514`) **[code]**. **Decision: the staleness signal invokes that same call** — `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, termDisposables, { kill: true })` — which kills the `claude` still holding the **pre-switch** single-use refresh token *and*, by draining the registered disposables at `:33-35`, releases the lane gate id the bullet above minted. **And the handle that reaches it from outside already exists, which rev 13 left unnamed and therefore implementable only as new machinery:** `term` and `termDisposables` are locals of `fetchViaPty`, but `fetchViaPty` already takes an `options.signal: AbortSignal` (`claude-pty.ts:221`) and wires it at `:349-358` to `settleAborted` (`:335-347`), whose body performs exactly this call at `:345` **[code]**. **The staleness signal is that abort**: the lane pull holds each in-flight probe's `AbortController` and aborts it, so the decision is implementable as stated rather than as a new handle, and §2f's wipe fence — for the probe and, as of rev 33, for the login session — reaches the same place. Disposing the registration alone would have left that CLI running on a credential the lane no longer holds, which is precisely the harm this decision enumerates. Dropping rather than waiting is the same trade as the skip above, in the other direction: a missed usage tick costs nothing, and a switch that blocks on a hidden process is a switch the human watches hang. **Mutation proof in §5:** attributing a stale probe's post, or letting its lane identity read stand after the switch, turns a named test red.
- **The watermark bullet is deleted, and the deletion is stated rather than the bullet quietly dropped.** Through rev 31 this bullet required the lane pull's tick to run `syncLane` over every lane it probed, *after* the probe exited and explicitly **not** subject to trigger (2)'s live-PTY precondition, so that a rotation the probe's own CLI performed would move the watermark and the desktop's next push would not be refused `push_stale`. **There is no watermark, no push and no `push_stale` (§2c), so the whole mechanism has no subject.** What replaces it is §2c's lane identity read — one read of the lane's `oauthAccount`, so the row's account label is current — which is not ordering-sensitive, cannot strand a lane, and needs no exemption from any precondition. The §5 fixture that drove an idle lane to prove the trigger-(2) exemption is deleted with the trigger; the fixture that proves the probe **defers a switch** in its own lane replaces it.
- **The close-wipe (§2f) — the fourth, and the one rev 13 did not enumerate.** A probe is not merely a reader of the lane: its `claude` can rotate and **write** `.credentials.json` back into the lane's config dir, which is the whole reason `cli-observed` exists. So a wipe that sweeps the lane directory while a probe is in flight can be undone by that probe's own write *after* the sweep completes — a lane reported wiped, holding a live credential at rest, with `laneState: 'loaded'` and §2a consequence 2's fail-closed promise false. **Decision: the wipe kills first and sweeps second, through the same staleness path the in-lane switch and the login capture use** (rev 34 — this bullet still read “the push”, whose subject is deleted, two paragraphs after the switch-gate bullet was rewritten for exactly that) — mark stale, `cleanupHiddenRateLimitPty(term, termDisposables, { kill: true })` (`hidden-pty-cleanup.ts:25-58`), reached through `fetchViaPty`'s `options.signal` (`claude-pty.ts:221`, wired `:349-358` → `settleAborted` `:335-347`, the call at `:345`) **[code]**, result dropped, and only then the sweep. **And killing what is in flight is only half of it, which rev 14 left implied: the next tick must not start one.** The pull therefore refuses to start a probe for a lane whose **wipe is pending or in progress** (the lifecycle sets that flag before it aborts, §2f) and for a lane whose `laneState` is **`absent`** — the second the pull already did, the first new in rev 15 — and §2f's post-sweep re-read is the backstop for a flag read a moment too early. §5 asserts the start-side refusal **separately from the kill**, because a suite that tests only the kill passes while the very next tick re-loads the lane it just watched being wiped. Stated normatively at §2f; stated here because this bullet list is what an implementer reads when wiring the probe's lifecycle, and the omission is what made the gap invisible.

**"The usage of the account attached to this terminal/agent" — the join, and why it does not go through `configDir`.** The obvious key is rev 10's: make `lastClaudeAuthSnapshot` a map keyed by normalized config dir. That is right for *attribution* and is kept. It is the wrong key for the **row**, for two reasons this tree makes concrete. First, `normalizeClaudeConfigDir` trims and folds separators but deliberately does **not** fold case — "preserve Linux case sensitivity" (`rate-limits/claude-usage-attribution.ts:29-33`) **[code]** — and the test that drops a mismatch is a bare `!==` (`:1476-1482`) **[code]**; on the **Windows** host the CLI's `%CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR%` and Orca's stored lane path can legitimately differ in drive-letter case or by an 8.3 component, and every post from that lane is then silently dropped (§2m(4)). Second, the config dir is the identifier §2a spent an entire decision making opaque before it reaches a peer-readable row.

**A better key is already on the wire and is being thrown away.** The managed statusline **already posts the paneKey**: `--data-urlencode "paneKey=${ORCA_PANE_KEY}"` (`claude/statusline-script.ts:159`; the Windows `cmd` twin at `:65`) **[code]**. But the parser reads only two fields out of the form body — `const fields = body as { payload?: unknown; configDir?: unknown }` (`shared/claude-statusline-rate-limits.ts:76`) — and returns `{ configDir, fiveHour, sevenDay }` (`:87`, the function spanning `:72-105`), which is the whole of `ClaudeStatusLineRateLimits` (`:22-34`); the route hands that object straight on (`agent-hooks/server.ts:2138-2141`) **[code]**. The paneKey is posted, parsed away, and never seen. **Decision: `ClaudeStatusLineRateLimits` gains `paneKey`** — a local type, additive, no wire change, no statusline-script change — and `ingestLiveClaudeRateLimits` resolves **pane → lane** through the binding registry §2a already made authoritative, falling back to the config-dir map only when the paneKey is absent (an older pane, a hand-run statusline). **That new key creates a cross-principal misattribution vector of its own, at the one spawn site that does not go near `computeLaneLaunch`, and it must be closed in the same slice — round 3's third major.** The lane usage probe (row 32, handed a lane `authPreparation` by Fact 3) builds its env as `{ ...process.env, TERM: 'xterm-256color' }` (`rate-limits/claude-pty.ts:247-248`) **[code]** with **no pane-identity scrub**. The PTY path scrubs exactly this and says why: `removeUnspecifiedPaneIdentityEnv(spawnEnv, args.env)` (`providers/local-pty-provider.ts:686`, def `:167-176`) under the comment "Orca can be launched from an Orca terminal; pane identity belongs to the child PTY, not the parent shell", with `ORCA_PANE_KEY` first in `PANE_IDENTITY_ENV_KEYS` (`shared/pane-identity-env.ts:15`, local twin `shared/pane-identity-env.ts:14`) **[code]**; `pty.ts:4775`'s `getInheritedAgentHookEnvKeysToDelete(env)` covers the hook coordinates for the same reason. So when Orca is launched **from an Orca terminal** — the case the comment documents — the probe's `claude` runs the lane's managed statusline with a **stale inherited paneKey** and posts it (`claude/statusline-script.ts:159`, Windows `cmd` twin `:65`) **[code]**. Today that is inert because the parser drops the field (`shared/claude-statusline-rate-limits.ts:76`, `:87`) **[code]**; this decision is what makes it harmful, because the paneKey now **wins** over the (correct) lane configDir, so one principal's probe usage lands on whatever pane that stale key names — plausibly the other principal's lane row. **Decision: the probe mirrors `:701` — pane-identity keys are deleted from its `spawnEnv` before `pty.spawn`** (a one-line scrub at `claude-pty.ts:247-251`, over the same key list), and §5 asserts it, because per-row usage attribution is exactly what Q7 is about. Strictly better than the config-dir key on every axis that matters here: exact, case-free, needs no opaque label, and it is the *same* identifier the lane is bound to, so usage attribution cannot disagree with §2h's owner label.

**The row is then a three-hop join, and every hop already exists:** `terminal.paneKey` → the pane's bound lane (§2a's binding registry, persisted by `persistPtyBinding` at `src/main/persistence.ts:6993-7027`, paneKey composed at `:7008`) **[code]** → that lane's **current** account (**§2c's lane identity read** — one read of the lane's own `oauthAccount` after each write; **not** §2e's residency index, which §2e deletes outright and which rev 32 left this hop still pointing at) → that account's usage row. So each terminal/agent row surfaces the usage of the account its lane holds *right now*, and **an in-lane switch or a completed login** re-points it without the row being rebuilt. §3 row 10 states the same join and is the one that had it right. Two additive fields, **Rule 1**, written in the same projection that already emits `credentialLane`/`laneState` (§2h): **`laneAccountLabel`** — Q3's friendly per-account name, falling back to the principal/owner label when the owner has set none — and **`laneUsage`**, the two windows plus `resetsAt`, the shape `ProviderRateLimits` already publishes (`shared/rate-limit-types.ts:48`) **[code]**. **Not** added: any account id, and the email only where Q3's owner setting permits it. **The two fields have *different* cross-principal visibility, and rev 11 left that undefined:** `laneAccountLabel` **is** peer-visible — Q3 asks precisely about "the identity label the other developer sees", so a row the other developer looks at carries the principal label plus, when the owner has set one, that account's owner-authored display name — **the owner-set label written at the host's own consent surface (§2b)**, not the push envelope's third member, which is deleted with the envelope; `laneUsage` is **not**. §2d's projection rule applies to this row too, so a peer looking at another principal's terminal sees `laneUsage` **omitted**, never zeroed — zero reads as "no usage left to worry about", omission reads as "not yours", and the client renders nothing.

**Cost, honestly.** The pull is `N` hidden `claude` processes per tick, where `N` is the number of **loaded** lanes; each holds its lane's own token for the probe's lifetime and fences that lane's switch while it runs. On a two-developer box `N ≤ 3` and the tick interval is unchanged. If Fact 2's Windows gate does not clear, `N = 0` on the shared host and only the statusline path feeds the bars. And a lane provenance still deliberately fails `isManagedClaudeAuth` (`claude-fetcher.ts:493-496`) **[code]**, which gates CLI *supplementation* of an OAuth result — that stays false for lanes.

**Two grants on one account share one quota, and the UI must say so in one sentence or the first limit reads as a bug — new in rev 32, and it is the second-order cost of the whole model.** Claude rate limits are a property of the **subscription/account**, not of an OAuth grant, so a lane login and a desktop login on the same account draw from one pool: per-lane login buys **concurrency and independent revocation, not capacity**. Two obligations follow, both on the presentation and neither on the fetcher. (1) The desktop's own usage bar and that lane's `laneUsage` row now derive from **two different grants reading one account's limits**, sampled at different times, so they will disagree transiently; rows must be de-duplicated by `accountUuid` and rendered as *one account's usage observed from two places* — never summed, never shown side by side as two budgets. `ProviderRateLimits` (`shared/rate-limit-types.ts:48`) **[code]** already carries the shape; what changes is the join, not the payload. (2) The one-sentence statement belongs next to the lane's account label, because that is where a person forms the wrong inference. **Not changed by any of this:** the `paneKey → lane → account` three-hop join, the pane-identity env scrub on the probe, the synthetic non-persisting gate id, and the peer-visibility split (`laneAccountLabel` peer-visible, `laneUsage` **omitted** for peers and never zeroed).

### (l) The phone — it may start a lane login, and it may take the code

Q1 makes the owner's phone a member of the owner's principal, so the phone's account switcher names a **lane**. Rev 10 could not ask whether the phone can push; rev 32 does not have to, because nothing pushes.

**The phone still holds no credential, and that fact is unchanged and now buys more.** The mobile client holds no Claude account store and no `ClaudeRuntimeAuthService` equivalent: `grep -rn "claudeAiOauth\|refreshToken\|refreshClaudeOauth" mobile/src mobile/app` returns **zero hits** **[code]**. Its only Claude-account code is a render path (`mobile/src/components/accounts-snapshot.ts`, `account-usage-state.ts`, `AccountUsage.tsx`) plus a dev harness, and its only secret storage is *pairing* material in `expo-secure-store` **[code]**. Under push that meant "a phone cannot push a blob it does not hold"; under per-lane login it means the phone can do **everything** in this flow, because the flow moves no secret: it displays a URL and it takes a code, and holds neither afterward.

**The two shapes rev 10 rejected, re-graded.** **Rejected (i) — give the phone an account store.** Still rejected, still for the same reasons, and now also unnecessary: the point of an account store was to have something to push, and there is nothing to push. **Rejected (ii) — "let the phone select a lane-resident account: there is nothing to select among, because L1 says a lane holds exactly one account at a time."** **That is now FALSE and is retired.** L1 is gone (§2e) and a lane may hold several logins (§2c), so in-lane selection is a real capability with real content, and it is the capability the phone gains.

**Decision — the phone acts directly on the lane, and the entire delegated-selection apparatus is deleted.** Steps 1–4 of rev 31's flow — the desktop publishing a delegable list, `accounts.lane.requestSwitch`, the `switch-requested` frame, the desktop performing an ordinary push, the phone's `pending` return and its stream-borne outcome — are replaced by **two ordinary lane RPCs the phone calls for itself**:

- **`accounts.lane.selectAccount({ laneAccountId })`** — switch among the logins the lane already holds (§2c(i)). It is a local file rewrite on the host, it completes synchronously, and it needs no other device to be reachable.
- **`accounts.lane.loginStart` / `loginSubmitCode` / `loginCancel` / `loginStatus`** — add a login the lane does not hold (§2c(ii)), with the URL rendered on the phone (a tappable link and a QR, since the browser that completes it may be the phone's own or another device's) and the code typed back into the same screen.

**What that deletes, named because each was a real cost of the old shape.** `accounts.lane.desktop_unavailable` and the liveness precondition it enforced — the pooled shared-control connection, the lane-status-subscription conjunct, the CLI-grant carve-out rev 21 added, the whole of rounds 7–9's transport reasoning — **is deleted**, because no other device has to be reachable for the phone to act. `accounts.lane.switch_timed_out` is deleted, because nothing is awaited. `accounts.lane.switch_lane_cleared` is deleted with the outstanding request it named. The delegable list, `setDelegableAccounts`, `lane-delegation-directory.ts`, `delegable_account_unknown` and `delegable_list_invalid` are deleted (§2b). **The one operation the phone could not complete alone is now completable alone**, which was the single named cost of §2l through rev 31 and is the clearest measure of what re-basing the source bought.

**The designation still gates the login, and the phone may hold it.** Under §2d(i) a login session is started only by the grant holding this principal's designation, so a *bound but undesignated* grant — the person's phone when their desktop is designated, or vice versa — may **switch** among the lane's logins but may not **start a login**, and is refused `accounts.lane.login_not_designated`. The owner default applied here is that **a phone is a legitimate designation**: it displays a URL and takes a code, holds no secret either way, and refusing it would encode a device-kind judgement the host provably cannot make (§2a: every candidate signal in this tree is absent or client-asserted). **Owner-pending (§7 question 13),** together with the alternative the design does not take by default: letting *any* grant bound to the principal start a login, which is friendlier and widens §4's substitution audience from one device to that person's devices.

**And mis-designation stops being inert, which is a genuine loss and is stated as one.** §2a says of a wrong tick that the failure is *inert* — "no push ever arrives, so no credential byte is written, `laneState` stays `absent`", and the fix is one re-tick. **That is no longer true.** A wrongly-designated grant of the right person can now *complete a login*, because a login needs only a browser and a person, not a credential store. What bounds it is unchanged and is the reason this is a loss rather than a hole: designation ranges **only over grants already bound to that principal** (§2a rule (i)), so the worst case is *the wrong device of the right person* loading that person's own lane with an account that person authenticated. It is not cross-principal and it cannot reach another lane. **What rev 34 removes from this sentence is the I6 clause:** the mis-designated grant is the caller that supplies `expectedEmail`, so I6 bounds its own mistyping and not which account the lane ends up holding (§2b, §4). Rule (i) is the bound, and it is the whole of it. **What rev 34 adds is the other half of “recovered by a re-tick”:** a re-tick that *moves* the designation now also **cancels** the session the old grant has in flight (§2d rule (3)), so the recovery reaches the window in which recovery matters instead of arriving after the wrong device has already completed. §2a's "harmless" is corrected to **"bounded to this principal's own devices, and recovered by a re-tick"**, and §4 carries it.

**Why this is the right shape and not a workaround.** It preserves what the design already paid for: the lane is still keyed by a **principal**, "who may spend this account" is still answered by the person and not by which device asked, and the phone is still *an extension of the desktop* rather than an independent client — it just no longer needs the desktop to be awake. It gains no secret it did not already have. And the one thing it costs is the one thing §4 now has to defend: the audience that can be shown an authorization URL is one device wider than it was.

### (m) Windows Server as the shared host

Rev 10 answered Windows in a paragraph, because the shared box was Linux. It is now the host. This section re-opens every platform-dependent claim in the design against `win32`. **Nothing below demotes the macOS or Linux material**; §2i keeps it.

**(1) The lane directory's DACL — and Orca already has the read-back verification this asks for.** `writeFileAtomically(…, { mode: 0o600 })` is a no-op for Windows ACLs — `writeFileSync`'s `mode` is inert there, and `ensureOwnerOnlyMode` returns early on `win32` before its `chmodSync` (`lane-credential-writer.ts:208-217`, the guard at `:1795-1797`) **[code]**. What Orca does instead is `src/shared/secure-file.ts` → `src/shared/secure-path-windows-acl.ts`, and it is stronger than rev 10 assumed. `restrictWindowsPathSync(targetPath, isDirectory)` (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:43-64`) runs `WINDOWS_RESTRICT_ACL_SCRIPT` (`:66-114`) under PowerShell, which: disables inheritance and drops inherited ACEs, `$acl.SetAccessRuleProtection($true, $false)` (`:77`); removes **every** existing ACE (`:78-80`); grants `FullControl` to exactly three SIDs — the current user, `S-1-5-18` (SYSTEM) and `S-1-5-32-544` (Administrators) (`:71`, `:85-95`); and then **reads the DACL back and verifies it**, throwing if inheritance is still enabled (`:97-100`), if any ACE names a SID outside the allowed set (`:102-106`), if any ACE is a deny (`:107-109`), or if any grant is less than FullControl (`:110-112`) **[code]**. That is precisely rev 10's "verify by reading back the DACL, not the mode bits" — already written, already synchronous, already throwing. **Decision: the lane writer uses this, not a mode bit, and uses the *synchronous* arm.** That matters because the default arm is not synchronous: `applySecurePathRestriction(…, sync=false)` on `win32` fires `bestEffortRestrictWindowsPath` and **returns `true` optimistically** (`secure-file.ts:201-209`, esp. `:206-208`), errors swallowed in the callback (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:37-39`) **[code]**; and directory hardening always takes that arm — `hardenSecurePathOnce` short-circuits every `win32` directory into `hardenSecureDirectoryOnce`, which caches the path as hardened whether or not the ACL landed (`secure-file.ts:59-73`, cache set at `:66`) **[code]**, deliberately, to stop a main-thread storm (#4901). So on Windows **a lane directory hardened through the ordinary path is best-effort and unverified**, which is not good enough for the directory a credential is about to land in. **The lane's rule, and it is scoped to a *native* lane — rev 13 stated it unconditionally and that version could not ship beside §2n, which is round 3's sixth major and the unfixed twin of the UNC refusal rev 13 did correct.** For a lane whose directory is a **local drive path**: `restrictWindowsPathSync(laneDir, true)` at creation, its boolean return **checked**, and provisioning **fails closed** if it is false — no lane exists until its DACL verified. This is the same fail-closed shape §2i uses for macOS, for the same reason: a silently unisolated lane is the worst class in this design. **For an S9e lane the rule does not apply at all, and the difference is a skip and not a no-op.** §2n writes **every** S9e lane through `\\wsl.localhost\<distro>\home\<user>\.local\share\orca\claude-lanes\<principalId>\`; `restrictWindowsPathSync` (`secure-path-windows-acl.ts:43-64`) runs `Get-Acl`/`Set-Acl` under PowerShell and returns **`false` on any throw** (`:59-63`) **[code]**, so a redirector path that cannot carry a DACL returns false — and the unconditional rule would then refuse S9e provisioning outright, every time. §2m(4)(b) called the verification "a no-op there", which is not what a `false` return is. **Decision, mirroring §2m(4)'s carve-out exactly rather than inventing a second shape:** the DACL step is **not run** for a candidate `parseWslUncPath` matches (`shared/wsl-paths.ts:6-17`, regex at `:8`) **[code]**; that lane's isolation control is §2n(i)'s in-distro `chmod 0700/0600`, read back and verified inside the distro the way `assertManagedAuthPath` already re-canonicalizes there, and provisioning fails closed on *that* verification instead. Every other UNC root stays refused at provisioning (§2m(4)). This mattered for scheduling as well as for tidiness, and rev 15 records how the schedule resolved: **the owner's answer is native shells (§2m(8)), so S9e is deferred** — but the carve-out stays exactly as written, because the two rules still could not both ship, and §2n is a design held ready rather than a slice to be re-derived when a WSL-forced pane appears.
